Nearby Special Lagrangians
Mohammed Abouzaid, Yohsuke Imagi

Abstract
Let X X be a Calabi–Yau manifold and Q ⊂ X Q\subset X a compact connected embedded special Lagrangian without boundary. We combine methods from symplectic topology and geometric analysis, to study those compact irreducibly immersed special Lagrangians which are C 0 C^{0} close to Q Q and have unobstructed Floer cohomology. We prove that if the fundamental group π 1 ​ Q \pi_{1}Q has no non-abelian free subgroup then every such Lagrangian L L is unbranched (that is, the projection L → Q L\to Q is a covering map); moreover, if π 1 ​ Q \pi_{1}Q is abelian then L L is C 1 C^{1} close to Q ⊂ X . Q\subset X. We prove a stronger uniqueness theorem when π 1 ​ Q \pi_{1}Q is finite. The π 1 ​ Q \pi_{1}Q conditions, the Floer cohomology condition and the special Lagrangian condition are all essential as we show by counterexamples.



1
Introduction

We begin with a definition we use throughout the paper.

Definition 1.1
.

A
Calabi–Yau
manifold is a Kähler manifold
X
equipped with a holomorphic volume form
\Omega.

A
special
Lagrangian in a Calabi–Yau manifold
X
is a Lagrangian of
phase
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
in the sense of Definition
2.32
.

Remark 1.2
.

The definition agrees with that in the literature on special Lagrangians except that Joyce
[
34
]
and others call
X
an
almost
Calabi–Yau manifold to indicate that this need not be Ricci-flat. We do more symplectic topology in the present paper and therefore leave out the word almost so as not to be confused with almost complex structures.
Special Lagrangians are in general defined as Lagrangians of any constant phase. We mention explicitly what the phases are if they are not
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau manifold and
Q\subset X
a closed connected embedded special Lagrangian.
Closed means compact without boundary.
The
C^{1}
nearby problem for
Q\subset X
is solved by McLean
[
43
]
; that is,
those special Lagrangians which are
C^{1}
close to
Q\subset X
form a smooth manifold whose tangent space at
Q
is isomorphic to the (de Rham) cohomology group
H^{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}).

We study the
C^{0}
nearby problem, which is to classify those closed special Lagrangians
L
contained in a given neighbourhood of
Q\subset X.

Donaldson and He
[
15
,
29
,
30
]
have proved recently an existence theorem about this.
We prove on the other hand a uniqueness and non-existence theorem, Theorem
1.3
below.

We make now the definitions we use to state our results.
Let
U\subseteq X
be a Weinstein neighbourhood of
Q
(which may be embedded in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}Q).

Let
\iota:\hat{L}\to U
be a Lagrangian immersion.
We suppose that every self-intersection point of
\iota
is a transverse double point unless otherwise mentioned.
In many places we refer only to the image
L:=\iota(\hat{L})
and call this simply the Lagrangian.
We say that it is
closed
if
\hat{L}
is compact without boundary.
We say that it is
irreducibly
immersed if the domain
\hat{L}
is connected.

We say that a Lagrangian immersion
\hat{L}\to L\subset U
is
unbranched
if the composition with the projection map
U\subset T^{*}Q\to Q
is a finite cover
\hat{L}\to Q
.
This is equivalent to saying that there exists a finite cover
P\to Q
such that the pull-back of
L\subset U
under the induced map
T^{*}P\to T^{*}Q
is a union of smooth perturbations of
P
(if
L
is embedded, this union is disjoint).
Here a smooth perturbation means the graph over
P
of some closed
1
-form (so this is a Lagrangian).
We say that the Lagrangian is
branched
if it is not unbranched.

We give also a brief account of Floer cohomology groups and Fukaya categories which we use.
We assign to the symplectic manifold
U
above a filtered strict
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U).
This is a version of the Fukaya categories defined by Akaho–Joyce
[
9
]
and Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
]
which we explain in §
2
.
The objects of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
are closed Lagrangians in
U
equipped with some additional data, the most important of which are bounding cochains in the sense of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
]
.
It is a property of the Lagrangians that they have these data; that is, some Lagrangians do have the additional data, in which case we say that they have
unobstructed Floer cohomology
or
HF^{*}
unobstructed
for short, and others have no such data at all, in which case we say that they have
obstructed Floer cohomology
or
HF^{*}
obstructed
for short.
Floer cohomology groups are the morphism groups of the cohomology category
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
of the strict
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U).
The symbol
HF^{*}
is a standard notation for these.

As we are working not in the whole
X
but in the Weinstein neighbourhood
U
the zero-section
Q
(and any Lagrangian that is
C^{1}
close to it) has
HF^{*}
unobstructed in
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
because this does not bound any non-constant holomorphic discs in
U
(it has possibly
HF^{*}
obstructed in the Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
of the whole
X
).

Also as
U
is an exact symplectic manifold we can do the virtual counts in any characteristic
p

[
23
]
. The advantage of having
p>0
is that we can cancel out the effect of pseudo-holomorphic discs whose contributions are multiples of
p;
for instance, with
p=2
we can do without relative spin structures.
Thus by varying
p
we can include more Lagrangians with
HF^{*}
unobstructed.
The zero-section
Q\subset U
has
HF^{*}
unobstructed for
any

p
because it has essentially no holomorphic discs.
On the other hand, we shall need the nearby special Lagrangians to have
HF^{*}
unobstructed only for some
p.

In these circumstances our theorem may be stated as follows:

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau manifold and
Q\subset X
a closed connected embedded special Lagrangian; then the following hold.

(i)

Let the fundamental group
\pi_{1}Q
be finite and let
U\subseteq X
be any Weinstein neighbourhood of
Q.
Then every closed special Lagrangian in
U
with
HF^{*}
unobstructed is necessarily of phase
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and equal to
Q.

(ii)

If
\pi_{1}Q
has no non-abelian free subgroup, there exists a Weinstein neighbourhood
U\subseteq X
of
Q
such that every closed irreducibly immersed special Lagrangian
L\subset U
with
HF^{*}
unobstructed is unbranched.
Moreover, if
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian then
L
is a McLean
C^{1}
perturbation of
Q.

Here is a sketch of the proof. Suppose first that
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian as in the latter part of (ii). As
L
has
HF^{*}
unobstructed there is a corresponding object
{\mathbf{b}}
of the Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
and we can define the Floer cohomology group
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}}),
which is a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded vector space.
Since the Lagrangian
L
is special it follows that
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
is non-zero and supported in a single degree; the non-zero property will be proved in Corollary
2.56
, and the single degree property will be proved in Lemma
3.4
.
We use then the known techniques for dealing with
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U),
which we will explain in §
2
.
The key fact is that
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
is a certain representation of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}Q
and that this representation determines
{\mathbf{b}}
up to quasi-isomorphism. As
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian there is a one-dimensional sub-representation of
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}}).
This may be realized by a McLean
C^{1}
perturbation of
Q
with some local system over it. Then by a Thomas–Yau theorem, Proposition
2.67
(i), we can show that the original special Lagrangian
L
agrees with the
C^{1}
perturbation of
Q.

The same method applies to
\pi_{1}Q
which has no non-abelian free subgroup as in (ii) above. In this case we shall need to pass to a finite-index subgroup of
\pi_{1}Q
for the algebraic reason. Denote by
P\to Q
the corresponding finite cover, and by
f:T^{*}P\to T^{*}Q
the induced finite cover between the cotangent bundles.
The McLean
C^{1}
perturbation
\hat{L}
will then be defined in
T^{*}P
and the original
L
will be the image of
\hat{L}
under
f:T^{*}P\to T^{*}Q.
So
L
is unbranched.

Part (i) is proved in §
3.1
with simpler algebraic arguments. The conclusion is stronger in two respects: we do not have to shrink the Weinstein neighbourhood
U,
and we can include special Lagrangians of any phase.

Apart from the
HF^{*}
hypothesis Theorem
1.3
(i) is stronger than the result of Tsai–Wang
[
61
,
62
]
which we recall in Proposition
4.1
.
They prove this by a simple Riemannian geometry method, supposing that
U
is Ricci-flat and
Q
Ricci-positive. Then by Myers’ theorem the fundamental group
\pi_{1}Q
is necessarily finite as in (i) above. On the other hand, Tsai–Wang’s method applies to any singular special Lagrangians in the sense of geometric measure theory
[
42
]
. The
HF^{*}
hypothesis of our theorem presupposes that the Lagrangians are not very singular, about which we discuss more in Remark
3.18
after we prove the theorem.

The hypothesis on
\pi_{1}Q
is essential to Theorem
1.3
(ii).
For instance, if
Q
is a compact Riemann surface of genus
>1
then
\pi_{1}Q
has free non-abelian subgroups and the cotangent bundle of such a surface admits many branched nearby special Lagrangians given by the hyperKähler rotations of Hitchin spectral curves
[
31
, §5.1]
(where the hyperKähler metric is given by Feix
[
17
]
and Kaledin
[
35
]
).
We give in Example
5.12
a higher-dimensional version of this.
He
[
29
]
constructs also branched special Lagrangians which are locally modelled upon the product of
S^{1}
and a
2
-fold spectral curve. His construction applies to many
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
-homology
3
-spheres
Q

[
30
]
and their fundamental groups have non-abelian free subgroups (more precisely, his examples do not include finite quotients of
S^{3}
or
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}P^{3}\#{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}P^{3};
cf. Proposition
5.16
). Thus no contradiction occurs with Theorem
1.3
(ii).

The abelian condition is essential to the latter part of Theorem
1.3
(ii).
We prove in Corollary
4.7
that there are multi-valued graphs over a certain flat manifold
Q.
This
Q
has a non-trivial finite cover by
T^{n}
and the fundamental group
\pi_{1}Q
is accordingly not abelian.

We show also that the other hypotheses of Theorem
1.3
are also indispendable.
For this we use Lagrangian surgeries obtained from Morse
1
-forms on
Q.

We prove in Example
5.10
that there are examples satisfying the conditions of Theorem
1.3
(ii) except that the Lagrangians should be special.
We prove in Corollary
5.3
that these Lagrangian are not special even in the weak categorical sense.

We examine now the
HF^{*}
hypothesis. This would in general be hard to verify directly (because the Maurer–Cartan equation is defined by using all the holomorphic curve moduli spaces). On the other hand, so far as we know there are no examples in which we cannot apply Theorem
1.3
just because the Lagrangians have
HF^{*}
obstructed (which would be hard to verify for the same reason). We change the point of view in Corollary
5.13
: we
use
Theorem
1.3
to find examples with
HF^{*}
obstructed. These are branched special Lagrangians and we argue indirectly: if they had
HF^{*}
unobstructed, they would satisfy the conclusion of Theorem
1.3
, which is impossible because they are branched; they have thus
HF^{*}
obstructed. This has a further consequence, Corollary
5.15
, which is about Morse
1
-forms and perhaps of interest in itself.

We begin in §
2
with the more careful treatment of Fukaya categories.
In §
3
we prove Theorem
1.3
.
In §
4
we recall Tsai–Wang’s result and give the examples related to it.
This section is essentially about minimal submanifolds and geometric measure theory, which has nothing directly to do with Floer cohomology groups or Fukaya categories.
In §
5
we give the branched examples and prove the relevant results.

2
Fukaya Categories

In this section we explain the definition and properties of the Fukaya categories.
The key result is the split-generation theorem for cotangent bundles, including non-exact Lagrangians; see Theorem
2.84
. This is known for exact Lagrangians and we extend it to non-exact Lagrangians. We must include non-exact Lagrangians because of the McLean perturbations, which are non-exact except the trivial perturbations.

We use pseudo-holomorphic curve moduli spaces to define the Fukaya categories.
As we include non-exact Lagrangians we cannot follow the direct perturbation method of Seidel
[
53
]
and we follow instead the virtual perturbation method of Akaho–Joyce
[
9
]
and Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
]
. But still we cannot use their results as they are. The reason is that we shall be concerned with
wrapped
Fukaya categories and therefore need to include countably many Lagrangians. This causes a new problem which we explain now. Recall that we can deal only with finitely many kinds of Kuranishi spaces in doing the virtual counts
[
21
, §7.2.3]
and the results are accordingly a finite approximation of what we want. These are called gapped
A_{N}
categories in our simplified notation, Definition
2.14
(or gapped
A_{NK}
algebras in their notation
[
9
,
21
]
). We pass the the limit, called gapped
A_{\infty}
categories, by the obstruction theory arguments as in the literature
[
9
,
21
]
. But their statements are all about single or finitely many Lagrangians, so we make the version for countably many Lagrangians; see Theorem
2.26
.

The gapped
A_{\infty}
categories are curved
A_{\infty}
categories, including the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
terms. We make them into strict
A_{\infty}
categories by adding bounding cochains, following the references
[
21
,
9
]
. We define then the wrapped Fukaya categories by the localization method, which has been used already in the literature
[
26
]
(the idea is due to Paul Seidel and the first author). The advantage of this is that we can make the definition only from the algebraic properties of Hamiltonian continuations, without going back to the study of pseudo-holomorphic curve moduli spaces.

We prove the split-generation theorem by a method of Paul Seidel and the first author (also from their unpublished work). This is related to the proof of Fukaya, Seidel and Smith
[
24
]
for exact Lagrangians (they assume that the ground field has characteristic
\neq 2
). They use Lefschetz fibrations, Seidel long exact sequences, and equivariant Fukaya categories, and we replace the last ingredient with the use of Viterbo restriction functors. When working with non-exact Lagrangians, the first two ingredients have already appeared in the literature, so we focus on a discussion of Viterbo restriction functors in the non-exact setting. After providing the argument for this generalisation, the remainder of the proof extends fairly immediately.

There is another method
[
2
]
of proving the split-generation theorem, using symplectic cohomology (for time-one periodic Hamiltonian orbits). One should then prove that it has the same relation as in the exact case to the algebraic invariants obtained from the Fukaya category.
One could do this in closed symplectic manifolds (as will appear in
[
6
]
) and for monotone Lagrangians (as is done already in
[
49
]
) but not yet in the circumstances above.

We begin in §
2.1
with the treatment of Novikov fields.
The major difference from the references
[
21
,
9
]
is that we extend the definition of Novikov fields used in the references
[
21
,
9
]
; see Definition
2.2
. We do this because we will use Lie–Kolchin’s theorem, Theorem
3.17
, for the proof of Theorem
1.3
. We shall therefore need the Novikov fields to be algebraically closed, and our definition will be suitable for this purpose.

In §
2.2
we define the gapped
A_{\infty}
categories and provide the obstruction theory arguments. Apart from the issue mentioned above, the major difference from the references
[
21
,
9
]
is that we extend the definition of gapped
A_{\infty}
algebras; see Remark
2.15
.
We do this because we include filtered local systems (in the sense of Definition
2.12
) in the Fukaya categories.

The gapped structure will be used only for the process of defining the curved
A_{\infty}
categories, which we shall in practice be able to treat as
filtered

A_{\infty}
categories (in the sense of Definition
2.14
) forgetting the gap conditions.

In §§
2.3
and
2.4
we define the curved
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
of a symplectic manifold
X.

In §
2.5
we define the strict
A_{\infty}
categories
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X),{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
including the bounding cochains.
In §
2.6
we recall generalized Thomas–Yau theorems.
In §
2.7
we explain the effect of symplectic and Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms upon the Fukaya categories.
In §
2.8
we introduce the wrapped Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
by the localisation method.
In §
2.9
we define the exact Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
and show that its cohomology category
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
is equivalent to that in the literature.
In §
2.10
we turn to the study of cotangent bundles; we prove two lemmas we shall need to deal with McLean perturbations, which we will use to prove Theorem
1.3
(ii).
In §
2.11
we prove the split-generation theorem for the Fukaya categories in cotangent bundles.
In §
2.12
we study the Yoneda functors obtained from the split-generation theorem.

2.1
Novikov Fields

We begin by recalling the definition of non-Archimedean valued fields. We call them valued fields for short.

Definition 2.1
.

By a
valued field
we mean the pair
(K,{\mathbf{v}})
of a field
K
and a function
{\mathbf{v}}:K\to(-\infty,\infty],
called a valuation, such that
{\mathbf{v}}^{-1}(+\infty)=\{0\}
and for
a,b\in K
we have
{\mathbf{v}}(ab)={\mathbf{v}}a+{\mathbf{v}}b
and
{\mathbf{v}}(a+b)\geqslant\min\{{\mathbf{v}}a,{\mathbf{v}}b\}.

By a valued field
extension
of
(K,{\mathbf{v}})
we mean a valued field
(K^{\prime},{\mathbf{v}})
such that
K
is a sub-field of
K^{\prime}
and the two valuations agree on
K.
We use the same symbol
{\mathbf{v}}
for different valuations.

Every valued field
(K,{\mathbf{v}})
has a sub-ring
K^{0}\subset K
defined by
K^{0}:=\{a\in K:{\mathbf{v}}a\geqslant 0\},
which is a local ring with maximal ideal
K^{+}:=\{a\in K:{\mathbf{v}}a>0\};
in other words,
a\in K^{0}
is a unit if and only if
{\mathbf{v}}a=0.

Let
(K,{\mathbf{v}})
be a valued field and define a function
|\,\,|:K\to[0,\infty)
by
|a|:=e^{-{\mathbf{v}}a}
for
a\in K.
Then for
a,b\in K
the
strong
triangle inequality
|a+b|\leqslant\penalty\max\{|a|,|b|\}
holds and there is a distance function
{\rm d}:K\times K\to[0,\infty)
defined by
(a,b)\mapsto|a-b|,
making
K
into a metric space
(K,{\rm d}).

We call
(K,{\mathbf{v}})
a
complete
valued field if
(K,{\rm d})
is complete.

We generalize the definition of Novikov fields in the references
[
9
,
21
]
.

Definition 2.2
.

Define the
minimum Novikov field

(\Lambda^{\min},{\mathbf{v}})
to be the Novikov field in the literature
[
9
,
21
]
without the Maslov index factor
e
in their notation. We begin by recalling this. Let
{\mathbf{k}}
be a field and
{\mathbf{k}}[{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}]
its group ring. Denote by
T\in{\mathbf{k}}[{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}]
the formal variable corresponding to
1\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
so that every element
a\in{\mathbf{k}}[{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}]
may be written as
\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}}a_{\gamma}T^{\gamma},\ a_{\gamma}\in{\mathbf{k}}
with
\supp a:=\{\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}:a_{\gamma}\neq 0\}
a
finite
subset of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

Define a function
{\mathbf{v}}:{\mathbf{k}}[{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}]\to(-\infty,\infty]
by
a\mapsto\min(\supp a).
Then the function
|\,\,|:{\mathbf{k}}[{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}]\to[0,\infty)
defined by
|a|:=e^{-{\mathbf{v}}a}
for
a\in\Lambda
satisfies the strong triangle inequalities, making
{\mathbf{k}}[{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}]
into a metric space; and
\Lambda^{\min}
is its completion. The convolution products extend to the completion, making
\Lambda^{\min}
into a ring. This is in fact a field because of the inversion formula
(1-a)^{-1}=1+a+a^{2}+\dots
for
a\in{\mathbf{k}}[{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}]
with
{\mathbf{v}}a>0.
The function
{\mathbf{v}}
extends to the completion, making it into a complete valued field
(\Lambda^{\min},{\mathbf{v}}).

We denote an element of
\Lambda^{\min}
by an infinite sum
\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}}a_{\gamma}T^{\gamma},\ a_{\gamma}\in{\mathbf{k}},
with
\supp a:=\{\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}:a_{\gamma}\neq 0\}
a
discrete
subset of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

By
a Novikov field
we mean a complete valued field extension of
(\Lambda^{\min},{\mathbf{v}}).

We call
{\mathbf{k}}
the
ground
field of the Novikov field.

We prove now that algebraically closed Novikov fields exist for arbitrary characteristic. We give two proofs: one is Krasner’s lemma, Lemma
2.5
, a general fact about valued fields, and the other is an explicit construction in Proposition
2.6
(ii).

Let
(K,{\mathbf{v}})
be a valued field. Define a subring
K^{0}\subseteq K
defined by

K^{0}:=\{a\in K:{\mathbf{v}}a\geqslant 0\}.
This has a unique maximal ideal
K^{+}:=\{a\in K:{\mathbf{v}}a>0\}
and the residue field

\rf K:=K^{0}/K^{+}.

The
value group

\vg K\subseteq{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
is a subgroup of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
defined by

\vg K:={\mathbf{v}}(K\setminus\{0\})\subseteq{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

We recall the following basic facts:

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
K
be a complete valued field, and
L/K
an algebraic extension;
then the valuation of
K
extends uniquely to
L
so that:

(a)

if
L/K
has finite degree, the valuation on
L
is complete; and

(b)

if
L/K
has infinite degree, the valuation of
L
is strictly not complete.

Also, if
L/K
is finite then

[L:K]\geqslant[\rf L:\rf K][\vg L:\vg K].

(2.1)

More precisely, there exist two intermediate fields of
L/K,

the maximal unramified field
L^{\rm unr}
and the maximal tamely-ramified field
L^{\rm tame},
such that:

(i)

K\subseteq L^{\rm tame}\subseteq L^{\rm unr}\subseteq L;

(ii)

\rf K\subseteq\rf L^{\rm unr}=\rf L^{\rm tame}\subseteq\rf L;

(iii)

\vg K=\vg L^{\rm unr}\subseteq\vg L^{\rm tame}\subseteq\vg L,

[\vg L^{\rm tame}:\vg L^{\rm unr}]=[L^{\rm tame}:L^{\rm unr}];

(iv)

if
\rf K
has characteristic
p=0
then
L=L^{\rm tame},
and if
p>0
then
[L:L^{\rm tame}]
is a power of
p.

Proof.

We consult Neukirch
[
45
]
;
the referred statements in what follows are all from Chapter II of this book.

The unique extension is by Theorem 4.8.
By Proposition 4.9 the extended valuation is equivalent to the
\ell^{\infty}
norm on a
direct sum
of
K,

whose elements have only finitely many nonzero components even if they are infinite dimensional.
So they are complete if and only if finite-dimensional.

The inequality (
2.1
) is from Proposition 6.8.
The field
L^{\rm unr}
is defined in Definition 7.4, and
L^{\rm tame}
in Definition 7.10 but under the hypothesis that
\rf K
has positive characteristic;
if
\rf K
has characteristic
0
we define
L^{\rm tame}:=L.

The parts (i), (ii) and (iv) are then obvious.
The part (iii) follows from Proposition 7.7; this is stated again in the positive-characteristic case,
but its proof applies also to the characteristic-zero case.
∎

Here the extension
L/L^{\rm tame}
is called the
wild
part of
L/K,

which is in general difficult to study.
But it has degree a power of
p;
and for
Galois
extensions of such a degree, there is a classification result:

Proposition 2.4

(Artin–Schreier)
.

Let
K
be any field of characteristic
p.

Then for every
a\in K
either:

(i)

the polynomial
f_{a}=f_{a}(x):=x^{p}-x-a\in K[x]
has a root
b\in K,

in which case its roots
b,b+1,\cdots,b+p-1
are all in
K;
or

(ii)

f_{a}\in K[x]
is irreducible, in which case
K[x]/f_{a}

is a degree-
p
Galois extension of
K.

Conversely:

(iii)

every degree-
p
Galois extension of
K
is of the form
K[x]/f_{a}
for some
a\in K;
or more generally

(iv)

every finite Galois extension of
K
of degree equal to a power of
p
is a repeated extension of this kind.

Proof.

For (i)–(iii) consult for instance Lang
[
41
, Chapter VI, Theorem 6.4]
;
and then for (iv) use the following fact
[
41
, Chapter I, Corollary 6.6]
:
for every finite group
G
of order a power of
p
there exists a normal series

G=G_{0}\supseteq G_{1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq G_{n}=\{1\}

(2.2)

with
[G_{i}:G_{i-1}]=p
for every
i.

∎

Finally we recall:

Lemma 2.5

(Krasner)
.

Every valued field
K
has a complete algebraically-closed extension.

Proof.

Let
L
be the algebraic closure of
K.

The valuation extends uniquely to
L
by the first assertion in Proposition
2.3
,
because
L
is the union of finite subextensions of
K.

The completion of this valued field
L
is algebraically closed, by Krasner’s lemma
[
45
, Chapter II, §6, Exercise 2]
.
∎

We turn now to the second method.
Let
{\mathbf{k}}
be a field. In Definition
2.2
we have defined the minimum Novikov field
\Lambda^{\min}
with ground field
{\mathbf{k}}.

We define the
maximum
Novikov field by

\Lambda^{\max}:=\left\{a=\sum_{\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}}a_{\gamma}T^{\gamma}:a_{\gamma}\in{\mathbf{k}}\text{ and }\supp a\subseteq{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\text{ well-ordered}\right\}

(2.3)

where
\supp a:=\{\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}:a_{\gamma}\neq 0\}.

Recall that
\supp a
being well ordered means that every nonempty subset has a minimum.
In particular,
\supp a
is
half discrete
in the sense that
for every
\gamma\in\supp a

there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\supp a\cap(\gamma,\gamma+\epsilon)=\emptyset;

(2.4)

because we can take
\gamma+\epsilon
to be the minimum of
\supp a\cap(\gamma,\infty).

The sums and products in
\Lambda^{\max}
are defined in the natural way: for
a,b\in\Lambda^{\max}
we can define

a+b:=\sum_{\gamma\in\supp a\cup\supp b}(a_{\gamma}+b_{\gamma})T^{\gamma}

(2.5)

because
\supp a\cup\supp b
is well ordered; and

ab:=\sum_{\gamma}\left(\sum_{\alpha+\beta=\gamma}a_{\alpha}b_{\beta}\right)T^{\gamma}

(2.6)

because
\sum_{\alpha+\beta=\gamma}
is finite, which follows since
\supp a,\supp b
are half discrete.

The valuation
{\mathbf{v}}:\Lambda^{\max}\to(-\infty,+\infty]
is defined by setting
{\mathbf{v}}0:=+\infty
and by taking in (
2.3
) the least
\gamma\in\supp a.

Then
(\Lambda^{\max},{\mathbf{v}})
is a complete valued field.
We have

\rf\Lambda^{\min}\cong\rf\Lambda^{\max}\cong{\mathbf{k}}\text{ and }\vg\Lambda^{\min}\cong\vg\Lambda^{\max}={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

(2.7)

We prove now the following proposition. Part (ii) with
{\mathbf{k}}
algebraically closed is the result we have promised to prove.

Proposition 2.6
.

(i)

\Lambda^{\min},\Lambda^{\max}
are both perfect.

(ii)

\Lambda^{\max}
is maximal in Krull’s sense
[
40
]
;
that is,
\Lambda^{\max}
has no nontrivial valued field extension
K
with
\rf K\cong{\mathbf{k}}
and
\vg K={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

In particular, if
{\mathbf{k}}
is algebraically closed, so is
\Lambda^{\max}.

(iii)

If
{\mathbf{k}}
is algebraically closed of characteristic
0,
so is
\Lambda^{\min}.

If
{\mathbf{k}}
has positive characteristic
p
then
\Lambda^{\min}
is not algebraically closed,
and its algebraic closure is strictly not complete.

Remark 2.7
.

These results do not assert that
\Lambda^{\max}
is the algebraic closure of
\Lambda^{\min}.
For rational-power series, the algebraic closure is described in Kedlaya
[
38
]
.

Proof of Proposition
2.6
.

For (i)
let
\Lambda
be either
\Lambda^{\min}
or
\Lambda^{\max}.

By definition, a field
\Lambda
is perfect if either it has characteristic
p=0
or the ring homomorphism
\Lambda\to\Lambda
defined by
x\mapsto x^{p}
is surjective.
Suppose
p>0
and consider the polynomial
x^{p}-a\in\Lambda[x],

a\in\Lambda.

We show that this has a root in
\Lambda.

If
a=0
it has root
0\in\Lambda.

If
a\neq 0
we can write
a=T^{{\mathbf{v}}a}b(1+c),

b\in{\mathbf{k}}^{*}
and
c\in\Lambda^{+}.

Let
b^{1/p}\in{\mathbf{k}}
be a root of
x^{p}-b\in{\mathbf{k}}[x],
which exists because
{\mathbf{k}}
is algebraically closed.
Define

(1+c)^{1/p}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\binom{1/p}{k}c^{k}\in\Lambda.

(2.8)

Then
T^{{\mathbf{v}}a/p}b^{1/p}(1+c)^{1/p}\in\Lambda
is a desired root.

The former part of (ii) is an old result
[
36
,
40
]
.
For the latter, if
{\mathbf{k}}
is algebraically closed, then
\rf\Lambda\cong{\mathbf{k}}
is algebraically maximal and
\vg\Lambda={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
is already maximal in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}};

so
\Lambda
has no nontrivial finite extension, or equivalently
\Lambda
is algebraically closed.

The former part of (iii) is proved by Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
22
, Lemma A.1]
but we give another proof, using Proposition
2.3
with
K=\Lambda^{\min}.
Suppose therefore that
L/K
is a finite extension, and note that:

•

since
\rf K
is algebraically closed it follows that the inclusions in (ii) are all equalities, so
K=L^{\rm unr};

•

since
\vg K={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
it follows that the inclusions in (iii) are all equalities, so
L^{\rm unr}=L^{\rm tame};
and

•

L=L^{\rm tame}
because
\rf K
has characteristic zero.

Consequently,
K=L;
that is,
K=\Lambda
has no nontrivial finite extension, or equivalently,
K
is algebraically closed.

For the latter part of (iii) let
\gamma<0
and consider the Artin–Schreier polynomial

x^{p}-x-T^{\gamma}\in\Lambda^{\min}[x].

(2.9)

If this had a root of the form
x=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_{n}T^{\gamma_{n}}
then by induction on
n
it must be of the form

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}T^{-\gamma/p^{n}}\in\Lambda^{\max}\setminus\Lambda^{\min};

(2.10)

so
\Lambda^{\min}
is not algebraically closed.

We show next that the algebraic closure of
\Lambda^{\min}
has infinite degree over
\Lambda^{\min}.

By an
Artin–Schreier
element over
K:=\Lambda^{\min}
we shall mean a root of an Artin–Schreier polynomial over
K.

Let
L/K
be the field extension which contains all the Artin–Schreier elements over
K.

We prove the following three facts in this order:

(I)

if
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}\in K
with
a_{0}=a_{1}+a_{2}
and if
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in L
are respectively roots of the
polynomials
x^{p}-x-a_{i}\in K[x],

i=0,1,2,
then

K(\alpha_{0})\subseteq K(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2});

(II)

if
\alpha
is an Artin–Schreier element over
K
then

K(\alpha)\subseteq K(\beta;\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{n})

where
\beta
is a root of the polynomial
x^{p}-x-b\in K[x]
for some
b\in K
with
{\mathbf{v}}b\geqslant 0,

and every
\delta_{i}
a root of the polynomial
x^{p}-x-c_{i}T^{\gamma_{i}}\in K[x]
for some
c_{i}\in{\mathbf{k}}
and
\gamma_{i}<0;
and

(III)

if the algebraic closure of
\Lambda^{\min}
had
finite
degree over
\Lambda^{\min},

we should have

L=K(\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m};\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{n})

where
\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m}
are such as
\beta
in (II) and
\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{n}
are under the same condition as in (II) but that
n
may of course be different.

For (I) note that
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
is a root of the polynomial
x^{p}-x-a_{0}\in K[x],
so
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+c
for some
c\in\{0,1,\cdots,p-1\},

which implies
K(\alpha_{0})\subseteq K(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
as we want.
For (II) write
\alpha
as a root of the polynomial
x^{p}-x-a\in K[x],

a\in K=\Lambda^{\min},
and recall that
a
has by definition only finitely many terms of negative powers with respect to
T;
that is,
a=b+\sum c_{i}T^{\gamma_{i}},

{\mathbf{v}}b\geqslant 0
and
\gamma_{i}<0.

Applying to this the part (I) repeatedly, we see that
K(\alpha)\subseteq K(\beta;\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{n})
as we want.
For (III) it is clear from definition that

L\supseteq K(\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{m};\delta_{1},\cdots,\delta_{n}).

On the other hand, by our hypothesis
L/K
is a finite extension and so is obtained from
K
by adding finite Artin–Schreier elements.
Hence using (II) repeatedly we get the other inclusion we want.

Now in (III) write
\delta_{i}
as a root of the polynomial
x^{p}-x-c_{i}T^{\gamma_{i}}\in K[x]
for some
c_{i}\in{\mathbf{k}}
and
\gamma_{i}<0.

Then
\delta_{i}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{i}^{1/p^{n}}T^{\gamma_{i}/p^{n}}
for some
c_{i}^{1/p^{n}}\in\overline{{\mathbf{k}}},
the algebraic closure of
{\mathbf{k}},
so

L\subseteq\left\{\sum a_{\gamma}T^{\gamma}:a_{\gamma}\in\overline{{\mathbf{k}}}\text{ and if $\gamma<0$ then $\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}(p;\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n})$}\right\}.

(2.11)

But as
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
is an infinite extension there is some
\epsilon<0
that is not in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}(p;\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n}).

Then by (
2.11
) the Artin–Schreier element
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}T^{\epsilon/p^{n}},
a root of the polynomial
x^{p}-x-T^{\epsilon}\in K[x],

is not in
L,
which contradicts the definition of
L.

So the algebraic closure of
\Lambda^{\min}
has infinite degree over
\Lambda^{\min}
and by Proposition
2.3
(b) it is strictly not complete.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

The rôle of Artin–Schreier polynonimals is essential to the proof above.
In fact, if
{\mathbf{k}}
is algebraically closed then every finite
normal
extension
L/\Lambda^{\min}
is a repeated Artin–Schreier extension, as we show now.
In the notation of Proposition
2.3
with
K=\Lambda^{\min},
we have again
K=L^{\rm tame}.

On the other hand, by (i) above,
L/L^{\rm tame}
is automatically Galois.
To this we can apply Proposition
2.4
so
L/\Lambda^{\min}
is a repeated Artin–Schreier extension as we want.

2.2
Gapped
A_{\infty}
Categories

We introduce the notion of valued vector spaces.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
(\Lambda,{\mathbf{v}})
be a Novikov field. By a
valued
\Lambda
-vector space
we mean the pair
(E,{\mathbf{v}})
of a
\Lambda
-vector space
E
and a function
{\mathbf{v}}:E\to(-\infty,+\infty],
called also the
valuation,
such that

{\mathbf{v}}^{-1}(+\infty)=\{0\};
for
x,y\in E
we have
{\mathbf{v}}(x+y)\geqslant\min\{{\mathbf{v}}x,{\mathbf{v}}y\};
and
for
a\in\Lambda,\ x\in E
we have
{\mathbf{v}}(ax)={\mathbf{v}}a+{\mathbf{v}}x
where we use both
{\mathbf{v}}:\Lambda\to(-\infty,\infty]
and
{\mathbf{v}}:E\to(-\infty,\infty].

Define a function
|\,\,|:E\to[0,\infty)
by
|x|:=e^{-{\mathbf{v}}x}
for
x\in E.
Then the strong triangle inequality
|x+y|\leqslant\penalty\max\{|x|,|y|\}
holds for
x,y\in E;
and in particular, there is a distance function
{\rm d}:E\times E\to[0,\infty)
defined by
(x,y)\mapsto|x-y|,
making
E
into a metric space
(E,{\rm d}).

We call
(E,{\mathbf{v}})
a
complete
valued vector space if
(E,{\rm d})
is a complete metric space.
The valued vector space structure of
(E,{\mathbf{v}})
extends to the metric space completion of
(E,{\rm d}),
making it into a complete valued vector space. We call this the
completion
of
(E,{\mathbf{v}}).

Let
(E,{\mathbf{v}}),(F,{\mathbf{v}})
be valued
\Lambda
-vector spaces and
\alpha:E\to F
a
\Lambda
-linear map which is continuous with respect to the metric space structures above.
Then there exists
{\mathbf{v}}\alpha\in(-\infty,\infty]
by
{\mathbf{v}}\alpha:=\inf_{x\in E}({\mathbf{v}}(\alpha x)-{\mathbf{v}}x);
that is,
|\alpha|:=e^{-{\mathbf{v}}\alpha}
is the operator norm of the bounded operator
\alpha:(E,|\,\,|)\to(F,|\,\,|).

We say that
\alpha
is
filtered
if
{\mathbf{v}}\alpha\geqslant 0.

We call it a
filtered isomorphism
if
\alpha
is a linear isomorphism with
\alpha,\alpha^{-1}
filtered. The latter is equivalent to
{\mathbf{v}}\alpha={\mathbf{v}}(\alpha^{-1})=0
or to
|\alpha|=|\alpha^{-1}|=1
because
|\alpha\beta|\leqslant\penalty|\alpha||\beta|
for linear maps
\alpha,\beta
between valued vector spaces.

Let
(E_{1},{\mathbf{v}}),\dots,(E_{d},{\mathbf{v}}),(F,{\mathbf{v}})
be valued
\Lambda
-vector spaces and
\alpha:E_{1}\times\dots\times E_{k}\to F
a
\Lambda
multi-linear map.
Define then
{\mathbf{v}}\alpha\in[-\infty,\infty]
by

{\mathbf{v}}\alpha:=\textstyle\inf_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in E_{1}\times\dots\times E_{k}}({\mathbf{v}}[\alpha(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})]-{\mathbf{v}}x_{1}-\dots-{\mathbf{v}}x_{k}).

(2.12)

We say that
\alpha
is
filtered
if
{\mathbf{v}}\alpha\geqslant 0.

We denote by
\hom(E_{1},\dots,E_{k};F)
the set of filtered
\Lambda
multi-linear maps from
E_{1}\times\dots\times E_{k}
to
F.

The pair
(\hom(E_{1},\dots,E_{k};F),{\mathbf{v}})
has the obvious structure of a valued
\Lambda
-vector space.
If
(F,{\mathbf{v}})
is complete then so is
(\hom(E_{1},\dots,E_{k};F),{\mathbf{v}}).

Let
(E_{i},{\mathbf{v}})_{i\in I}
be a family of complete valued
\Lambda
-vector spaces. Then there exists a valuation
\bigoplus_{i\in I}E_{i}\to(-\infty,\infty]
defined by
(x_{i})_{i\in I}\mapsto\min_{i\in I}{\mathbf{v}}x_{i}.

The
complete direct sum

(\widehat{\bigoplus}_{i\in I}E_{i},{\mathbf{v}})
is the completion of the direct sum
(\bigoplus_{i\in I}E_{i},{\mathbf{v}}).

We give a basic example of finite-dimensional valued
\Lambda
-vector space.

Example 2.10
.

Let
n>0
be an integer and
E
an
n
-dimensional
\Lambda
-vector space. We give
E
a certain valuation. Let
\bm{e}:=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}\subset E
be a basis with
{\mathbf{v}}e_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}e_{n}=0,
which exists because
T^{\gamma}\in\Lambda
for every
\gamma\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

Then
E
has a valuation
{\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{e}}:E\to(-\infty,\infty]
defined by
x=x_{1}e_{1}+\dots+x_{n}e_{n}\mapsto\min\{{\mathbf{v}}x_{1},\dots,{\mathbf{v}}x_{n}\}
for
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\Lambda.

By the strong triangle inequality every valuation
{\mathbf{v}}
on
E
satisfies
{\mathbf{v}}x\geqslant{\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{e}}x
for
x\in E.

In particular, if
\bm{f}=\{f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\}\subset E
is another basis with
{\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{f}}f_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{f}}f_{n}=0
then
{\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{f}}x\geqslant{\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{e}}x
for
x\in E.
Swapping the rôles of
\bm{f},\bm{e}
we get the reverse inequality; that is,
{\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{f}}={\mathbf{v}}_{\bm{e}},
which we call the
\ell^{\infty}
valuation on
E.

We give three other equivalent definitions of the
\ell^{\infty}
valuation on
E.

Let
{\mathbf{v}}:E\to(-\infty,\infty]
be any valuation.
We use the subring
\Lambda^{0}\subset\Lambda
defined in Definition
2.2
.
Regard
E
as an
\Lambda^{0}
module and define a
\Lambda^{0}
submodule
E^{0}\subset E
by
E^{0}:=\{e\in E:{\mathbf{v}}e\geqslant 0\}.

Then
\Lambda^{+}E^{0}:=\{ae:a\in\Lambda^{+},\ e\in E^{0}\}
is a
\Lambda^{0}
submodule of
E^{0}
and the quotient
\bar{E}:=E^{0}/\Lambda^{+}E^{0}
has the vector space structure over
\Lambda^{0}/\Lambda^{+}=:K.

We prove that the following four conditions are equivalent:
(i)

{\mathbf{v}}
is the
\ell^{\infty}
valuation;
(ii)

E^{0}
is a rank-
n
free
\Lambda^{0}
module;
(iii)

E^{0}
is a finitely generated
\Lambda^{0}
module; and
(iv)
the
K
-vector space
E^{0}/\Lambda^{+}E^{0}
has dimension
\geqslant n.

Clearly (i) implies (ii) and (ii) implies (iii). We prove that (iii) implies (iv). Take
e_{1},\dots,e_{p}\in E^{0}
such that their images
\bar{e}_{1},\dots,\bar{e}_{p}\in E^{0}/\Lambda^{+}E^{0}
are a
K
-basis. Then as
E^{0}
is finitely generated we can use Nakayama’s lemma
[
41
, Chapter X, Lemma 4.3]
(for non-Noetherian local rings) which implies
E^{0}=\Lambda^{0}e_{1}+\dots+\Lambda^{0}e_{p}.
So
E=\Lambda e_{1}+\dots+\Lambda e_{p}
and
p\geqslant n,
which is the condition (iv).
We prove now that (iv) implies (i). Take
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\in E^{0}
such that their images
\bar{e}_{1},\dots,\bar{e}_{n}\in E^{0}/\Lambda^{+}E^{0}
are linearly independent over
K.

Then
F:=\Lambda^{0}e_{1}+\dots+\Lambda^{0}e_{n}\subseteq E^{0}
and we show that the equality holds.
Otherwise there is some
x\in E^{0}\setminus F.

By re-ordering
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
we can write
x=a_{1}T^{\gamma_{1}}e_{1}+\dots+a_{m}T^{\gamma_{m}}e_{m}
where
m\leqslant\penalty n,

a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in E\setminus\{0\}
with
{\mathbf{v}}a_{1}=\dots={\mathbf{v}}a_{m}=0,

\gamma_{1}=\dots=\gamma_{l}<\gamma_{l+1}\leqslant\penalty\dots\leqslant\penalty\gamma_{m}.

Then

T^{-\gamma_{1}}x=a_{1}e_{1}+\dots+a_{l}e_{l}+a_{l+1}T^{\gamma_{l+1}-\gamma_{1}}+\dots+a_{m}T^{\gamma_{m}-\gamma_{1}}e_{m}

and
T^{-\gamma_{1}}x,a_{l+1}T^{\gamma_{l+1}-\gamma_{1}}+\dots+a_{m}T^{\gamma_{m}-\gamma_{1}}e_{m}
have positive valuation. So
\bar{a}_{1}\bar{e}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{l}\bar{e}_{l}=0
in
\bar{E}.
But none of
\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{l}
is zero, which contradicts that
\bar{e}_{1},\dots,\bar{e}_{n}
are linearly independent. Thus (i)–(iv) are equivalent.

Remark 2.11
.

Consider the category whose objects are
n
-dimensional valued
\Lambda
-vector spaces, whose morphisms are filtered homomorphisms.
Then there is an equivalence from the category of rank-
n
free
\Lambda^{0}
modules to that of
n
-dimensional valued vector spaces with
\ell^{\infty}
valuations. The function between the object sets is defined by
F\mapsto F\otimes_{\Lambda^{0}}\Lambda.
Each morphism
\phi\in\hom(F,F^{\prime})
is mapped to
\phi\otimes{\rm id}.

When
n=1
every valuation is the
\ell^{\infty}
valuation. So the category of rank-
1
free
\Lambda^{0}
modules is equivalent to that of
1
-dimensional filtered local systems.

We define then the filtered local systems.

Definition 2.12
.

By a
filtered
local system over a topological space we mean one of non-zero finite-dimensional valued vector spaces whose parallel transports are filtered homomorphisms of valued vector spaces.

We make now the relevant definitions about discrete sub-monoids of
[0,\infty).

Definition 2.13
.

Denote by
{\mathbb{N}}
the set of non-negative integers.
Let
\Gamma\subset[0,\infty)
be a discrete
sub-monoid,
that is, a discrete subset containing
0
and closed under addition.
By a
decomposition
of
(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
we mean
l\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{0\},

a=(a_{0},\dots,a_{l})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{\times(l+1)}
with
0=a_{0}\leqslant\penalty\dots\leqslant\penalty a_{l}=k,
and
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{l})\in\Gamma^{\times(l+1)}
with
\alpha_{0}+\dots+\alpha_{l}=\gamma,
such that
(l,\alpha_{0}),(a_{1}-a_{0},\alpha_{1}),\dots,(a_{l}-a_{l-1},\alpha_{l})\neq(0,0).

By a
reduced
decomposition of
(k,\gamma)
we mean
p,q\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\in\Gamma
with
0\leqslant\penalty p\leqslant\penalty q\leqslant\penalty k,

\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}=\gamma
and
(k+p-q+1,\alpha_{0}),(q-p,\alpha_{1})\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\};

see Equation (
2.15
) below for a typical formula where this notion is used.
Every decomposition
(l,a,\alpha)
with
a_{0}=0,\dots,a_{p}=p
and
a_{p+1}=q,\dots,a_{l}=q+l-p-1
for some
p,q
defines indeed the reduced decomposition
(p,q;\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}).

Choose a total order
\leqslant\penalty
on
({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
such that there is an ordered set isomorphism
(({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0\},\leqslant\penalty)\cong({\mathbb{N}},\leqslant\penalty),
which we denote by
(k,\gamma)\mapsto[k,\gamma],
and if
(l,a,\alpha)
are as above a decomposition of
(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
then

\max\{[l,\alpha_{0}],[a_{1}-a_{0},\alpha_{1}],\dots,[a_{l}-a_{l-1},\alpha_{l}]\}\leqslant\penalty[k,\gamma]

(2.13)

with equality if and only if at least
l
of
(l,\alpha_{0}),(a_{1}-a_{0},\alpha_{1}),\dots,(a_{l}-a_{l-1},\alpha_{l})
are equal to
(1,0).

Setting
(l,\alpha_{0})=(k,\gamma)
and
(a_{1}-a_{0},\alpha_{1}),\dots,(a_{l}-a_{l-1},\alpha_{l})=(1,0)
we get
(1,0)\leqslant\penalty(k,\gamma);
that is,
(1,0)
is the least element of
({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0\}.
In other words,
[k,\gamma]=0
if and only if
(k,\gamma)=(1,0).

We choose the order
\leqslant\penalty
for
every
discrete sub-monoid
\Gamma\subset[0,\infty)
so that if
\Gamma,\Delta\subset[0,\infty)
are two discrete sub-monoids with
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
then the induced map
({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0\}\to({\mathbb{N}}\times\Delta)\setminus\{(0,0\}
will preserve the orders.
Here is an explicit way of doing this. Set
[0]:=0
and for
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
set

[\gamma]:=\max\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}:\gamma=\gamma_{1}+\cdots+\gamma_{n};\ \gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}\}.

(2.14)

The maximum makes sense because
\Gamma\subseteq[0,\infty)
is discrete and has the least positive element.
It is clear that
[\gamma+\delta]\geqslant[\gamma]+[\delta]
for
\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma.
For
(k,\gamma),(l,\delta)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
define
(k,\gamma)\leqslant\penalty(l,\delta)
by either
k+[\gamma]+\gamma<l+[\delta]+\delta
or
k+[\gamma]+\gamma=l+[\delta]+\delta
and
\gamma\leqslant\penalty\delta.
Then we can check all the conditions above.

Let
(\Lambda,{\mathbf{v}})
be a Novikov field, which we shall fix in this section.
We define the notion of gapped
A_{N}
categories,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\},
over
(\Lambda,{\mathbf{v}}).

The morphisms spaces are
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded.
The
A_{\infty}
structures are
curved,
including the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
terms.

Definition 2.14
.

We begin with a
filtered
curved
A_{\infty}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
which consists of an object set
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
a family
({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathbf{v}})_{X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded complete valued
\Lambda
-vector spaces, and a family
({\mathfrak{m}}^{k}={\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}\in\hom^{1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1])),

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
such that

0=\sum(-1)^{\#}{\mathfrak{m}}^{k+p-q+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},{\mathfrak{m}}^{q-p}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})

(2.15)

where

x_{1}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,x_{k}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];

\#:=\deg x_{1}+\dots+\deg x_{p};
and
p,q
are any integers with
0\leqslant\penalty p\leqslant\penalty q\leqslant\penalty k.

For
k=0
we have
{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}_{X}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{1}_{XX}
for every
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
and (
2.15
) means
{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}_{XX}{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}_{X}=0\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{2}_{XX}.

Suppose now given
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

By a
gapped

A_{N}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
we mean an object set
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
a family
({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathbf{v}})_{X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded complete valued
\Lambda
-vector spaces, a discrete sub-monoid
\Gamma\subset[0,\infty)
and a family

({\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma}={\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}\in\hom^{1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1])),

(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with
[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty N
and
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
such that

0=\sum(-1)^{\#}{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}^{k+p-q+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a},{\mathfrak{m}}_{\beta}^{q-p}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})

(2.16)

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},\#,p,q
are as in (
2.15
) and the sum is taken over reduced decompositions
(p,q;\alpha,\beta)
’s of
(k,\gamma).

The operators
{\mathfrak{m}}_{\alpha}^{k+p-q+1},{\mathfrak{m}}_{\beta}^{q-p}
are well-defined because
\max\{[q-p,\alpha],[k+p-q+1,\beta]\}\leqslant\penalty N
by (
2.13
).

When
N=\infty
the gapped
A_{\infty}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
defines a filtered
A_{\infty}
category of the same object set, the same morphism spaces, and the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}
operators defined by
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}:=\sum T^{\gamma}{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
where
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
for
k=0
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
for
k>0.

The sum is well-defined because
\hom^{1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1])
is complete.

Remark 2.15
.

The definition of
\hom^{1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1]))
implies that every
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
is a
filtered
homomorphism between valued vector spaces.
This will be crucial to defining the Fukaya categories with filtered local systems.
In the references
[
9
,
21
]
they define
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
to be linear over the residue field
\Lambda^{0}/\Lambda^{+},
determined
uniquely
from the sum
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}};
this is not true in our circumstances.

They also work with gapped
A_{N}

algebras
which are gapped
A_{N}
categories with a single object; or they are expressible as the pair
(A,{\mathfrak{m}})
of a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded complete valued
\Lambda
-vector space
A
and a family
{\mathfrak{m}}=({\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma}\in\hom^{1}(A,\dots,A;A)),

(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with
[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty N,
satisfying the same equations.
If
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
is a gapped
A_{N}
category then
\widehat{\bigoplus}_{X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},
the complete direct sum, has a gapped
A_{N}
algebra structure
{\mathfrak{m}}
defined in the obvious way. Conversely, let
(A,{\mathfrak{m}})
be a gapped
A_{N}
algebra with
A=\widehat{\bigoplus}_{X,Y\in{\mathbin{\cal X}}}{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}
such that the induced map
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma}:A_{X_{1}Y_{1}}\times\dots\times A_{X_{k}Y_{k}}\to A
is zero unless
Y_{1}=X_{2},\dots,Y_{k-1}=X_{k};
and if this is the case then
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma}
has image in
A_{X_{1}Y_{k}}.
Then
{\mathbin{\cal X}},({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY})_{X,Y\in{\mathbin{\cal X}}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
define a gapped
A_{N}
category.

We define the truncations of gapped
A_{N}
functors,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\},
and the full subcategories of gapped
A_{N}
functors.

Definition 2.16
.

Let
M,N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}
with
M\leqslant\penalty N
and
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
a gapped
A_{N}
category.
The
A_{M}

truncation
of
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
means the same object set
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
the same morphism spaces
({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY})_{X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
and the subfamily
({\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}})_{[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty M};
these define a gapped
A_{M}
category.
By a
full subcategory

({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{m}})
of
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
we mean a subset
\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}\subseteq\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
the subfamily
({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY})_{X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}}
and the subfamily
({\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}})_{X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}};
these define a gapped
A_{N}
category.

We define the gapped
A_{N}
functors,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}}
be two filtered
A_{\infty}
categories.
By a
filtered
A_{\infty}
functor
from
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
we mean a function
f:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal B}}
between the object sets and a family

(f^{k}=f^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}\in\hom^{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{fX_{0}fX_{k}}[1])),

(2.17)

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
such that
(i)
for every
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
the element
f^{0}_{X}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{fXfX}^{0}
has positive valuation, that is,
{\mathbf{v}}f^{0}_{X}>0;
and for
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

\begin{split}\sum(-1)^{\#}f^{k+p-q+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},{\mathfrak{m}}^{q-p}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})\\
=\sum{\mathfrak{n}}^{l}(f^{a_{1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a_{1}}),\dots,f^{a_{l}-a_{l-1}}(x_{a_{l-1}+1},\dots,x_{a_{l}}).\end{split}

(2.18)

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},\#,p,q
are as in (
2.15
) and
a_{1},\dots,a_{l}
are any integers with
0\leqslant\penalty a_{1}\leqslant\penalty\dots\leqslant\penalty a_{l}=k.

The right-hand side of (
2.18
) converges by (i) above.

Suppose now that
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
are gapped
A_{N}
categories,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

Note that any two discrete sub-monoids of
[0,\infty)
are contained in some single discrete sub-monoid of
[0,\infty).

By a
gapped
A_{N}
functor
from
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to
{\mathbin{\cal B}}
we mean a function
f:\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}
between the object sets; a discrete sub-monoid
\Gamma\subset[0,\infty)
with respect to which
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}}
are gapped; and a family

(f^{k}_{\gamma}=f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}})\in\hom^{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{fX_{0}fX_{k}}[1]))

(2.19)

(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with
[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty N
and
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
such that

\begin{split}\sum(-1)^{\#}f_{\alpha}^{k+p-q+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},{\mathfrak{m}}_{\beta}^{q-p}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})\\
=\sum{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha_{0}}^{l}(f_{\alpha_{1}}^{a_{1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a_{1}}),\dots,f_{\alpha_{l}}^{a_{l}-a_{l-1}}(x_{a_{l-1}+1},\dots,x_{a_{l}})\end{split}

(2.20)

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},\#,p,q,\alpha,\beta
are as in (
2.16
) and
(l;a_{0},\dots,a_{p};\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{p})
any decomposition of
(k,\gamma).

The operators
{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha_{0}}^{l},f_{\alpha_{1}}^{a_{1}},f_{\alpha_{l}}^{a_{l}-a_{l-1}}
are well-defined because
[l,\alpha_{0}],[a_{1},\alpha_{1}],\dots,[a_{l}-a_{l-1},\alpha_{l}]\leqslant\penalty N
by (
2.13
).

When
N=\infty
the gapped
A_{\infty}
functor
f:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
defines a filtered
A_{\infty}
functor
(f^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}})^{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
between the corresponding filtered
A_{\infty}
categories, with
f^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}:=\sum T^{\gamma}f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
where
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
for
k=0
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
for
k>0.
The sum is well-defined because
\hom^{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{fX_{0}fX_{k}}[1])
is complete.

We define the identity functors of gapped
A_{N}
categories,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

Definition 2.18
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be a filtered
A_{\infty}
category; we leave out
{\mathfrak{m}}
for short.
The
identity
functor
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is defined by taking the function
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to be the identity;
f^{0}_{X}=0\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{0}_{XX}
for
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}};

f^{1}_{XY}:{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}\to{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}
to be the identity for
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}};
and
f^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}=0
for
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
k\geqslant 2.

Suppose now that
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is a gapped
A_{N}
category,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.
Then the identity functor
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is defined by taking the function
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to be the identity;
f^{10}_{XY}:{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}\to{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}
to be the identity for
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}};
and
f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}=0
for
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0),(1,0)\}.

We define the composites of gapped
A_{N}
functors,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

Definition 2.19
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathbin{\cal C}}
be filtered
A_{\infty}
categories and
f:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal B}},

g:{\mathbin{\cal B}}\to{\mathbin{\cal C}}
filtered
A_{\infty}
functors. Then the
composite

A_{\infty}
functor
g\circ f:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal C}}
means the ordinary composite
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal C}}
and the family
(g\circ f)^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}},

X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
k\in{\mathbb{N}},
defined by

(g\circ f)^{k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=\sum(-1)^{\#}g^{l}(f^{a_{1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a_{1}}),\dots,f^{a_{l}-a_{l-1}}(x_{a_{l}+1},\dots,x_{a_{l}}))

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},\#,a_{1},\dots,a_{l}
are as in (
2.18
).

Suppose now that
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathbin{\cal C}}
are filtered
A_{\infty}
categories,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\};
and
f:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal B}},

g:{\mathbin{\cal B}}\to{\mathbin{\cal C}}
filtered
A_{\infty}
functors.
Then the
composite

A_{N}
functor
g\circ f:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal C}}
means the ordinary composite
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal C}};
a discrete sub-monoid
\Gamma\subset[0,\infty)
with respect to which
f,g
are gapped; and the family
(g\circ f)^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}},

(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with
[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty N
and
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
defined by

(g\circ f)^{k}_{\gamma}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}):=\sum(-1)^{\#}g^{l}_{\alpha_{0}}(f_{\alpha_{1}}^{a_{1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a_{1}}),\dots,f_{\alpha_{p}}^{a_{p}-a_{p-1}}(x_{a_{p}+1},\dots,x_{a_{p}}))

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},\#,l,a_{1},\dots,a_{p},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p}
are as in (
2.20
).
This is independent of the choice of
\Gamma,
in the sense that
(g\circ f)^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}=0
for
\gamma
not in the smallest choice of
\Gamma.

We define the
homotopies
of gapped
A_{N}
functors,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

We make a rather strong hypothesis on the functions between object sets.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
be filtered
A_{\infty}
categories and
f,g:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
filtered
A_{\infty}
functors.
Suppose that
f,g
induce the same function
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}
between the object sets, which we denote by
f
for short. By a
homotopy

H:f\Rightarrow g
we mean a family

(H^{k}=H^{k}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{k}}\in\hom^{-1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{fX_{0}fX_{k}}[1])),

(2.21)

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
such that
(i)
for every
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
the element
H^{0}_{X}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{-1}_{fXfX}
has positive valuation, that is,
{\mathbf{v}}H^{0}_{X}>0;
and
(ii)
for
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

\displaystyle\sum(-1)^{\#}H^{k+p-q+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},{\mathfrak{m}}^{q-p}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum{\mathfrak{n}}^{i+j+1}(f^{a_{1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a_{1}}),\dots,f^{a_{i}-a_{i-1}}(x_{a_{i-1}+1},\dots,x_{a_{i}}),

\displaystyle H^{b_{0}-a_{i}}(x_{a_{i}+1},\dots,x_{b_{0}}),g^{b_{1}-b_{0}}(x_{b_{0}+1},\dots,x_{b_{1}}),\dots,g^{b_{j}-b_{j-1}}(x_{b_{j-1}+1},\dots,x_{b_{j}}))

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},\#,p,q
are as in (
2.18
); and
a_{1},\dots,a_{i},b_{0},\dots,b_{j}
any integers with
0\leqslant\penalty a_{1}\leqslant\penalty\dots\leqslant\penalty a_{i}\leqslant\penalty b_{0}\leqslant\penalty b_{1}\leqslant\penalty\dots\leqslant\penalty b_{j}=k.
The right-hand side converges by (i) above.

Suppose now that
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
are gapped
A_{N}
categories,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\};
and
f,g:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
gapped
A_{N}
functors with the same function
f:\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}
between the object sets.
By a
homotopy

H:f\Rightarrow g
we mean a discrete sub-monoid
\Gamma\subset[0,\infty)
with respect to which
f,g
are both gapped and a family

(H^{k}_{\gamma}=H^{k\gamma}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{k}}\in\hom^{-1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{fX_{0}fX_{k}}[1])),

(2.22)

(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with
[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty N
and
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}},
such that

\displaystyle\sum(-1)^{\#}H_{\alpha}^{k+p-q+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},{\mathfrak{m}}_{\beta}^{q-p}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha_{0}}^{i+j+1}(f_{\alpha_{1}}^{a_{1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a_{1}}),\dots,f_{\alpha_{i}}^{a_{i}-a_{i-1}}(x_{a_{i-1}+1},\dots,x_{a_{i}}),

\displaystyle H_{\beta_{0}}^{b_{0}-a_{i}}(x_{a_{i}+1},\dots,x_{b_{0}}),g_{\beta_{1}}^{b_{1}-b_{0}}(x_{b_{0}+1},\dots,x_{b_{1}}),\dots,g_{\beta_{j}}^{b_{j}-b_{j-1}}(x_{b_{j-1}+1},\dots,x_{b_{j}}))

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},\#,p,q,\alpha,\beta
are as in (
2.20
) and

i+j+1,

(a_{0},\dots,a_{i},b_{0},\dots,b_{j}),

(\alpha_{0},\dots\alpha_{i},\beta_{0},\dots,\beta_{j})
any decomposition of
(k,\gamma).

The operators

{\mathfrak{n}}_{\alpha_{0}}^{i+j+1},

f_{\alpha_{1}}^{a_{1}},

\dots,

f_{\alpha_{i}}^{a_{i}-a_{i-1}},

H_{\beta_{0}}^{b_{0}-a_{i}},

g_{\beta_{1}}^{b_{1}-b_{0}},

\dots,

g_{\beta_{j}}^{b_{j}-b_{j-1}}
are well-defined because
[i+j+1,\alpha_{0}],

[a_{1},\alpha_{1}],\dots,[a_{i},\alpha_{i}],

[b_{0}-a_{i},\beta_{0}],

[b_{1}-b_{0},\beta_{1}],\dots,[b_{j}-b_{j-1},\beta_{j}]
are all
\leqslant\penalty N
by (
2.13
).

When
N=\infty
the gapped
A_{\infty}
homotopy
H:f\Rightarrow g
defines a filtered
A_{\infty}
homotopy
(H^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}})^{k\in{\mathbb{N}}}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
between the corresponding filtered
A_{\infty}
functors,
with
H^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}:=\sum T^{\gamma}H^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
where
\gamma\in\Gamma\setminus\{0\}
for
k=0
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
for
k>0.
The sum is well-defined because
\hom^{-1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{fX_{0}fX_{k}}[1])
is complete.

Remark 2.21
.

We can make an equivalent definition by using
models
of
{\mathbin{\cal B}}\times[0,1]
as Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono do
[
21
, §4.2]
. This is the result of obstruction theory arguments
[
21
, §4.4]
which extend readily to our context; the major difference is that mentioned in Remark
2.15
.

We define the notion of being homotopic for gapped
A_{N}
functors,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
be gapped
A_{N}
categories and
f,g:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
gapped
A_{N}
functors which induce the same function between the object sets.
We say that
f,g
are
homotopic
if there is a homotopy from
f
to
g.

The following is a straightforward extension of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono’s result for gapped
A_{N}
algebras.

Lemma 2.23

(cf.​
[
21
, Proposition 4.2.37]
)
.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
be gapped
A_{N}
categories and suppose given a function
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}.

Then being homotopic is an equivalence relation for those gapped
A_{N}
functors from
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
to
({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
inducing the given function
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}.
∎

We define the homotopy equivalences of gapped
A_{N}
categories,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}\sqcup\{\infty\}.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
be gapped
A_{N}
categories of the same object set and
f:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
a gapped
A_{N}
functor which induces the identity on the object set.
We call
f
a
homotopy equivalence
if there exists a gapped
A_{N}
functor
g:({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})\to({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
which induces the identity on the object set and such that
g\circ f,f\circ g
are homotopic to the identity functors of
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
respectively.
We say that
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
are
homotopy equivalent
if there exists a homotopy equivalence
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}}).

The following is also a straightforward extension of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono’s result for gapped
A_{N}
algebras.

Lemma 2.25

(cf.​
[
21
, Corollary 4.2.44 and Remark 7.2.71]
)
.

Being homotopy equivalent is an equivalence relation for gapped
A_{N}
categories of the same object set.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}}),({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
be gapped
A_{N}
categories of the same object set and
f:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
a gapped
A_{N}
functor which induces the identity on the object set.
Then
f
is a homotopy equivalence if and only if for any
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
the co-chain map
f^{10}_{XY}:({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{10}_{XY})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}}_{fXfY},{\mathfrak{n}}^{10}_{XY})
induces an isomorphism of the cohomology groups. ∎

Here is the obstruction theory result for gapped
A_{N}
categories which extends that of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Theorem 7.2.72]
.

Theorem 2.26
.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
be a gapped
A_{N+1}
category,
N\in{\mathbb{N}},
and
({\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{n}})
a full subcategory of the
A_{N}
truncation of
({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}}).

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{m}})
be a gapped
A_{N}
category with
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}=\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\prime},
and
g:({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{n}})
a homotopy equivalence which induces the identity on the object set.
Then there exist a gapped
A_{N+1}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
with
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}=\obj{\mathbin{\cal B}},
which contains
({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{m}})
as a full subcategory in its
A_{N}
truncation; and a gapped
A_{N+1}
functor
f:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
which induces the identity on the object set, whose
A_{N}
truncation agrees with
g
on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}.

Proof.

Recall from definition that the
A_{0}
truncation of
({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{m}})
is a family of co-chain complexes.
We assign to
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
a co-chain complex
({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{10}_{XY})
and a co-chain map
f^{10}_{XY}:({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{10}_{XY})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{n}}^{10}_{XY}).

For
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
set
({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{10}_{XY}):=({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{m}}^{10}_{XY})
and
f^{10}_{XY}:=g^{10}_{XY}.

For
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
X\notin\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
or
Y\notin\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
set
({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{10}_{XY}):=({\mathbin{\cal B}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{n}}^{10}_{XY})
and define
f^{10}_{XY}:{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}\to{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{XY}
to be the identity map.
In both cases the co-chain map
f^{10}_{XY}:({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{10}_{XY})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{n}}^{10}_{XY})
induces an isomorphism of the cohomology groups.

We extend these co-chain complexes to a gapped
A_{N}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
and these co-chain maps to a gapped
A_{N}
functor
f^{N}:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})^{N}
where
({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})^{N}
is the
A_{M}
truncation of
({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}}).

We do this by an induction on
M\in\{0,\dots,N-1\}.

Suppose therefore that we have defined
{\mathfrak{m}}^{l\delta}_{X_{0}\dots X_{l}}
for
(l,\delta)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with
[l,\delta]\leqslant\penalty M
and for
X_{0}\dots X_{l}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}.

We extend these to a gapped
A_{M+1}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
and a gapped
A_{M+1}
functor
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})^{M+1}.

Denote by
(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)\setminus\{(0,0)\}
the element with
[k,\gamma]=M+1.

We assign to
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
some filtered homomorphisms

{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}},f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
which satisfy the
A_{M+1}
equations.
For
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
we use the given
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}},f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}};

and otherwise we introduce some new
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}},f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}.

Define a degree-one
\Lambda
-linear map
{\partial}
from
\hom^{*}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1])
to itself by

{\partial}\phi:={\mathfrak{m}}^{1}_{0}\circ\phi-(-1)^{\deg\phi}\phi\circ(\text{the co-derivation of }{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}_{0}).

(2.23)

This is well-defined because
{\mathbf{v}}\phi\geqslant 0
implies
{\mathbf{v}}({\partial}\phi)\geqslant 0.
And
{\partial}\circ{\partial}=0.

Define an
obstruction co-cycle

({\rm o}{\mathfrak{m}})^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}\in\hom^{2}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1])
by

(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\mapsto\sum(-1)^{\#}{\mathfrak{m}}^{k+p-q+1}_{\alpha}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},{\mathfrak{m}}^{q-p}_{\beta}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})

where
[k+p-q+1,\alpha]\leqslant\penalty M
or
[q-p,\beta]\leqslant\penalty M.
Doing the same computation as Fukaya and others
[
21
, (4.5.2.1)]
we find
{\partial}({\rm o}{\mathfrak{m}})^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}=0;
that is,
({\rm o}{\mathfrak{m}})^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
is certainly a co-cycle.
Their computation
[
21
, (4.5.2.4)]
shows also that the
A_{M}
homotopy equivalence
f:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
induces an isomorphism from the
{\partial}
-cohomology group

H^{2}(\hom({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1]))
to the
{\partial}
-cohomology group
H^{2}(\hom({\mathbin{\cal B}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1])),

mapping the cohomology class of
({\rm o}{\mathfrak{m}})^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
to that of
({\rm o}{\mathfrak{n}})^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}.

But the latter vanishes for the obvious reason:
({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})
is already an
A_{M+1}
category.
Thus
({\rm o}{\mathfrak{m}})^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
is a co-boundary, written as
{\partial}\mu^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
for some

\mu^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}\in\hom^{1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1]).

(2.24)

Define then an obstruction co-cycle in
\hom^{1}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}[1],\dots,{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}[1];{\mathbin{\cal B}}_{X_{0}X_{k}}[1])
by sending
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
to

\begin{split}\left(\sum{\mathfrak{m}}^{l}_{\alpha_{0}}(f^{a_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{a_{1}}),\dots,f^{a_{l}-a_{l-1}}_{\alpha_{l}}(x_{a_{l-1}},\dots,x_{a_{l}}))\right)+f^{1}_{0}\circ\mu^{k}_{\gamma}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\\
+\sum(-1)^{\#}f^{k+p-q+1}_{\alpha}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},{\mathfrak{m}}^{q-p}_{\beta}(x_{p+1},\dots,x_{q}),x_{q+1},\dots,x_{k})\end{split}

where
[k+p-q+1,\alpha]\leqslant\penalty M
or
[q-p,\beta]\leqslant\penalty M;
and
l,

a_{1},\dots,a_{l},

\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{l}
are a decomposition of
(k,\gamma)
with
(l,\alpha_{0})>(1,0)
so that
[a_{1},\alpha_{1}],\dots[a_{l}-a_{l-1},\alpha_{l}]\leqslant\penalty M.

As
f^{1}_{0}
is a co-chain equivalence we can modify
\mu^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
if we need, so that the corresponding obstruction co-cycle will vanish.
So there is a gapped
A_{M+1}
functor
f:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})^{M+1}
as we want, completing the induction.

We have thus obtained a gapped
A_{N}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
and a gapped
A_{N}
functor
f:({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})\to({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathfrak{n}})^{N};
that is,
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}},

f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}
are given for
every

(k,\gamma)\in({\mathbb{N}}\times\Gamma)^{\prime}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
with
[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty N.
Extend these to
(k,\gamma)
with
[k,\gamma]=N+1,
by repeating the obstruction theory arguments above; then the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 2.27
.

The first step above has no counterpart in their original proof
[
21
, Theorem 7.2.72]
.
The last step is the closest to it; the difference is only that mentioned in Remark
2.15
.
The induction steps are similar but we must be more careful: we cannot change the given
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}},

f^{k\gamma}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}.

2.3
Fukaya Categories

We begin with the definition of ambient spaces in which we do Floer theory.
The example we have in mind is cotangent bundles, but we make a more general definition:

Definition 2.28
.

A compact symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
with contact boundary is a symplectic manifold which is given a
1
-form
\lambda
near
{\partial}X\subset X
such that
d\lambda=\omega
and the vector field
v_{\lambda}
given by
v_{\lambda}\lrcorner\,\omega=\lambda
is outwards pointing on
{\partial}X
. We call
\lambda
a
Liouville
1
-form
of
(X,\omega).

In these circumstances there exists near
{\partial}X\subseteq X
a unique smooth function
r_{\lambda}
with values in
(0,1]
such that

r_{\lambda}|_{{\partial}X}=1\text{ and }dr_{\lambda}(v_{\lambda})=r_{\lambda}.

(2.25)

We call
r_{\lambda}
the
radius
function near
{\partial}X.

We say that an almost complex structures
J
is compatible with the contact boundary if it is compatible with
\omega
in the usual sense,
\lambda=Jdr_{\lambda}
near
{\partial}X
and
J
is invariant near
{\partial}X
under the flow of
v_{\lambda}.

If there exists a
global

1
-form
\lambda
with
d\lambda=\omega
and satisfying the conditions above then we call
(X,\lambda)
a
Liouville domain
.

We consider the following class of Lagrangians:

Definition 2.29
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold with contact boundary, and
\lambda
a Liouville
1
-form of it. Let
\hat{L}
be a compact manifold possibly with boundary and
\iota:\hat{L}\to X
a Lagrangian immersion.
By an abuse of notation we shall usually mention only the image
L:=\iota(\hat{L})\subseteq X
in place of the immersion map,
and say that
L
is compact if so is
\hat{L}.

We say that
L
is
generic
if its every self-intersection point is a transverse double point outside
{\partial}X.

We say that
L
has
Legendrian
collar if
\iota({\partial}\hat{L})\subseteq{\partial}X,

\iota^{*}\lambda=0
near
{\partial}\hat{L}\subseteq\hat{L}

and
\iota^{*}r_{\lambda}
has regular value
1
(this is automatically true if
\hat{L}
is closed).

Suppose now that
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
are two Lagrangians in
X
given by immersions
\hat{L}_{0}\to X
and
\hat{L}_{1}\to X.

We say that
L_{0},L_{1}
are
generic
if the immersion
\hat{L}_{0}\sqcup\hat{L}_{1}
is generic.

We recall the notion of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded Lagrangians in
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded symplectic manifolds.
There are several ways of doing this
[
51
]
.
We choose that which uses almost complex structures on the ambient symplectic manifold.

Definition 2.30
.

A
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading
of
(X,\omega)
is the choice up to homotopy of a nowhere-vanishing section
\Omega^{2}
of the squared canonical bundle of
X
relative to some
\omega
-compatible almost complex structure
J.

Remark 2.31
.

A
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading of
(X,\omega)
exist if and only if
2c_{1}(X)=0\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})

where
c_{1}(X)
denotes the first Chern class of
X.

Given a quadratic volume form
\Omega^{2}
representing a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading on
X
, we associate to each Lagrangian immersion
\iota:\hat{L}\to X
a unique smooth function
\phi:\hat{L}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
with
\iota^{*}\Omega^{\otimes 2}=e^{i\phi}{\rm vol}^{2}.
Here
{\rm vol}^{2}
is the squared volume form of
\hat{L}
with respect to the metric induced from
\omega,J.
To define
{\rm vol}^{2}
we do not need
\hat{L}
to be orientable.

Definition 2.32
.

We say that a Lagrangian immersion
\iota:\hat{L}\to X
is
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
if this is equipped with a lift of
\phi
to the universal cover
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\cong S^{1}.

We say that
\iota
is
special of phase
\phi
with respect to
\Omega^{\otimes 2}
if
\phi
is locally constant.
When
\hat{L}
is connected,
\phi
must be constant and we shall say
\iota
has phase
\phi\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.
We shall also use this constant function for the grading of
\iota.

The shift operator
[1]
maps the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded Lagrangian
(\iota,\phi)
to
(\iota,\phi-2\pi).

Remark 2.33
.

The Lagrangian immersion admits a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading if and only if the group homomorphism
\phi_{*}:H_{1}(L,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})\to H_{1}(S^{1},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
vanishes.

The definition above of special Lagrangians is more general than that in the literature
[
34
]
. These agree when
J
is integrable and
\Omega^{2}
is the square of some holomorphic volume form.

Definition 2.34
.

Let
\iota:\hat{L}\to L\subset X
be a generic Lagrangian immersion.
We consider the fibre product

L\times_{X}L:=\{(x,y)\in\hat{L}\times\hat{L}:\iota(x)=\iota(y)\}

(2.26)

which consists of the diagonal

\{(x,x)\in\hat{L}\times\hat{L}\},
identified with
\hat{L},
and the self-intersection pairs in
\hat{L}.

Suppose that
L
is
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded by a phase function
\phi:\hat{L}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
and we define then a
Maslov index
function

\mu_{L}:L\times_{X}L\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Set
\mu_{L}\equiv 0
on the diagonal
\hat{L}.

We associate to each pair
(x_{1},x_{2})\in L\times_{X}L
an integer called the
Maslov index
. Put
x:=\iota(x_{1})=\iota(x_{2})\in X
and take a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear isomorphism
(T_{x}X,J|_{x})\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}
which maps
\omega
to

\frac{i}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}dz_{j}\wedge d\bar{z}_{j},

T_{x_{2}}L
to
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\subseteq{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}

and
T_{x_{1}}L
to

\{(e^{i\theta_{1}}t_{1},\cdots,e^{i\theta_{n}}t_{n})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}:t_{1},\cdots,t_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\}

for some
\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n}\in(0,\pi).

These
\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n}
are unique up to order so we can define

\mu_{L}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\frac{1}{2\pi}[2\theta_{1}+\cdots+2\theta_{n}+\phi(x_{2})-\phi(x_{1})].

(2.27)

The definition of phase functions implies readily that
\mu_{L}(x_{1},x_{2})
is in fact an integer.

Suppose now that
L_{1},L_{2}\subseteq X
are two generic Lagrangians,
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded by
\phi_{1}:L_{1}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
and
\phi_{2}:L_{2}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
respectively.
The fibre product
L_{1}\times_{X}L_{2}
is then a finite set and we define the Maslov index function

\mu_{L_{1}L_{2}}:L_{1}\times_{X}L_{2}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
by sending
(x_{1},x_{2})
to the right-hand side of (
2.27
) above with
\phi_{1}(x_{2}),\phi_{2}(x_{2})
in place of
\phi(x_{1}),\phi(x_{2})
respectively.

Remark 2.35
.

Since
\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{n}\in(0,\pi)
it follows by (
2.27
) that

\mu_{L}(x_{1},x_{2})-n<\frac{\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}}{2\pi}<\mu_{L}(x_{1},x_{2}).

(2.28)

In particular, if
\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}
is nearly zero then
\mu_{L}(x_{1},x_{2})\in[0,n]

and if
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}
then
\mu_{L}(x_{1},x_{2})\in[1,n-1].

Example 2.36
.

Take
X={\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n},

\Omega=dz_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dz_{n}
and
L_{2}={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\subset{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}.

Define a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading
\phi_{2}:L_{2}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
by
\phi_{2}\equiv 0.

Take
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m}\in(\frac{\pi}{2},\pi)
and
\theta_{m+1},\dots,\theta_{n}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2}).

For
s\in[0,1]
define

L_{s}:=\{(e^{is(\theta_{1}-\pi)}t_{1},\dots,e^{is(\theta_{m}-\pi)}t_{m},e^{is\theta_{m+1}}t_{m+1},\dots,e^{is\theta_{n}}t_{n}):t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\}

so
L_{0}=L_{2}.

Define a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading
\phi_{s}:L_{s}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
by starting with
\phi_{2}\equiv 0
on
L_{2}
and lifting the path
[0,s].
Then

\phi_{s}:=2s[(\theta_{1}-\pi)+\dots+(\theta_{m}-\pi)+\theta_{m+1}+\dots+\theta_{n}]
and in particular
