\phi_{1}=2(\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n})-2m.
The Maslov index of
(L_{1},L_{2})
at the intersection point
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}
is now equal to

\frac{1}{2\pi}[2\theta_{1}+\cdots+2\theta_{n}+\phi_{2}-\phi_{1}]=m.

This agrees with the Morse index of the quadratic function
(\tan\theta_{1})t_{1}^{2}+\dots+(\tan\theta_{n})t_{n}^{2}
on
L_{2}.
Our convention is thus the same as Seidel’s
[
51
, §2d, (v)]
.

We extend the definition of relative spin structures to countably many Lagrangians. These will equip the pseudo-holomorphic curve moduli spaces with natural orientations.

Definition 2.37
.

Let
X
be a symplectic manifold,
\hat{L}
a compact manifold and
\iota:\hat{L}\to X
a generic Lagrangian immersion; we allow
\hat{L},X
to have boundary. By a
relative spin structure
of
\iota:\hat{L}\to X
we mean the data
(\tau,V,o,\sigma,s)
where
\tau
is a triangulation of
X,

V
an oriented vector bundle over the
3
-skeleton
[X,\tau]^{3}
of
(X,\tau),

o
an orientation of
\hat{L},

\sigma
a triangulation of
\hat{L}
such that
\iota
induces a simplicial map
(\hat{L},\sigma)\to(X,\tau),
and
s
a spin structure of the bundle
\iota^{*}V\oplus T\hat{L}
restricted to the
2
-skeleton of
(\hat{L},\sigma).

Suppose now that
(L_{N})_{N=0}^{\infty}
is a sequence of compact manifolds which may have boundary, and
\iota:\bigsqcup_{N=0}^{\infty}L_{N}\to X
a generic Lagrangian immersion. By a
relative spin structure
of
\iota:\bigsqcup_{N=0}^{\infty}L_{N}\to X
we mean a sequence
(\tau_{N},V_{N},o_{N},\sigma_{N},s_{N})_{N=0}^{\infty}
such that each
(\tau_{N},V_{N},o_{N},\sigma_{N},s_{N})
is a relative spin structure of
\iota|_{L_{N}}:L_{N}\to X,
each
(X,\tau_{N})
is a subcomplex of
(X,\tau_{N+1}),
and the restriction of each
V_{N+1}
to
[X,\tau_{N}]^{3}
agrees with
V_{N}.

Remark 2.38
.

Suppose that
V_{0}=V_{1}=V_{2}=\dots=:V,
that
V
is defined over the whole
X
and that each
s_{N}
is a spin structure of
\iota^{*}V\oplus TL_{N}
over the whole
L_{N}.

Then each Stiefel–Whitney class
w_{2}(TL_{N})\in H^{2}(L_{N},{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
is the pull-back of
w_{2}(V)\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}).

In this case we refer to
w_{2}(V)\in H^{2}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
as the
background class
of the relative spin structure.

We can in practice take
X
to be embedded in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}Q
over a closed oriented manifold
Q
and take
V=\pi^{*}TQ,
where
\pi:T^{*}Q\to Q
is the projection. So the background class is
\pi^{*}w_{2}(TQ)
and the zero-section
Q
will be given a spin structure of
V|_{Q}\oplus TQ.

We introduce now the notion of branes:

Definition 2.39
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold with contact boundary, and
\lambda
a Livouville
1
-form of it.
By a
brane
on
X
we mean a pair
(L,E)
where

•

L\subset X
is a compact
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded generically-immersed Lagrangian with Legendrian collar; and

•

E
is a filtered local system over the domain
\hat{L}
of the immersion
\hat{L}\to L\subset X.

We write only
E
in place of
(L,E)
when we want to save notation.

We choose countably many branes and a Novikov field.

Hypothesis 2.40
.

Let
(X,\omega)
be a compact symplectic manifold with contact boundary, and
\lambda
a Louville
1
-form of it.
Let
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
be a countable set of branes such that any two distinct Lagrangian immersions in
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
are generic in the sense of Definition
2.29
; we allow some of the Lagrangian immersions to be the same.
Fix a Novikov field
(\Lambda,{\mathbf{v}}).
When
\Lambda
has characteristic
\neq 0
we suppose that
(X,\omega)
has no non-constant pseudo-holomorphic sphere (cf.
[
23
]
).
When
\Lambda
has characteristic
\neq 2
we suppose that
X
and the underlying Lagrangians have a relative spin structure with respect to some order of the Lagrangians.

We introduce also the homologically perturbed Floer complexes:

Definition 2.41
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
be as in Hypothesis
2.40
and
(L_{0},E_{0}),(L_{1},E_{1})\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
two elements.
Denote by
\pi_{-}:L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1}\to L_{0}
and

\pi_{+}:L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1}\to L_{1}
the projections.
The
homologically perturbed Floer complex
associated to the pair
(L_{0},E_{0}),(L_{1},E_{1})
is the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded finite-dimensional valued
\Lambda
-vector space

CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{1}):=H^{*}(L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1},\hom(\pi_{-}^{*}E_{0},\pi_{+}^{*}E_{1}))[-\mu_{L_{0}L_{1}}]

(2.29)

where
[-\mu_{L_{0}L_{1}}]
denotes the degree shift by Maslov indices.
Since the fibres of
E_{0},E_{1}
are valued vector spaces it follows that so are
\hom(\pi_{-}^{*}E_{0},\pi_{+}^{*}E_{1})
and
CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{1}).

Remark 2.42
.

The definition (
2.29
) is actually the result of applying the filtered homological perturbation lemma
[
21
, Theorem A]

to the singular or de Rham model in the literature.
More precisely, in any construction of Lagrangian Floer cohomology groups we have to choose a chain model of the
Floer complex
CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{1})
with
L_{0}=L_{1}.

Akaho–Joyce
[
9
]
and Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
]
both take the singular chain model.
Fukaya
[
19
]
takes the de Rham model assuming that the ground field
{\mathbf{k}}
contains
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

We come now to the construction of curved Fukaya categories.

Theorem 2.43
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
be as in Hypothesis
2.40
and put
n:={\frac{1}{2}}\dim X.

Then
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
has the structure of a gapped
A_{\infty}
category whose morphism spaces are given by (
2.29
) and which has the following properties:

•

the
A_{\infty}
structure is curved in such a way that if
(L,E)\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X),
if
L
has no self-intersection point of index
2
or
n-2,

and if
L
does not bound any non-constant pseudo-holomorphic discs, then
(L,E)
has curvature
{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}=0;

•

for a pair
E_{0},E_{1}\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
, the differential

{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}:CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{1})\to CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{1})
has leading term
{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}_{0}=0;
and

•

for a triple
E_{0},E_{1},E_{2}\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
which are supported on the same Lagrangian, the leading term
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}_{0}
of the product map

{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}:CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{1})\times CF^{*}(E_{1},E_{2})\to CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{2})

(2.30)

vanishes with the following exceptions: on the diagonal component
\hat{L}
of
L\times_{X}L
this agrees with the usual cup product map

H^{*}(\hat{L},\hom(E_{0},E_{1}))\otimes H^{*}(\hat{L},\hom(E_{1},E_{2}))\to H^{*}(\hat{L},\hom(E_{0},E_{2}));

and on each self-intersection pair
(a,b)\in L\times_{X}L
it is given by the map

\hom(E_{0}|_{a},E_{1}|_{b})[-\mu_{ab}]\otimes\hom(E_{1}|_{b},E_{2}|_{a})[-\mu_{ba}]\to H^{n}(\hat{L},\hom(E_{0},E_{2})).

2.4
Proof of Theorem
2.43

We begin by taking the singular chain model of (
2.29
) as in Remark
2.42
:

Definition 2.44
.

Let
L
be a manifold, and
E
a filtered local system over
L.

The smooth singular chain complex
C_{*}(L,E)
is the
\Lambda^{0}
module generated by pairs
(\sigma,\epsilon)
where

\sigma
is a smooth singular
i
-simplex in
L,
and
\epsilon
a (flat) section of
\sigma^{*}E
of nonnegative valuation, modulo the relation

a(\sigma,\epsilon)=(\sigma,a\epsilon)\text{ for }a\in\Lambda^{0}.

(2.31)

The differential
d
on
C_{*}(L,E)
is defined by

d(\sigma,\epsilon)=\sum_{j=0}^{i}(-1)^{j}(\sigma\circ\delta^{j},\epsilon\circ\delta^{j})

(2.32)

where
\delta^{j}
is the
j^{\rm th}
face inclusion map.

If
L
has boundary, we define
C_{*}(L,{\partial}L;E):=C_{*}(L,E)/C_{*}({\partial}L,E).

We regard this, by Poincaré duality, as a
cochain
complex.

In our circumstances, to
(L_{i},E_{i})\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X),

i=0,1,

we assign

C_{*}(L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1},{\partial}(L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1});\hom(\pi_{-}^{*}E_{0},\pi_{+}^{*}E_{1}))

(2.33)

where
\pi_{+},\pi_{-}
are as in Definition
2.41
.

Let
J
be a compatible almost complex structure on
X
in the sense of Definition
2.28
.
We introduce now the moduli spaces of
J
-holomorphic discs.
Let
L_{0},\cdots,L_{k}\subseteq X
be
k+1
underlying Lagrangians in
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X).

Definition 2.45
.

We denote by

{\mathbin{\cal M}}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})
the compact moduli space of finite-area stable
J
-holomorphic discs in
X

with
k+1
boundary points, whose corresponding segments are ordered counterclockwise and lifted to
L_{0},\cdots,L_{k}.

More precisely, for each genus-zero prestable bordered Riemann surface
\Sigma
we choose a continuous map
S^{1}\to{\partial}\Sigma
as Akaho–Joyce do
[
9
, Definition 4.1]
; this is essentially unique.
Denote by
\zeta_{0},\dots,\zeta_{k+1}\in S^{1}
the points corresponding to the marked points on
{\partial}\Sigma;
and by
\hat{L}_{0},\dots,\hat{L}_{k}
the domains of the immersed Lagrangians.
For each holomorphic curve
u:\Sigma\to X
we choose a continuous map
S^{1}\setminus\{\zeta_{0},\dots,\zeta_{k+1}\}\to\hat{L}_{0}\sqcup\dots\sqcup\hat{L}_{k}
as Akaho–Joyce
[
9
, Definition 4.2]
. We require that the
k+1
connected components of
S^{1}\setminus\{\zeta_{0},\dots,\zeta_{k+1}\}
should map to
\hat{L}_{0},\dots,\hat{L}_{k}
respectively.

For
\gamma\geqslant 0
we denote by
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})
the subset made from
J
-holomorphic curves of area
\gamma.

We recall the maximum principle on Riemann surfaces with Neumann boundary conditions, under the assumption that the almost complex structure of compatible with the contact boundary:

Lemma 2.46

(Abouzaid–Seidel
[
8
, Lemma 7.2]
)
.

There exists a compact set of
X\setminus{\partial}X
which contains the image of every non-constant
J
-holomorphic curve included in
{\mathbin{\cal M}}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0}).

∎

Hence by the Gromov compactness theorem we get

Corollary 2.47
.

Every
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})
is compact. ∎

Suppose now that
L_{0},\cdots,L_{k}
are given local systems
E_{0},\cdots,E_{k}
respectively.
Note that for every
i=1,\cdots,k
there is an evaluation map

{\rm ev}_{i}:{\mathbin{\cal M}}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})\to L_{i-1}\times_{X}L_{i}

(2.34)

where
L_{-1}:=L_{k}.

So
{\mathbin{\cal M}}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})
has local systems of the form
{\rm ev}_{i}\pi_{\pm}^{*}E_{j}
for
i,j=0,\dots,k.

Using the parallel transport map
{\rm ev}_{i}^{*}E_{i}\cong{\rm ev}_{i+1}^{*}E_{i}
we get a natural map of local systems

\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\hom({\rm ev}_{i}^{*}E_{i-1},{\rm ev}_{i}^{*}E_{i})\to\hom({\rm ev}_{0}^{*}E_{0},{\rm ev}_{0}^{*}E_{k}).

(2.35)

Let
\Gamma\subseteq[0,\infty)
be a discrete sub-monoid which contains the areas of every pseudoholomorphic disc with boundary on the underlying Lagrangians of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X).

The gapped
A_{N}
categories in what follows will be gapped with respect to
\Gamma.

We fix an order on the countable set
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
and use the same order on the underlying Lagrangians of it.
Here are the next three steps:

(I)

For each
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
construct a gapped
A_{N}
category
{\mathbin{\cal C}}_{N}
whose objects are the first
N
Lagrangians and whose morphism space between
(L_{0},E_{0}),(L_{1},E_{1})
is (
2.33
). We denote this morphism space by
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(E_{0},E_{1}).

As Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
23
, Remark 13.5]
point out we can work with the whole complex (
2.33
), not with countably generated sub-complexes of it as in the earlier works
[
9
,
21
]
.

(II)

Construct a homotopy equivalence from
{\mathbin{\cal C}}_{N}
to the
A_{N}
truncation of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}_{N+1}.

(III)

Lift the gapped
A_{N}
structure of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}_{N}
to the gapped
A_{N+1}
structure.

We do (I) as follows.
By Corollary
2.47
every moduli space of the form
{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})
is a finite union of Kuranishi spaces, which are oriented naturally by the relative spin structure.
Suppose that for each
i=1,\dots,k
we are given
x_{i}\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(E_{i-1},E_{i}).

We choose a multivalued perturbation of the fibre product

{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})\times_{\rm ev}(x_{1}\times\cdots\times x_{k}),

(2.36)

where
{\rm ev}:=({\rm ev}_{1},\cdots,{\rm ev}_{k}),
whenever
[k,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty N.

Combining this perturbation with (
2.35
) we get an element of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(E_{0},E_{k})
which we call the virtual chain for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}).
It has coefficients in the ground field
{\mathbf{k}}\subset\Lambda
by the result of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
23
]
.
Varying
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}
we get a map

{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma}:\prod_{i=1}^{k}{\mathbin{\cal C}}(E_{i-1},E_{i})\to{\mathbin{\cal C}}(E_{0},E_{k}).

(2.37)

One can prove that there is a consistent choice of the virtual chains above such that
({\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma})
defines the gapped
A_{N}
structure.

Step (II) is done by a standard cobordism (or bifurcation) argument.
Step (III) is a consequence of Theorem
2.26
.
Once these have been done, we get a gapped
A_{\infty}
category whose
A_{N}
truncation is homotopy equivalent to
{\mathbin{\cal C}}_{N}.
Applying the homological perturbation lemma to
{\mathbin{\cal C}}_{N}
we get a gapped
A_{\infty}
category that we want.
∎

2.5
Bounding Cochains

We define the units and bounding cochains of filtered
A_{\infty}
categories.

Definition 2.48
.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
be a filtered
A_{\infty}
category. By
units
of this we mean a family
(e_{X}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{0}_{XX})_{X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
of degree-zero morphisms such that
{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}_{X}e_{X}=0
for
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}};

x={\mathfrak{m}}^{2}_{XXY}(e_{X},x)
for
x\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}
with
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}};

x=(-1)^{\deg x}{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}_{YXX}(x,e_{X})
for
x\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{YX}
with
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}};
and
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{X_{0}\dots X_{k}}(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})=0
for
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
k\geqslant 2
and for
x_{1}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}},\dots,x_{k}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{k-1}X_{k}}
containing at least one of
e_{X_{1}},\dots,e_{X_{k}}.

Let
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
be a filtered
A_{\infty}
category. We call this a
strict

A_{\infty}
category if
{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}_{X}=0\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{1}_{XX}
for every
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}.

If it is strict then define the cohomology category
H{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to be the associative category consisting of the same objects; the morphism space
(H{\mathbin{\cal A}})_{XY}:=H^{*}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}_{XY})
for
X,Y\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}};
and the product map induced from the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
operators. Define also the category
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to be the subcategory of
H{\mathbin{\cal A}}
consisting of the same objects and whose every morphism space
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY}
is the degree-zero part
H^{0}({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{XY},{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}_{XY}).

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}}
be filtered strict
A_{\infty}
categories and
f:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal B}}
a filtered
A_{\infty}
functor. We call
f
a
strict

A_{\infty}
functor if
f^{0}_{X}=0\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{0}_{XX}.
This induces then a functor between the cohomology categories, in the obvious way.

Let
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be any and
b\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{1}_{XX}
a degree-one element with
{\mathbf{v}}b>0.
We call
b
a
bounding cochain
if
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{X\dots X}(b^{\times k})=0.
The sum converges because
{\mathbf{v}}b>0.

We define then a filtered strict
A_{\infty}
category
({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{n}}).
Define
\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
to be the set of pairs
(X,b)
with
X\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and
b\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{1}_{XX}
a bounding cochain. For
Y_{0}=(X_{0},b_{0}),

Y_{1}=(X_{1},b_{1})\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
set
{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}_{Y_{0}Y_{1}}:={\mathbin{\cal A}}_{X_{0}X_{1}}.

For
Y_{0}=(X_{0},b_{0}),\dots,Y_{k}=(X_{k},b_{k})\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
define
{\mathfrak{n}}^{k}_{Y_{0}\dots Y_{k}}:{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}_{Y_{0}Y_{1}}[1]\times\dots\times{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}_{Y_{k-1}Y_{k}}[1]\to{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}_{Y_{0}Y_{k}}[1]
by

(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\mapsto\textstyle\sum_{p_{0},\dots,p_{k}=0}^{\infty}{\mathfrak{m}}^{k+p_{0}+\dots+p_{k}}(b_{0}^{\times p_{0}},x_{1},b_{1}^{\times p_{1}},\dots,b_{k-1}^{\times p_{k-1}},x_{k},b_{k}^{\times p_{k}}).

The sum converges because
{\mathbf{v}}b_{0}>0,\dots,{\mathbf{v}}b_{k}>0.

Since
{\mathbf{v}}{\mathfrak{m}}^{k+p_{0}+\dots+p_{k}}\geqslant 0
it follows that
{\mathbf{v}}{\mathfrak{n}}^{k}_{Y_{0}\dots Y_{k}}\geqslant 0;
that is,
{\mathfrak{n}}^{k}_{Y_{0}\dots Y_{k}}
is a
filtered
homomorphism.
The
A_{\infty}
equations of
({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathfrak{m}})
imply those of
({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{n}}).

Since
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}(b^{\times k})=0
it follows that
{\mathfrak{n}}^{0}=0.
Thus
({\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime},{\mathfrak{n}})
is a filtered strict
A_{\infty}
category.

If
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is a filtered
A_{\infty}
category with units then the strict
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}^{\prime}
corresponding to it has units too. This is a straightforward extension of the
A_{\infty}
algebra case
[
21
, Lemma 5.2.17]
.

We introduce now the strict
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
where ‘nc’ stands for not necessarily closed Lagrangians.

Definition 2.49
.

We define the strict
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
to be that made from
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
by using bounding cochains as above.
We denote an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
by a triple
{\mathbf{b}}=(L,E,b)
where
(L,E)
is an object of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
and
b
a bounding cochain in
CF^{*}(E,E).

We say that
{\mathbf{b}}
is
supported
on
L
or that
L

underlies

{\mathbf{b}}.

We define the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
\Lambda
-linear associative category
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
to be the cohomology category of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X).

We denote by
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{0},{\mathbf{b}}_{1})
its morphism space between two objects
{\mathbf{b}}_{0},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}.

The following is a straightforward extension of the result of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, §7.3]
for embedded Lagrangians.

Theorem 2.50
.

The filtered
A_{\infty}
categories
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X),{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
have units. ∎

Remark 2.51
.

Strict unitality fails at the level of singular chains, because the perturbations of Kuranishi spaces of the form (
2.36
) need not be compatible with forgetting marked points. One can however prove that for
(L,E)\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
there exists a
homotopy
unit of
CF^{*}(E,E)
whose leading term is equal to the fundamental class of
L.
This may be done by taking fundamental chains of the underlying Lagrangians in
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)

and constructing in a consistent way the homotopies between those perturbed Kuranishi spaces for which some of
x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}
in (
2.36
) are the fundamental chains, and those obtained from the forgetful maps. The homotopy units become the ordinary units after passing to the minimal models.

If
{\mathbf{k}}
contains
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
we can make the units directly as Fukaya
[
19
]
does for embedded Lagrangians, using the de Rham model for (
2.29
).

We recall the notion of zero objects in
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
and give a corollary to Theorem
2.50
.

Definition 2.52
.

We call
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
a
zero
object if the unit of
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
is zero.

Corollary 2.53
.

If
{\mathbf{b}}=(L,E,b)\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
is a zero object, the Lagrangian
L\subset X
has at least one self-intersection point of index
-1.

Proof.

Since
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
is
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded it follows that the unit in
HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
may be cancelled out only by elements of
CF^{-1}(E,E),

which come from self-intersection points of
L
of index
-1.

∎

Corollary
2.53
gives another proof of the following fact.

Corollary 2.54

(Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Theorem E]
)
.

Every
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
supported on an embedded Lagrangian is non-zero. ∎

Take
\Omega^{2},J
as in Definition
2.30
so that we can speak of special Lagrangians in
X.

Consider
nearly
special Lagrangians which have phase sufficiently close (in the
C^{0}
sense) to a constant function. The estimate (
2.28
) implies then

Proposition 2.55
.

If
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
is supported on a generically immersed nearly special Lagrangian then the Floer cochain group
CF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
supported in degrees
0,\cdots,n;
and accordingly, so is the cohomology group
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}).
∎

This and Corollary
2.53
imply

Corollary 2.56
.

Every
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
supported on a nearly special Lagrangian is non-zero. ∎

We define the Fukaya category which we use most often.

Definition 2.57
.

We denote by
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
the full subcategory of objects supported on
closed
Lagrangians; that is, the underlying Lagrangian of an object
{\mathbf{b}}
of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
is a Lagrangian immersion
L\to X
from a compact manifold
L
without boundary.

We prove now a Poincaré duality theorem. We are in the right context for doing so; but we shall not need the result for the later treatment, and the reader in a hurry can proceed safely to Proposition
2.66
below.

Theorem 2.58
.

Let
{\mathbf{b}}
be an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
whose local system has one-dimensional fibres.
Then there exists a nondegenerate pairing

HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\otimes HF^{n-*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\to\Lambda.

(2.38)

Proof.

We begin by recalling the notion of filtered
A_{\infty}
bimodules.
Let
C
be a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
\Lambda
-linear filtered curved
A_{\infty}
algebra.
By a
bimodule
over
C
we mean
a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
\Lambda
module
{\mathbin{\cal P}}
equipped with operations

{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathbin{\cal P}}}^{r|1|s}:\left(C[1]\right)^{\otimes r}\otimes{\mathbin{\cal P}}\otimes\left(C[1]\right)^{\otimes s}\to{\mathbin{\cal P}},

(2.39)

r,s=0,1,2,\cdots,
which satisfy the bimodule version of the
A_{\infty}
equation. An
A_{\infty}
bimodule bimodule is
filtered
if it is equipped with a valuation, so that
A_{\infty}
bimodule operations do not decrease valuations.

Example 2.59
.

The
A_{\infty}
algebra
C
itself has the structure of a
diagonal
bimodule with operations

{\mathfrak{m}}^{r|1|s}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},p,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})=(-1)^{r+\sum\deg x_{i}}{\mathfrak{m}}^{r+1+s}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},p,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}),

(2.40)

r,s=0,1,2,\cdots;

x_{1},\cdots,x_{r},p,y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\in C.

Example 2.60
.

If
{\mathbin{\cal P}}
is an
A_{\infty}
bimodule over
C
then its
dual

{\mathbin{\cal P}}^{\vee}:=\hom({\mathbin{\cal P}}^{\vee},\Lambda)
has the structure of a bimodule given by

{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathbin{\cal P}}^{\vee}}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{s},\psi,x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})(p)=(-1)^{\#}\psi\left({\mathfrak{m}}_{{\mathbin{\cal P}}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},p,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})\right),

(2.41)

r,s=0,1,2,\cdots;

x_{1},\cdots,x_{r},y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}\in C,

\psi\in{\mathbin{\cal P}}^{\vee},

p\in{\mathbin{\cal P}}
and

\#=1+\deg\psi+(\deg y_{1}+\dots+\deg y_{s})(\deg x_{1}+\dots+\deg x_{r}+\deg p+\deg\psi).

Next let
{\mathbf{b}}=(L,E,b)\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
and apply to the single brane
(L,E)
the proof of Theorem
2.43
.
Then we get a gapped
A_{\infty}
algebra
C.

We use the same notation for the diagonal bimodule, and denote by
C^{\vee}
its dual bimodule.
Recall that the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{1}
of
C
has leading term equal to the usual differential, whose cohomology group is

CF^{*}(E,E):=H^{*}(L\times_{X}L,\hom(E,E))[-\mu_{L}].

(2.42)

We prove

Proposition 2.61
.

There exists a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
\Lambda
-linear natural isomorphism of graded vector spaces

CF^{*}(E,E)\cong CF^{*}(E,E)^{\vee}[-n].

(2.43)

Proof.

If
\Lambda
has characteristic
\neq 2
then the condition that
L
be relatively spin includes the data of an orientation of the domain
\hat{L}
of the immersed Lagrangian
L\subset X
, so there is a Poincaré duality isomorphism

H^{*}(\hat{L},\hom(E,E))\times H^{*}(\hat{L},\hom(E,E))\to\Lambda[-n]

(2.44)

where we do not have to change the local-system term because
\hom(E,E)^{\vee}\cong\hom(E,E).

For an off-diagonal self-intersection pair
(x,y)\in L\times_{X}L
there is also a pairing

(x,y)\otimes(y,x)\to\Lambda[-n]

(2.45)

which completes the proof.
∎

The following is the heart of the proof of Theorem
2.58
.

Lemma 2.62
.

There exists a bimodule homomorphism

\Psi:C\to C^{\vee}[-n]

(2.46)

such that the map
\Psi^{0|1|0}:C\to C^{\vee}[-n]
has leading term equal to (
2.43
).

Proof.

Recall that
C
is by construction an inverse limit of some gapped
A_{K}
algebras, which we denote by
(C_{K})_{K=0}^{\infty}.

Regard this as a diagonal bimodule with operations of the form

{\mathfrak{m}}^{r|1|s}=\sum{\mathfrak{m}}^{r|1|s}_{\gamma}T^{\gamma},

(2.47)

r,s\geqslant 0
and
[r+s+1,\gamma]\leq K.

For each
K
we construct a bimodule map

\Psi_{K}:C_{K}\to C^{\vee}_{K}

which consists of a collection of

\Psi_{K}^{r|1|s}:C_{K}^{\times r}\times C_{K}\times C_{K}^{\times s}\to C^{\vee}_{K}[-n]

(2.48)

indexed by nonnegative integers
r
and
s
.
We regard the output as an input by duality, so that we have to construct a compatible collection of maps

C_{K}\times C_{K}^{\times r}\times C_{K}\times C_{K}^{\times s}\to\Lambda[-n].

(2.49)

Here we use the hypothesis that the local system of
{\mathbf{b}}
has one-dimensional fibres.

In order to construct this map, we use the stable moduli spaces of pseudoholomorphic discs with
r+s+2
boundary points, ordered as in Figure
2.1
: the marked point on the right corresponds to the input, and on the left to the output.

C^{\vee}
C
x_{r}
x_{r-1}
y_{1}
y_{2}
x_{1}
y_{s}

Figure 2.1:
Moduli spaces of discs for the duality map

We choose inductively a family of perturbation data for the fibre products of the form

{\mathbin{\cal M}}_{\gamma}^{r+s+2}\times_{\rm ev}(q\times x_{1}\times\cdots\times x_{r}\times p\times\tau_{1}\times\cdots\times\tau_{s})

(2.50)

where
{\rm ev}
denotes the evaluation maps at the boundary marked points ordered along the boundary and
q,x_{1},\dots,x_{r},p,y_{1},\dots,y_{s}
are singular simplices in
C
with
[r+s+1,\gamma]\leqslant\penalty K.

We make these compatible with the previous perturbations of (
2.36
) and those for the lower-order maps relative to
r,s.
Then we get a
A_{K}
bimodule homomorphism, which we denote by
\Psi_{K}:C_{K}\to C^{\vee}_{K}[-n].

From the
A_{K}
bimodule maps of this form we construct an
A_{\infty}
bimodule map by the inductions and obstruction theory arguments. The algebraic framework for this is given by Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, §5.2]
, used originally for the deformation invariance of Floer cohomology groups;
which is in fact a homotopy equivalence of two bimodules
[
21
, Theorem D]
.

Recall that we have constructed an
A_{K}
homotopy equivalence between
C_{K}
and the
A_{K}
truncation of
C_{K+1}.

From this we get an
A_{K+1}
algebra
C_{K,K+1}
which is
A_{K+1}
homotopy equivalent to
C_{K+1}
and whose
A_{K}
trunation is
C_{K}.

The
A_{K+1}
bimodule map

C_{K+1}\to C^{\vee}_{K+1}[-n]

which induces an
A_{K+1}
bimodule map (over the algebra
C_{K,K+1}
)

C_{K,K+1}\to C^{\vee}_{K,K+1}[-n].

(2.51)

which we denote still by
\Psi_{K}.
In the previous stage we have constructed an
A_{K}
bimodule map

\Psi_{K-1}:C_{K-1,K}\to C^{\vee}_{K-1,K}[-n].

(2.52)

This is homotopic to the
A_{K}
reduction of (
2.51
) by a choice of homotopy between the defining perturbation data. Using homological perturbation, and proceeding by induction, we get the desired system of bimodule maps.
∎

Combining Lemma
2.62
with the
A_{\infty}
bimodule version of Whitehead’s theorem
[
21
, Theorem 5.2.35]
we get

Corollary 2.63
.

\Psi
is a homotopy equivalence of bimodules.∎

The bimodule homotopy equivalence
\Psi:C\to C^{\vee}[-n]
induces for every bounding cochain
b\in C^{1}

another bimodule homotopy equivalence

\Psi_{b}:C\to C^{\vee}[-n],

(2.53)

relative to the
b
-deformed
A_{\infty}
structure; and for
x_{1},\cdots,x_{r},p,y_{1},\cdots,y_{s}\in CF^{*}(E,E)
the term

\Psi^{r|1|s}_{b}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},p,y_{1},\ldots,y_{s})

(2.54)

is given by

\sum\Psi(b,\ldots,b,x_{1},b,\ldots,b,x_{r},b,\ldots,b,p,b,\ldots,b,y_{1},b,\ldots,b,y_{s},b,\ldots,b).

(2.55)

Hence, passing to the cohomology groups, we get an isomorphism

HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\cong HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})^{\vee}[-n]

(2.56)

which completes the proof of Theorem
2.58
.
∎

Theorem
2.58
implies

Corollary 2.64
.

Let
{\mathbf{b}}
be a non-zero object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
whose local system has one-dimensional fibres.
Then
HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\neq 0.
∎

Remark 2.65
.

This is proved already for embedded Lagrangians without local systems by Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Theorem E]
.
The second author
[
33
, Lemma 4.4]
gives two other proofs, including immersed Lagrangians. One uses the cyclic symmetry
[
19
]
assuming that the ground field
{\mathbf{k}}
contains
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.
The other uses open-closed maps
[
21
, Theorem 6.4.2]
.

We end by recalling a result of the second author
[
33
, Corollary 4.7 (ii)]
which we state in a slightly different way because we include local systems.

Proposition 2.66
.

Let
{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
be isomorphic in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
and supported on closed special Lagrangians of phase
\phi,\phi^{\prime}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
respectively which need not be
0
modulo
2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Suppose
HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\neq 0.

Then
\phi-\phi^{\prime}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.
∎

Proof.

Recall from Corollary
2.56
that
{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
are non-zero objects so that both
HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
and
HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
are non-zero. Then the same proof works as in
[
33
, Corollary 4.7 (ii)]
.
∎

Corollary
2.64
gives a sufficient condition for the hypothesis above.

2.6
Thomas–Yau Theorems

We recall a few generalizations of Thomas–Yau’s theorem proved by the second author
[
33
]
.
In this subsection we work with Calabi–Yau manifolds and integrable complex structures.

Proposition 2.67
.

Let
X
be a Calabi–Yau manifold of complex dimension
n.
Let
{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
be supported on closed irreducibly immersed special Lagrangians
L,L^{\prime}
of phase
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
respectively which need not be
0
modulo
2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.
Suppose that either
L
or
L^{\prime}
embedded. Then the following hold.

(i)

If
\phi=\phi^{\prime}
then either
L=L^{\prime}\subset X
or

HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
is supported in degrees
1,\cdots,n-1.

(ii)

If
{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
are isomorphic in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
with
HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\neq 0
then
L=L^{\prime}\subset X.

Remark 2.68
.

The point of (i) is that the degrees are strictly
>0
and
<n.

It follows immediately from (
2.28
) that they belong to
\{0,\dots,n\}.

Proof of Proposition
2.67
.

Let
L
be embedded. Then we can work in a Weinstein neighbourhood of
L
which is a Stein manifold. This is the condition (ii) of the second author’s result
[
33
, Theorem 6.2]
, which implies (i) above. We prove (ii) now. By Proposition
2.66
the two special Lagrangians have the same phase up to shift. Hence it follows by (i) that either
L=L^{\prime}
or
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
is supported in
k,\dots,k+n-2
for some
k\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.
But the latter is impossible because the graded vector space
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\cong HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
has two non-trivial degrees with difference
n.
So
L=L^{\prime}.

∎

We recall also the following version.
We say that a Calabi–Yau manifold
X
is
real analytic
if the Kähler form of
X
is real analytic with respect to the underlying real analytic structure of the complex manifold
X.

Proposition 2.69
.

Let
X
be a real analytic Calabi–Yau manifold and
L,L^{\prime}\subset X
two closed irreducibly-immersed Lagrangians which are, near
L\cap L^{\prime},
both special and graded by
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

Then either
L,L^{\prime}
agree with each other near
L\cap L^{\prime}
or any pair of objects supported on them have Floer cohomology supported in degrees
1,\dots,n-1.

Proof.

Suppose
L\neq L^{\prime}
and denote by
S\subseteq L\cap L^{\prime}
the set of intersection points at which
L,L^{\prime}
have at least one common tangent space. The second author
[
33
, Theorem 5.1(i)]
proves then that there exists a neighbourhood
U\subset X
of
S
and arbitrarily-small Hamiltonian perturbations of
L,L^{\prime}
respectively which intersect generically each other with no intersection point in
U
of index
0
or
n.

On the other hand, these have no intersection point of index
0
or
n
outside
U

[
33
, Lemma 2.7]
.
So they have no intersection point at all of index
0
or
n.

Since
L,L^{\prime}
are both graded by
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
it follows by (
2.28
) that every intersection point of the two perturbed Lagrangians has degree in
\{1,\dots,n-1\}.
So their Floer complex is supported in degrees
1,\dots,n-1,
which completes the proof.
∎

2.7
Hamiltonian Continuations

We return now to the symplectic topology context.
We recall the definition of homotopies between strict
A_{\infty}
functors.
Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}}
be strict
A_{\infty}
categories. Then we can define the functor category
\fct({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}})
which is a strict
A_{\infty}
category whose objects are strict
A_{\infty}
functors from
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to
{\mathbin{\cal B}}.

We say that two objects
f,g\in\obj\fct({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}})
are
homotopic
if they are isomorphic in
H^{0}\fct({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}}).

Here we do not impose any conditions on the object sets as we do in Definition
2.20
.

We say that a strict
A_{\infty}
functor
f:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal B}}
is a
homotopy equivalence
if there exists
g\in\fct({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathbin{\cal A}})
such that
g\circ f
is isomorphic in
H^{0}\fct({\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal A}})
to the identity on
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and
f\circ g
isomorphic in
H^{0}\fct({\mathbin{\cal B}},{\mathbin{\cal B}})
to the identity on
{\mathbin{\cal B}}.

Seidel
[
53
, Theorem 2.9]
proves that
f
is an homotopy equivalence if and only if the induced functor
Hf:H{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to H{\mathbin{\cal B}}
is an equivalence of categories.

We explain now the effect of a symplectomorphism of
X
upon the Fukaya categories.
Recall from Definition
2.28
that the radius function
r_{\lambda}
is defined near
{\partial}X.

Note also that every symplectictomorphism acts naturally upon the objects of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X).

Lemma 2.70
.

Let
\phi:X\to X
be a symplectomorphism isotopic to the identity and which agrees near
{\partial}X
with the Hamilotonian diffeomorphism of
cr_{\lambda}
for some
c>0.

Suppose that
\phi
induces a bijection of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X).

Then there exists an equivalence

\phi_{*}:H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)\to H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)

(2.57)

such that for every
{\mathbf{b}}=(L,E,b)\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
the image
\phi{\mathbf{b}}
is given by a certain bounding cochain
\phi_{*}b
for
(\phi_{*}L,\phi_{*}E)\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X).

The functor
\phi_{*}
is compatible with composition in the sense that, for a pair
\phi
and
\psi
of boundary-linear symplectormorphisms,
the composite
\psi_{*}\circ\phi_{*}
is naturally isomorphic to
(\psi\circ\phi)_{*}.

Proof.

The strict
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
is made from a gapped
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
whose definition involves the choice of an almost complex structure
J
and other data.
We can then define another gapped category by using
\phi_{*}J.

We want to show that the two gapped
A_{\infty}
categories are homotopy equivalent. This is done by Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Theorem A]
and by Akaho–Joyce
[
9
, Theorem 11.2(a)]
for gapped
A_{\infty}
algebras by interpolating between the almost complex structures; we do so while maintaining compatibility with the contact boundary, thus ensuring compactness.
They use the obstruction theory arguments for gapped
A_{N}
functors
[
21
, Lemma 7.2.129]
for gapped
A_{\infty}
algebras. We extend them to gapped
A_{\infty}
categories in the same way as in the proof of Theorem
2.26
.
Then we get a homotopy equivalence of the two gapped categories.
Fukaya
[
20
, Proposition 13.18]
proves that this induces a homotopy equivalence of the corresponding strict categories.
There is thus an equivalence (
2.57
) of the cohomology categories.

To find the natural isomorphism between
\psi_{*}\circ\phi_{*}
and
(\psi\circ\phi)_{*}
we begin with homotopies of gapped
A_{N}
categories. We lift them to gapped
A_{\infty}
categories by an obstruction theory for homotopies of homotopies. This is done by Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Corollary 7.2.219]
for gapped
A_{N}
algebras. We extend it in the same way as in the proof of Theorem
2.26
. Then we get a homotopy between
\psi_{*}\circ\phi_{*}
and
(\psi\circ\phi)_{*}
as gapped
A_{\infty}
functors. This induces again a homotopy between the strict
A_{\infty}
categories, which induces in turn a natural isomorphism that we want.
∎

There is more to say if the symplectomorphism
\phi
in Lemma
2.70
is a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism:

Proposition 2.71
.

Let
\phi:X\to X
be the Hamiltonian diffeomorphism of a smooth function
X\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
which agrees near
{\partial}X
with
cr_{\lambda}
for some
c>0.

Suppose that
\phi
induces a bijection of
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X).

Then there exists a natural transformation
\kappa_{\phi}
from
\phi_{*}
to the identity functor; that is,
for any
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2}\in{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\phi}{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\kappa_{\phi}{\mathbf{b}}_{2}}

(2.58)

If either
{\mathbf{b}}_{1}
or
{\mathbf{b}}_{2}
are supported on a compact Lagrangian,
then these arrows are isomorphisms.
If there is another such Hamiltonian diffeomorphism
\psi:X\to X

then

\kappa_{\psi\phi}{\mathbf{b}}=\kappa_{\psi}(\phi{\mathbf{b}})\circ\kappa_{\phi}{\mathbf{b}},\;\;\;{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X).

(2.59)

Proof.

We explain how to deduce the statement from the result of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Theorem G (G.4)]
who prove that pairs of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms induce bimodule homomorphisms of Floer groups (Akaho–Joyce explain in
[
9
, Theorem 13.6]
how to extend this to immersed Lagrangians, and it is also straightforward to include local systems). As in Lemma
2.70
, the construction of the bimodule homomorphisms involves pseudo-holomorphic curve equations with respect to varying almost complex structures on the target, and we require their compatibility with the contact boundary to ensure compactness.

Define
\kappa_{\phi}{\mathbf{b}}\in HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
to be the image of the unit of
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
under the bimodule homomorphism
(\phi,{\rm id})_{*}:HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\to HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
induced by the pair
(\phi,{\rm id})
of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms.
We prove then that the diagram (
2.58
) commutes.
The pair
(\phi,{\rm id})
of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms induces the bimodule homomorphism
(\phi,{\rm id})_{*}:HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{2},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})\to HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{2},{\mathbf{b}}_{2}).
In particular, this is a left module homomorphism and there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})\times HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{2},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{2})\times HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{2},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2}).}

(2.60)

Consider now the direct sum
{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2}.
If these are supported on distinct Lagrangians
L_{1},L_{2}
respectively then regard
{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2}
as an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
supported on
L_{1}\cup L_{2}.
If they are supported on the same Lagrangian then regard it as an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
with local system
E_{1}\oplus E_{2}
where
E_{1},E_{2}
are the local systems of
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2}
respectively.
In either case the pair
(\phi,{\rm id})
induces a bimodule homomorphism
(\phi,{\rm id})_{*}:HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2})\to HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2}).
In particular, this is a right module homomorphism and there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2})\times HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2})\times HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1}\oplus{\mathbf{b}}_{2}).}

We look at the following component, which commutes too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1})\times HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{1})\times HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}}_{1},{\mathbf{b}}_{2}).}

(2.61)

This combined with (
2.60
) implies the commutative diagram (
2.58
).

We finally prove (
2.59
).
Using (
2.61
) with
\psi
in place of
\phi,

\phi{\mathbf{b}}
in place of
{\mathbf{b}}_{1},
and
{\mathbf{b}}
in place of
{\mathbf{b}}_{2}
we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},\phi{\mathbf{b}})\times HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\psi\phi{\mathbf{b}},\phi{\mathbf{b}})\times HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF^{*}(\psi\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}).}

(2.62)

Consider the unit of
HF^{0}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},\phi{\mathbf{b}})
and
\kappa_{\phi}{\mathbf{b}}\in HF^{*}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
and look at the image of this pair. Then we see that

\kappa_{\psi}(\phi{\mathbf{b}})\circ\kappa_{\phi}{\mathbf{b}}=(\psi,{\rm id})_{*}\kappa_{\phi}{\mathbf{b}}=(\psi,{\rm id})_{*}(\phi,{\rm id})_{*}{\mathbf{b}}.

(2.63)

But Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Theorem G (G.4)]
prove
(\psi,{\rm id})_{*}(\phi,{\rm id})_{*}=(\psi\phi,{\rm id})_{*}
so the right-hand side of (
2.63
) is equal to
(\psi\phi,{\rm id})_{*}{\mathbf{b}}=\kappa_{\psi\phi}{\mathbf{b}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

2.8
Wrapped Fukaya Categories

In general, if
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is an
A_{\infty}
category and
Z\subseteq H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal A}}
a set of morphisms,
there exists a universal pair consisting of an
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{Z^{-1}}
and an
A_{\infty}
functor
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{Z^{-1}}
such that every element of
Z
becomes invertible in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{Z^{-1}}.
The key property that we shall use is the fact that this construction is compatible with passage to triangulated categories in the sense that there is a natural equivalence of triangulated categories

H^{0}\left({\mathbin{\cal A}}_{Z^{-1}}\right)\cong(H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal A}})_{Z^{-1}}

(2.64)

where the right-hand side is the localisation of a triangulated category.

The next result is a key computational tool for computing Fukaya categories from localisations, and also implies the above equivalence of triangulated categories in this specific context:

Proposition 2.72
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be an
A_{\infty}
category,
and
f:H{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to H{\mathbin{\cal A}}
an equivalence.
For objects
a,b\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
write
{\mathbin{\cal A}}(a,b)
the morphism space of the pair
(a,b).

Let
\kappa
be a natural transformation from
f
to the identity functor;
and
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{\kappa^{-1}}
the localisation of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
by the set
\{\kappa_{a}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}(fa,a)\}_{a\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
.
Then for any two objects
a,b\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
there exist natural isomorphisms

H{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{\kappa^{-1}}(a,b)\cong\displaystyle\varinjlim_{i\to\infty}H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(f^{i}a,b)\cong\displaystyle\varinjlim_{j\to\infty}H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(a,f^{-j}b)\cong\displaystyle\varinjlim_{i,j\to\infty}H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(f^{i}a,f^{-j}b)

(2.65)

where
f^{-1}:H{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to H{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is an inverse to
f.

Also for
a,b,c\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal A}}
there exists a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\varinjlim_{i\to\infty}H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(f^{i}a,b)\otimes\displaystyle\varinjlim_{j\to\infty}H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(b,f^{-j}c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\displaystyle\varinjlim_{i,j\to\infty}H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(f^{i}a,f^{-j}c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle H{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{\kappa^{-1}}(a,b)\otimes H{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{\kappa^{-1}}(b,c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{\kappa^{-1}}(a,c)}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

(2.66)

where the horizontal arrows are products maps and the vertical isomorphisms are obtained from those of (
2.65
).

Proof.

Assuming that the category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is triangulated, the localisation
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{Z^{-1}}
may be constructed as the categorical quotient of
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
by the subcategory consisting of the cones of all morphisms
\kappa:a\to f(a)
. Morphisms in the quotient category are then computed by the direct limit of the groups
H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(a,f^{-j}b)
as proved in
[
55
, Lemma 7.18]
. The fact that this is isomorphic to the direct limit of the groups
H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(f^{i}a,b)
is then a consequence of iteratively applying the functor, and the expression in terms of the direct limit of the groups
H{\mathbin{\cal A}}(f^{i}a,f^{-j}b)
is implied by the constancy of the colimit with respect to either variable. The expression for the product is an immediate consequence of functoriality.
∎

We shall also need the following observation:

Lemma 2.73
.

Assume that
K\subseteq Z\subseteq H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal A}}
are two sets of morphisms such that for each element
\beta\in Z
, there exists a morphism
\gamma
such that
\beta\circ\gamma
is a composition of morphisms in
K
.
Then the natural functor
H{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{K^{-1}}\to H{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{Z^{-1}}
is an equivalence.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{K^{-1}}
satisfies the universal property of the localisation away from
Z
, i.e. that every morphism in
Z
is already invertible in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{K^{-1}}
. This is immediate from the assumption because a composition of morphisms in
K
becomes invertible in
{\mathbin{\cal A}}_{K^{-1}}
, and the only way for a product of morphisms to be invertible is if both factors are so.
∎

We now arrive at the definition of the wrapped category in the contact-type setting:

Definition 2.74
.

Let
\Phi
be a set of Hamiltonians
\phi:X\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
which are linear at the boundary in the sense that they agree with
\phi=cr,
for some constant
c>0,
near
{\partial}X
. Assume that this set contains Hamiltonians whose slopes are arbitrarily large.
Identify these with the time-one maps they generate, and suppose that if
\phi\in\Phi
and
E\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X)
then
\phi_{*}E\in{\mathbin{\cal C}}(X).

Then
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
is defined as the
localisation
of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
by the set

\{\kappa_{\phi}\in HF^{0}(\phi{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}):\phi\in\Phi,{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)\}

of continuation morphisms.

As a consequence of Lemma
2.73
this definition does not depend on the set of Hamiltonians which are chosen: if we add or subtract a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism to the set
\Phi
, then the resulting localisation does not change because of the existence of continuation maps to Hamiltonians of larger slope.

The closed Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
is embedded naturally as a full subcategory of
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)

because the continuation morphisms are invertible in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X).

2.9
Exact Lagrangians

Suppose now that the Liouville
1
-form
\lambda
is given globally on
X
so that we can speak of exact Lagrangians in
(X,\lambda).
Denote by
{\mathbf{k}}\subset\Lambda
the ground field of the Novikov field.
Denote by
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(X)
the full subcategory of those objects
(L,E)
for which:

•

the Lagrangian
L\subset X
is embedded and exact; that is,
L
has no self-intersection point and
\lambda|_{L}
is an exact
1
-form;

•

the local system
E
is of the form
{\mathbin{\cal E}}\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}\Lambda
for some
{\mathbf{k}}
local system
{\mathbin{\cal E}}
on
L;
and

•

(L,E)
is given the trivial bounding cochain, which exists because

L
is embedded and does not bound any noncontant pseudoholomorphic disc.

For
(L,E)\in{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
take any primitive function
h:L\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
with
dh=\lambda|_{L}.

For
(L_{0},E_{0}),(L_{1},E_{1})\in{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
we define the
action
function

{\mathbin{\cal A}}:L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}

(2.67)

by
{\mathbin{\cal A}}(x):=h_{0}(x)-h_{1}(x)
where
h_{0},h_{1}
are the primitives of
(L_{0},E_{0}),(L_{1},E_{1})
respectively.
We recall:

Proposition 2.75
.

Let
(L_{0},E_{0}),\cdots,(L_{k},E_{k})\in{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
and
h_{0},\cdots,h_{k}
their primitives respectively.
As in the proof of Theorem
2.43
take on
X
a compatible almost-complex structure
J,
define the moduli space
{\mathbin{\cal M}}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})
and define the evaluation map

{\mathbin{\cal M}}(L_{1},\cdots,L_{k};L_{0})\to\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(L_{i}\times_{X}L_{i+1})\times(L_{0}\times_{X}L_{k}).

(2.68)

Then the fibre of this map over a point
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k};x_{0})
consists of
J
-holomorphic discs of area

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\mathbin{\cal A}}(x_{i})\right)-{\mathbin{\cal A}}(x_{0}).

(2.69)

Proof.

In the cyclic notation, modulo
k+1
for indices, the area is equal to

\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}\int_{x_{i-1}}^{x_{i}}\lambda&=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}h_{i-1}(x_{i})-h_{i-1}(x_{i-1})=\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}h_{i-1}(x_{i})-\sum_{i=0}^{k}h_{i}(x_{i})\\
&=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i-1}(x_{i})-h_{i}(x_{i})\right)+h_{k}(x_{0})-h_{0}(x_{0})\end{split}

(2.70)

which is equal to (
2.69
).
∎

This implies that the
A_{\infty}
structure of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
is reducible to
{\mathbf{k}}
:

Corollary 2.76
.

{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
has the structure of a
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear
A_{\infty}
category
whose hom space between two objects
(L_{0},{\mathbin{\cal E}}_{0}),(L_{1},{\mathbin{\cal E}}_{1})
is given by

CF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}({\mathbin{\cal E}}_{0},{\mathbin{\cal E}}_{1}):=H^{*}(L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1},\hom_{\mathbf{k}}({\mathbin{\cal E}}_{0},{\mathbin{\cal E}}_{1})[-\mu_{L_{0}L_{1}}])

(2.71)

and whose structure maps
{\mathfrak{m}}_{{\rm ex}}^{k}
are defined by the same disc counts but without the
T^{\gamma}
factor.
The
\Lambda
-linear extension of this
A_{\infty}
category is isomorphic to that of the subcategory
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
with the same objects, under the maps

T^{\mathbin{\cal A}}:CF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}({\mathbin{\cal E}}_{0},{\mathbin{\cal E}}_{1})\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}\Lambda\to CF^{*}(E_{0},E_{1}),\;\;\;E_{i}={\mathbin{\cal E}}_{i}\otimes_{\mathbf{k}}\Lambda,

(2.72)

defined as the multiplication by
T^{{\mathbin{\cal A}}(x)}
on the component of each
x\in L_{0}\times_{X}L_{1}.

∎

Proof.

This follows from the proof of Theorem
2.43
;
the point is that once we have fixed the data
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k};x_{0})
relevant to the
A_{\infty}
operation in Equation (
2.37
),
the
\gamma
in the structure map
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma}
will be equal to (
2.69
).
∎

Choose now a countable set of Hamiltonians as in Definition
2.74
.
Denote by
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
the localisation of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
with respect to this.
Using (
2.72
) we regard
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
as a full subcategory of
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X).

Consider now a Fukaya category
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm dir}(X)
with the same object set
as
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
but defined by direct perturbations
[
53
]
.
Denote by
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{\rm dir}(X)
the localisation of this with respect to the same Hamiltonians.
We prove that
H{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
is equivalent to
H{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{\rm dir}(X).

We consider only the cohomology categories rather than the
A_{\infty}
categories, which will do for the treatment in the later sections.

Proposition 2.77
.

H{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)
is equivalent to
H{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{\rm dir}(X).

Proof.

We define a functor
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X)\to H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm dir}(X).
We leave out the local systems to save notation.
For two exact embedded Lagrangians
L_{a},L_{b}
we define a
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear map

HF^{*}_{{\rm ex}}(L_{a},L_{b})\to HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{a},L_{b}).

Suppose either
L_{a}
or
L_{b}
has no boundary.
Then
HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{a},L_{b})
is defined as
HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{a}L_{a},\phi_{b}L_{b})
for some Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms with compact support in
X\setminus{\partial}X.

More precisely, the Floer group
HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{a}L_{a},\phi_{b}L_{b})
is defined by the direct perturbations. But these may be regarded as a different choice of virtual chains, so the resulting Floer groups will be canonically isomorphic. This is done by Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Theorem G]
(in the context of the present paper the similar arguments have appeared in the proof of Lemma
2.70
).
There is thus a canonical isomorphism
HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{a},L_{b})\cong HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{a}L_{a},\phi_{b}L_{b}).

There is another isomorphism
HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{a}L_{a},\phi_{b}L_{b})\cong HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{a},L_{b})
from which we get an isomorphism
HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{a},L_{b})\cong HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{a},L_{b})
that we want.

Suppose
L_{a},L_{b}
have both Legendrian collar. Then the morphisms of
(L_{a},L_{b})
form the wrapped Floer groups
\varinjlim HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi L_{a},L_{b})
and
\varinjlim HF_{\rm dir}^{*}(\phi L_{a},L_{b}).

Since the isomorphism

HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi L_{a},L_{b})\to HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(\phi L_{a},L_{b})
exists for every
\phi
we get an isomorphism
HW_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{a},L_{b})\to HW^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{a},L_{b}).

We prove that the
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear maps thus defined are compatible with the products.
Let
a,b,c\in{\mathbb{N}}
and suppose first that at least two of
L_{a},L_{b},L_{c}
have no boundary.
Write
HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{a},L_{b})=HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{a}L_{a},\phi_{b}L_{b})
and
HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{b},L_{c})=HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}L_{b},\phi_{c}L_{c}).

Put
K:=\phi_{b}^{-1}\phi_{a}L_{a}\sqcup\phi_{b}L_{b}.

Recall that the isomorphism
HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(K,L_{c})\cong HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}K,\phi_{c}L_{c})
is a bimodule isomorphism with respect to
(\phi_{b})_{*}:HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(K,K)\to HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}K,\phi_{b}K)
and
(\phi_{c})_{*}:HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{c},L_{c})\to HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{c}L_{c},\phi_{c}L_{c}).
It is in particular a left module isomorphism with respect to
(\phi_{b})_{*}
so the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(K,K)\times HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(K,L_{c})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(K,L_{c})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}K,\phi_{b}K)\times HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}K,\phi_{c}L_{c})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}K,\phi_{c}L_{c})}

commutes. The upper arrow contains the product map
HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{a},L_{b})\times HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{b},L_{c})\to HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{b},L_{c})
and the lower arrow contains the product map
HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{a}L_{a},\phi_{b}L_{b})\times HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}L_{b},\phi_{c}L_{c})\to HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi_{b}L_{b},\phi_{c}L_{c}).
These are thus compatible.

Suppose now that at least two of
L_{a},L_{b},L_{c}
have Legendrian collar. Then
HW^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{a},L_{b})=\varinjlim HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi^{n}L_{a},L_{b})
and
HW^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{b},L_{c})=\varinjlim HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{b},\phi^{-n}L_{c}).

The isomorphisms

HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi^{n}L_{a},L_{b})\to HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(\phi^{n}L_{a},L_{b})

and

HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(L_{b},\phi^{-n}L_{c})\to HF^{*}_{\rm dir}(L_{b},\phi^{-n}L_{c})

are compatible with the product maps, completing the proof.
∎

2.10
Cotangent Bundles

Let
Q
be a compact connected Riemannian manifold without boundary, and
X\subset T^{*}Q
the unit disc sub-bundle.
Recall that the wrapped Floer group
HW_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
is computed by
Abbondandolo, Portaluri and Schwarz
[
1
]
; they construct a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded ring isomorphism

HW_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)\cong H_{-*}(\Omega_{q}Q,{\mathbf{k}})

(2.73)

where
\Omega_{q}Q
denotes the based-loop space and
H_{-*}(\Omega_{q}Q)
is non-positively graded;
We shall not need an
A_{\infty}
lift of (
2.73
) as treated by the first author
[
4
]
.

To define the Fukaya categories
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X),

{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
we do the following:

•

We take the background class on
T^{*}Q
to be the pull-back by the projection
T^{*}Q\to Q
of the Stiefel–Whitney class
w_{2}(Q)\in H^{2}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}).

So the zero-section
Q\subset T^{*}Q
has a relatively spin structure, and we give it a natural one which we shall define shortly below.

•

Take the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading of
T^{*}Q
to be that by the complexification of a squared real volume form on
Q.

So there exist
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-gradings of
Q.

We include in
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
the object supported on
Q
with the trivial rank-one local system and the trivial bounding cochain.

•

We give
T^{*}Q
the standard Liouville
1
-form
\lambda
so that
Q
is an exact Lagrangian.
We fix a point
q\in Q
and include in
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)
the object supported on the fibre
T_{q}^{*}Q
with the trivial relative spin structure, an arbitrary
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-grading, the trivial rank-one local system and the trivial bounding cochain.

Here is the natural relative spin structure on
Q
:

Lemma 2.78
.

The Lagrangian
Q\subset T^{*}Q
has a relative spin structure such that the
\pi_{1}Q
representation on
HF^{*}(Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
is trivial. If
{\mathbf{k}}
has characteristic
2
this representation is automatically trivial.

Proof.

If
{\mathbf{k}}
has characteristic
2
then
Q
defines an object of the exact Fukaya category over
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}

and the
\pi_{1}Q
representation on
HF^{*}(Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
makes sense over
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.

But this is automatically trivial because
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
has only one unit.

Suppose now that
{\mathbf{k}}
has characteristic
\neq 2
and choose at first any relative spin structure on
Q
.
Then
Q
defines an object of the exact Fukaya category over
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
(it is only here in the present paper that we use Fukaya categories over a ring).
The object
Q
defines a representation
\pi_{1}Q\to\mathop{\rm GL}_{1}{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and such representations are classified by elements of
H^{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}).

Since the set of relative spin structures is a torsor over the same group
H^{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}),
we can then choose a possibly different relative spin structure which makes this representation trivial.
∎

We compute the representations corresponding to
C^{1}
perturbations of the zero-section
Q\subset T^{*}Q.

Lemma 2.79
.

Let
\alpha
be a closed
1
-form
\alpha
on
Q
whose graph
Q^{\alpha}\subset T^{*}Q
lies in the sub-bundle
X\subset T^{*}Q.

Let
E
be a filtered local system over
Q^{\alpha}
and regard this as an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X),
which makes sense because
H_{2}(T^{*}Q,Q^{\alpha};{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})=0
and no non-constant pseudo-holomorphic curve exists for
(T^{*}Q,Q^{\alpha}).

Denote by
T^{-[\alpha]}:\pi_{1}Q\to\Lambda^{*}
the one-dimensional representation
x\mapsto T^{-[\alpha]\cdot x}.

Regard
E
as the representation of
\pi_{1}Q^{\alpha}\cong\pi_{1}Q.

Then
T^{-[\alpha]}\otimes E
is isomorphic to the
\pi_{1}Q
representation on
HW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E)
corresponding to the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
operator
HW^{0}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)\times HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E)\to HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E)
under the isomorphism
HW^{0}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)\cong\Lambda[\pi_{1}Q].

Proof.

Let
x\in\pi_{1}(Q,q)
be any element.
Then there exists a corresponding element of
HW^{*}_{{\rm ex}}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
under the isomorphism
HW^{*}_{{\rm ex}}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)\cong{\mathbf{k}}[\pi_{1}Q].

Choose a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism
\phi:T^{*}Q\to T^{*}Q
such that
\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q)
intersects
T_{q}^{*}Q
transversely at
q\in Q
in the zero-section and there exists an element of
CF^{*}_{{\rm ex}}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),T_{q}^{*}Q)
corresponding to
x.

Denote by
\tau:T^{*}Q\to T^{*}Q
the fibrewise translation by
\alpha.
Put
\lambda^{\prime}:=\tau_{*}\lambda=\lambda+\pi^{*}\alpha
where
\pi
is the projection
T^{*}Q\to Q.
This induces a category isomorphism
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X,\lambda)\to H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X,\lambda^{\prime})
and accordingly a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),T_{q}^{*}Q)\times HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,\tau^{*}E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{*}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),\tau^{*}E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),T_{q}^{*}Q)\times HF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces HF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),E)}

where we have written
HF^{\prime}
for the morphism spaces of
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X,\lambda^{\prime}).

Write
CF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}
for the morphism spaces of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{{\rm ex}}(X,\lambda^{\prime}).

Since
CF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}(T_{q}^{*}Q,Q^{\alpha})
has no differential it follows then that the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
operator

CF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),T_{q}^{*}Q)\times CF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E)\to CF_{{\rm ex}}^{\prime}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),E)

agrees with the parallel transport map of
E|_{q}.

The areas of the
J^{\prime}
-holomorphic discs are equal to
\int_{x}^{q}\lambda^{\prime}+\int_{x}^{q}\lambda^{\prime}+\int_{x}^{x}\lambda^{\prime}
where the integrals are as in Figure
2.2
.

q
q
\ell
\bar{x}

Figure 2.2:
a pseudoholomorphic disc for
(q,\hat{x},q)

The first integral is equal to
h_{\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q)}(x)-h_{\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q)}(q)={\mathbin{\cal A}}(x).

The second integral vanishes.
The third integral is equal to
-[\alpha]\cdot x.

Thus the map

CF^{*}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),T_{q}^{*}Q)\times CF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E)\to CF^{*}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),E)

maps
(x,q^{\prime})
to
T^{{\mathbin{\cal A}}(x)-\alpha\cdot x}\rho(x)q^{\prime}.

So
T^{{\mathbin{\cal A}}(x)}x
acts by
T^{-\alpha\cdot x}
upon
CF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E).

As the functor
\tau_{*}:H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q,\lambda)\to H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q,\lambda^{\prime})
is an isomorphism,
T^{{\mathbin{\cal A}}(x)}x
has the same action upon
CF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,\tau^{*}E),
which completes the proof.
∎

We prove another lemma we shall need to prove Theorem
1.3
(ii).
For
y\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
denote by
|y|\geqslant 0
the infinimum of the length of a piecewise smooth loop
\gamma:[0,1]\to Q
with
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=q
and whose homology class
[\gamma]
modulo torsion agrees with
[x].
If
y\neq 0
then
y
may be represented by some geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to Q
with
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=q
of length
|y|.
This may be proved by the standard method in Riemannian geometry, explained by Sakai
[
50
, Chapter V, Lemma 1.5]
for instance (although he deals with free homotopy classes in place of homology classes modulo torsion, this does not affect the proof). We call
|y|
the
least length
of
y.
We say that a geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to Q
is
based
if
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=q.

For
x\in\pi_{1}Q
denote by
[x]\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion)
the image of the natural projection
\pi_{1}Q\to H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion),
and by
|x|
the least length of
[x]\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion}).

Lemma 2.80
.

Let
{\mathbf{b}}
be an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
and put
V:=HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}}),
a finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-vector space. Denote by
\rho:\pi_{1}Q\to\mathop{\rm GL}V
the
\Lambda
-linear representation defined by the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
operator
HW^{0}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)\times V\to V
and the isomorphism
HW^{0}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)\cong\Lambda[\pi_{1}Q].

Let
\Gamma<\pi_{1}Q
be a subgroup,
W\subseteq V
a one-dimensional subspace and
\sigma:\Gamma\to\mathop{\rm GL}W
a sub-representation of
\rho|_{\Gamma}.

Denote by
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma:\Gamma\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
the composite
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma:\Gamma\to\mathop{\rm GL}W\cong\Lambda^{*}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
of group homomorphisms, the last being the original valuation of the Novikov field
\Lambda.

Then for every
x\in\Gamma
with
[x]\neq 0\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion)
we have
|{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)|\leqslant\penalty|x|.

Remark 2.81
.

We can also define the least length in the based homotopy class
x
itself, without passing to the homology class
[x]
modulo torsion, and the same estimate will still hold. But this is weaker than the estimate above because there are fewer loops contained in
x.
We shall need the stronger estimate for Proposition
3.6
below.

Proof of Lemma
2.80
.

Define the wrapped Floer group
HW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
by choosing a countable set
{\mathbin{\cal H}}\subset C^{\infty}(X,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
such that
(i)
every based geodesic may be lifted to a Hamilonian chord for some
H\in{\mathbin{\cal H}};
and
(ii)
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for every Hamiltonian chord
x:[0,1]\to X
for
H
we have
\sup_{t\in[0,1]}|H\circ x(t)|\leqslant\penalty C.

Part (i) is possible by the following fact: denote by
|p|^{2}:X\to[0,1]
the squared distance function from the zero-section
Q\subset X
and let
F:[0,\infty)\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
be smooth with derivative
F^{\prime}\neq 0;
then Hamiltonian chords of
F(|p|^{2}):X\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
are the lifts of based geodesics in
Q.

Part (ii) is possible as in the symplectic cohomology case treated by Venkatesh
[
64
, §2.2]
.

Take any
x\in\Gamma
with
[x]\neq 0\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion).

Choose a geodesic loop
\gamma:[0,1]\to Q
based at
q,
of length
|x|
and with
[\gamma]=[x]\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion).

Abusing notation denote by
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(Q,q)
the based homotopy class of
\gamma.

Using the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space isomorphism

\hom(\pi_{1}P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\cong\hom(H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion),{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})

we see then that
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)={\mathbf{v}}\sigma[\gamma].

Choose now some
H\in{\mathbin{\cal H}}
whose Hamiltonian flow contains the lift of
\gamma.

Recall that
\sigma[\gamma]=(\rho[\gamma])|_{W}:={\mathfrak{m}}^{2}(T^{A(\gamma)}[\gamma],*)|_{W}
and that
{\mathbf{v}}{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
is a filtered homomorphism.
Then
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)={\mathbf{v}}\sigma[\gamma]\geqslant{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\gamma)+{\mathbf{v}}{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}\geqslant{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\gamma)
and

-{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)=-{\mathbin{\cal A}}(\gamma)\leqslant\penalty\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{*}\lambda-\int_{0}^{1}H\circ\gamma(t)dt\leqslant\penalty|x|+C.

(2.74)

Take any
k\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\setminus\{0\}.
Then
[x^{k}]=k[x]\neq 0\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion)
so we can apply (
2.74
) to
x^{k}
in place of
x,
which implies

-k{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)=-{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x^{k})\leqslant\penalty|x^{k}|+C\leqslant\penalty k|x|+C

(2.75)

where the first equality follows since
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma:\Gamma\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
is a group homomorphism and the last from the definition of
|x|.

Dividing (
2.75
) by
k
and letting
k
tend to infinity we get

-{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)\leqslant\penalty|x|.
Replacing
x
by
x^{-1}
we get the same inequality with
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)
in place of
-{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x).
Consequently

|{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)|\leqslant\penalty|x|.

∎

Remark 2.82
.

The proof above is a piece of action-complete Floer theory
[
54
,
63
,
64
]
and local Fukaya categories
[
7
]
.

The proof above of Lemma
2.80
implies also

Corollary 2.83
.

In the circumstances of Lemma
2.80
suppose that the underlying Lagrangian of the object
{\mathbf{b}}
lies within distance
\delta>0
from the zero-section
Q\subset T^{*}Q.

Then
|{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)|\leqslant\penalty\delta|x|
for every
x\in\Gamma.

Proof.

Consider the fibrewise rescale of
T^{*}Q
by
\delta.
The pull-back by this of the canonical
1
-form
\lambda
on
T^{*}Q
is
\delta\lambda,
and the estimate above follows from the proof above.
∎

2.11
The Split-Generation Theorem

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be a strict
A_{\infty}
category with units and
{\mathbin{\cal F}},{\mathbin{\cal G}}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal A}}
full subcategories.
We say that
{\mathbin{\cal F}}
is
split-generated
by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}
if the image of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}
under
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\Pi\Tw{\mathbin{\cal A}}
lies in the full subcategory split-generated by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}.
Here
\Pi\Tw{\mathbin{\cal A}}
is defined for instance by Seidel
[
53
, Lemma 4.7]
. The full subcategory split-generated by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}
is defined by Seidel
[
53
, after Lemma 4.8]
.

Take
{\mathbin{\cal A}}={\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
, with respect to a fixed background class on
X
that we omit from the notation, and denote by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}\subset{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
the full subcategory consisting of the single object
T_{q}^{*}Q.
We prove

Theorem 2.84
.

The Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)\subset{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
is split-generated by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}.

We begin by studying the Viterbo restriction functors.
We work in a slightly more general context than above. Let
E
be a Liouville domain and
X\Subset E
a Liouville subdomain (i.e. a codimension
0
submanifold of
E
, with smooth contact boundary). We assume that we have fixed a background class in
H^{2}(E,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2)
for the construction of Fukaya categories on
E
, and use the restriction of this class to
X
when discussing Fukaya categories on
X
.
We shall assume that all objects of
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(E)
are supported on Lagrangians which are equipped with a primitive function for the Liouville
1
-form, which is constant along the intersection with
\partial X
. In this setting, Abouzaid and Seidel
[
8
]
construct a Viterbo restriction functor

{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(E)\to{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(X).

(2.76)

On the other hand, the maximum principle for pseudoholomorphic discs in
E
bounded by Lagrangians in
X
yields an
A_{\infty}
functor

{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)\to{\mathbin{\cal F}}(E)

(2.77)

on compact Lagrangians. The compatibility between these two functors is given by the following result:

Proposition 2.85
.

The left and right modules over
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{\rm ex}}(E)
associated to the image of each object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
in
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(E)
are equivalent to the pullbacks of the associated modules over
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
under the Viterbo restriction map.

Proof.

We make a little modification of Abouzaid–Seidel’s techniques
[
8
]
. We begin by constructing a Hamiltonian
H
such that for each exact Lagrangian
L\subset E
whose primitive vanishes away from
X
, the generators of the Floer complex
CF^{*}(L,L;H)
which lie away from
X
have strictly positive action. In
[
8
]
one constructs a family depending on a parameter
\rho\in(0,1]
by rescaling
H
along the Liouville flow inside
X
, yielding a function that does not depend on
\rho
away from
X
; this has the property that the action of all generators in
X
are scaled by
\rho
, while those outside have constant action. We consider instead a family parametrised by
\rho\in[1,\infty)
which is constant in
X
, and obtained by rescaling outside; now the action in
X
is constant, but the action outside is scaled.

Consider now a closed (unobstructed) Lagrangian
K
in
X
; we shall omit all notation for the choices of background classes, local systems, and bounding cochains because they are not relevant to our argument, and discuss only the left module structure, since the argument for the right module structure is exactly the same. The goal is to show that the
A_{\infty}
module structure of
CF^{*}(K,L)
over the wrapped Floer cochains of
L
in
E
is equivalent to the module structure obtained by pullback from the corresponding module structure after restriction to
X
. This amounts to constructing a homotopy between the given module structure and one for which we can prove the vanishing of any
A_{\infty}
module operation with input including one chord with boundary on
L
lying away from
X
.

The essential fact that we shall use is that, since
L
is exact, Stokes’s theorem implies that the topological energy of each holomorphic polygon
u
contributing to the
A_{\infty}
module operations is the sum of the negative of the action of the inputs chords with endpoints on
L
, the difference between the values of the primitive of
\lambda
chosen on
L
at the two intersection points with
K
corresponding to the input and output, and the integral
\int\partial^{K}u^{*}\lambda
along the boundary component mapping to
K
. Since
K
is Lagrangian, the restriction of
\lambda
to
K
is closed, so this last integral depends only on the homotopy class of the path.

We now consider the homotopy of
A_{\infty}
-modules associated to the above family of Hamiltonians indexed by a finite interval
\rho\in[1,R]
. We claim that this defines a homotopy between the initial
A_{\infty}
module structure, and one with the property that the
A_{\infty}
action for a given set of inputs, one of which lies outside of
X
, has valuation which is bounded by a quantity which goes to
+\infty
in the limit
R\to+\infty
. The argument is exactly the same as the proof of
[
8
, Lemma 7.4]
: all of these terms in the expression for the topological energy of a polygon are independent of
\rho
, except those corresponding to inputs lying outside
X
; since these are rescaled by
\rho
and are negative, we find that the topological energy of a potential polygon is negative for sufficiently large
\rho
, hence that the moduli space is empty.

Essentially the same argument as in
[
8
]
then implies that the homotopy associated to the entire path
\rho\in[1,\infty)
is well-defined: even though the indexing family is non-compact, for any given collection of inputs, the contribution with a fixed valuation will be supported in a finite interval, which implies convergence by the completeness of the Novikov field.
∎

We shall use the following consequence:

Corollary 2.86
.

If
{\mathbf{b}}
is an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
whose image in
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(E)
lies in the category split-generated by a collection of exact Lagrangians, then
{\mathbf{b}}
lies in the subcategory of
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
split-generated by their images under the restriction functor.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal L}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(E)
and
{\mathbin{\cal R}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(E)
denote the left and right modules over the exact category
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{{\rm ex}}}(E)
associated to
{\mathbf{b}}
. The criterion for split-generation is that the map from the tensor product of these modules to the self-Floer cohomology of
{\mathbf{b}}

{\mathbin{\cal L}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(E)\otimes_{{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{{\rm ex}}}(E)}{\mathbin{\cal R}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(E)\to CF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})

(2.78)

hits the unit on cohomology. By the above result, these modules are isomorphic to the pullbacks of the modules
{\mathbin{\cal L}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(X)
and
{\mathbin{\cal R}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(X)
over the image of
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{{\rm ex}}}(E)
(which we call
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{{\rm ex}}}(X)
associated to
{\mathbf{b}}
). The proof amounts to the homotopy commutativity of the following triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal L}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(E)\otimes_{{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{{\rm ex}}}(E)}{\mathbin{\cal R}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces CF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal L}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(X)\otimes_{{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{{{\rm ex}}}(X)}{\mathbin{\cal R}}_{{\mathbf{b}}}(X)}

(2.79)

which follows by the same argument as the above proposition: the map in Equation (
2.78
) is obtained by counting discs with an arbitrary number of inputs that are chords with boundary on
L
, and two of which are intersection points of
K
and
L
. The output is a marked point along the interval mapping to
K
. As before, the integrated maximum principle implies that the image of the discs whose inputs are chords that are contained in
X
cannot escape this region, so that the goal is to show that the family associated to rescaling the Hamiltonian outside of
X
by a parameter
\rho\in[1,\infty)
has the property that there are no discs with prescribed inputs and homotopy class of the path along
K
whenever
\rho
is sufficiently large.
∎

We return now to the case where
X
is a sufficiently small disc sub-bundle in
T^{*}Q.
In
[
24
]
Fukaya, Seidel and Smith construct a Lefschetz fibration with total space a Liouville subdomain of
T^{*}Q
(which they call
T^{*}N
), whose critical points are of either disjoint from
Q
or correspond to the critical points of a Morse function on it (see the discussion preceding
[
24
, Lemma 7]
). Taking the inverse image of a small region in the base of the Lefschetz fibration produces a subdomain of
T^{*}Q
, containing the
0
-section, and which has the property that all its Lefschetz thimbles intersect the
0
-section transversely at a point, i.e. are isotopic to cotangent fibres. Combining this with Seidel’s doubling construction
[
53
, §18]
we get

•

a Liouville manifold
E
(which Seidel denotes by
\tilde{E}
) containing
X
as a Liouville subdomain and

•

a collection
S_{1},\cdots,S_{k}\subset E
of Lagrangian spheres (which Seidel denotes by
\tilde{\Delta}_{i}
), whose intersection with
X
are Lefschetz thimbles associated to a Morse function on
Q
, and with the property that there is a composition
\phi
of Dehn twists about
S_{i}
such that
\phi X
is disjoint from
X
(up to Hamiltonian isotopy); see the first paragraph in the proof of
[
53
, Lemma 18.15]
.

Since we are interested in studying Fukaya categories that are possibly twisted by background classes, we need to know that any background class on
X
extends to a background class on
E
:

Lemma 2.87
.

The restriction map
H^{*}(E)\to H^{*}(X)
admits a splitting which vanishes on every sphere.

Proof.

Seidel’s construction exhibits
E
as a branched double cover of a Liouville domain containing
X
as a deformation retract. A splitting is then provided by the double covering map.
∎

From these properties we get:

Lemma 2.88
.

If
X\subset E
is a sufficiently small Weinstein neighbourhood of
Q
, then

•
