the intersection of each Lagrangian
S_{i}
with
X
is Hamiltonian isotopic to a cotangent fibre, and

•

the image of every closed Lagrangian in
X
under
\phi
is disjoint from
X
(up to Hamiltonian isotopy).∎

Fix a background class on
X
and the class induced in
E
by the splitting above. Introduce the Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(E)
which contains the Lagrangians
S_{i}

with the trivial relative spin structures (which exists because every
S_{i}
is the double of a disc in
X
), the trivial rank-one local systems and the trivial bounding cochains.

Remark 2.89
.

Here we allow the ground field
{\mathbf{k}}
to have characteristic
2,
because we do
not
take the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-invariant Fukaya category as Seidel does in the discussion of equivariant Fukaya categories in
[
53
]
, which is used in
[
24
]
.

The key result about this Fukaya category is:

Lemma 2.90
.

Every object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(E)
supported in
X
lies in the category split-generated by the spheres
S_{i}
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that for exact Lagrangians by Seidel
[
53
, Proposition 18.17]
, using Oh’s result
[
46
, Section 9]
for Seidel sequences in the nonexact case. Oh proves indeed that given two objects
{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
and a Lagrangian sphere
S
, with corresponding Dehn twist
\tau_{S}
, we have a null homotopy for the composition

CF^{*}(\tau_{S}{\mathbf{b}},S)\otimes CF^{*}(S,{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\to CF^{*}(\tau_{S}{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\to CF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})

(2.80)

where the first map is given by composition and the second by multiplication with a distinguished closed morphism in
CF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},\tau_{S}{\mathbf{b}})
. So the cone of this morphism
{\mathbf{b}}\to\tau_{S}{\mathbf{b}}
lies in the category generated by
S
.

Using this sequence repeatedly we see that if
{\mathbf{b}}
is an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(E)
supported in
X
then there is some closed morphism
\alpha
from
{\mathbf{b}}
to
\phi{\mathbf{b}}
whose cone lies in the category generated by
S_{1},\cdots,S_{k}.

But the Lagrangians underlying
{\mathbf{b}}
and
\phi{\mathbf{b}}
may be made disjoint after a Hamiltonian isotopy,
so
\alpha
is exact and the cone of
\alpha
is isomorphic to a direct sum of
{\mathbf{b}}
and
\phi{\mathbf{b}}
(with shifts).
This implies that
{\mathbf{b}}
is a summand of the object lying in the category generated by
S_{1},\cdots,S_{k}
.
∎

We are ready to prove the split-generation result:

Proof of Theorem
2.84
.

Lemma
2.87
implies that any background class on
X
is the restriction of a background class on
E
. If
\dim Q\neq 2
, the Lagrangian spheres
S_{i}
are automatically relatively spin. In the remaining case, we note that the only possible background class is represented by the Poincaré dual of the zero section, which corresponds to a cotangent fibre; and its pullback to
E
is represented by the inverse image of a cotangent fibre, whose intersection with
S_{i}
vanishes with
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2
coefficients because the submanifolds intersect an even number of times. The result then follows by applying Lemma
2.90
and Corrolary
2.86
, using as well the fact that all cotangent fibres are equivalent in the wrapped Fukaya category, so that one suffices.
∎

2.12
Yoneda Functors

We recall now some standard facts about about
A_{\infty}
Yoneda functors. We follow Seidel
[
53
]
with minor modifications.
For a strict
A_{\infty}
category
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with units denote by
\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}
the category of left
A_{\infty}
modules (Seidel
[
53
, (2f)]
works with right modules in place of left modules, and with cohomology units in place of strict units).
Seidel
[
53
, (1l) and (2g)]
defines the
Yoneda
functor
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}
which is a strict
A_{\infty}
functor, and proves that this induces a fully faithful functor
H{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}})
between the cohomology categories
[
53
, Corollary 2.13]
.

Seidel
[
53
, (2.13)]
proves also that if
{\mathbin{\cal A}},{\mathbin{\cal B}}
are strict
A_{\infty}
categories and
F:{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to{\mathbin{\cal B}}
an
A_{\infty}
functor then the pull-back functor
F^{*}:\Mod{\mathbin{\cal B}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and the Yoneda functors
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}},

{\mathbin{\cal B}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal B}}
fit into the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal B}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod{\mathbin{\cal B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{*}}

(2.81)

which commute after passing to the cohomology categories.

Lemma 2.91
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be a strict
A_{\infty}
category with units. Let
{\mathbin{\cal F}},{\mathbin{\cal G}}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be full subcategories such that
{\mathbin{\cal F}}
is split-generated by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}.
Consider the composite
{\mathbin{\cal F}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}}
of the inclusion functor
{\mathbin{\cal F}}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal A}},
the Yoneda functor
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and the pull-back functor
\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}}.

Then the induced functor
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}\to H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}})
is fully faithful.

Proof.

We repeat the argument by Seidel
[
53
, Lemma 4.7]
. Denote by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime}\subset\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}
the full subcategory split-generated by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}.

Then the composite functor
{\mathbin{\cal F}}\subseteq{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}
has image in
{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime}.
Using (
2.81
) with
{\mathbin{\cal B}}={\mathbin{\cal G}}
we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{\mathbin{\cal F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{\mathbin{\cal A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H{\mathbin{\cal G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}}).}

(2.82)

Here
H{\mathbin{\cal G}}\to H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}})
is induced from the Yoneda functor and accordingly fully faithful.
As (
2.82
) commutes the composite
H{\mathbin{\cal G}}\to H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}})
factoring through
H{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime},H{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and
H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}})
is fully faithful.
But
{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime}
is split-generated by
{\mathbin{\cal G}}
so
H{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime}\to H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}})
is fully faithful too.
The arrow
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}\to H{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime}
is induced from the Yoneda functor
{\mathbin{\cal A}}\to\Mod{\mathbin{\cal A}}
and therefore fully faithful too.
The composite
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}\to H{\mathbin{\cal G}}^{\prime}\to H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}})
is thus fully faithful.
∎

We return now to the circumstances of Theorem
2.84
with
{\mathbin{\cal A}}={\mathbin{\cal W}}(X),

{\mathbin{\cal F}}={\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
and
{\mathbin{\cal G}}\subset{\mathbin{\cal W}}(X)
the full subcategory made of the single object
T_{q}^{*}Q.

Lemma
2.91
implies then

Corollary 2.92
.

The induced functor
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)\to H(\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}})
is fully faithful.

The rest of the section is devoted to showing that this functor is compatible in a certain sense with passing to finite covers of
Q.

Denote by
CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
the endomorphism
A_{\infty}
algebra of the single object
T_{q}^{*}Q\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal G}}.

Then
\Mod{\mathbin{\cal G}}
is the category of
A_{\infty}
modules over
CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q).

Write
X=T^{*}Q
to save notation.
The Yoneda functor is then an
A_{\infty}
functor
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q)\to\Mod CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q).

Suppose now that
f:P\to Q
is a finite cover and
p\in f^{-1}(q)
any point.
We define then a corresponding
A_{\infty}
functor
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}P)\to\Mod CW^{*}(T_{p}^{*}P,T_{p}^{*}P)
and compare this with that on
Q.

We denote by the same
f
the induced map
T^{*}P\to T^{*}Q
between the cotangent bundles, which is also a finite cover. We pull back to
T^{*}P
the branes on
T^{*}Q.
More precisely, the pull-back of an underlying Lagrangian in
T^{*}Q
is possibly disconnected, in which case we include all the connected components of it; for instance, we include the fibre
T_{p}^{*}P.

We pull back to
T^{*}P
the relative spin structure on
T^{*}Q,
with which we introduce the Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}P).

We define then an
A_{\infty}
functor
f_{*}:{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}P)\to{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}Q)
which acts as the set-theoretic push-forward upon the underlying Lagrangians.
Choose a compatible almost complex structure on
T^{*}Q
and pull this back to
T^{*}P.

Fix
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
for a moment and recall that
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}P),{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}Q)
are made from gapped
A_{N}
categories.
We define a gapped
A_{N}
functor between these which respects all the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{k}_{\gamma}
operators, simply by pushing forward the pseudo-holomorphic discs. We define in the same way an inverse system of homotopy equivalences with which we can pass to the limits. Then we get a gapped
A_{\infty}
functor between the curved
A_{\infty}
categories
{\mathbin{\cal C}}(T^{*}P),{\mathbin{\cal C}}(T^{*}Q).
Including the bounding cochains we get an
A_{\infty}
functor
f_{*}:{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}P)\to{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}Q)
that we want.

We pass now to the wrapped categories.
By definition there exist a localization functor
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}Q)\to{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)
and accordingly a composite functor
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}P)\to{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q).

We pull back to
T^{*}P
the set
\Phi
of Hamitlonians used to define
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q),
with which we define the wrapped category
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}P).

We show that the functor
{\mathbin{\cal F}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}P)\to{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)
descends to
{\mathbin{\cal W}}_{\rm nc}(T^{*}P).

Let
\psi:T^{*}P\to T^{*}P
be the time-one diffeomorphism of a Hamiltonian we have chosen, which is the pull-back of some Hamiltonian
\phi\in\Phi.

Denote by the same
\phi
the time-one diffeomorphism
T^{*}Q\to T^{*}Q.

Then the push-forward of
\psi(T_{p}^{*}P)
is equal to
\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q)
and the push-forward of the continuation morphism
\kappa_{\psi}\in HF^{*}(\psi(T_{p}^{*}P),T_{p}^{*}P)
is the continuation morphism
\kappa_{\phi}\in HF^{*}(\phi(T_{q}^{*}Q),T_{q}^{*}Q).
The latter is already invertible in
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)

and hence we get by the universal property the functor
{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}P)\to{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)
that we want.

We define now a pull-back functor
f^{*}:{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q)\to{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}P)
between the Fukaya categories whose underlying Lagrangians are compact without boundary.
Take
(L,E,b)\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q)
with
L
a Lagrangian,
E
a local system and
b
a bounding cochain. We define a gapped
A_{\infty}
homomorphism

C_{*}(L,E)\to C_{*}(f^{*}L,f^{*}E)

(2.83)

that assigns to each singular chain in
L
the sum of all possible inverse images in
f^{*}L.

Using the fact that discs are simply connected, we see that the moduli space of discs in
T^{*}P
is a cover of the moduli space of discs in
T^{*}Q
, and hence that a choice of virtual chains for
T^{*}Q
determines the corresponding choice for
T^{*}P
by passing to the cover. This implies that (
2.83
)
is compatible with the gapped
A_{N}
algebra structures on the singular chain complexes
C_{*}(L,E),C_{*}(f^{*}L,f^{*}E).

The same argument shows that the homotopies required to construct the inverse system are also compatible with the pull-back by
f.

So we can take the limit with respect to
N
and (
2.83
) is a gapped
A_{\infty}
homomorphism.
This maps the bounding cochain
b\in C_{*}(L,E)
to a bounding cochain in
C_{*}(f^{*}L,f^{*}E),
which defines an
A_{\infty}
functor
f^{*}:{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q)\to{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}P)
that we want.

Note that for every Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}Q
we have
f_{*}f^{*}L=L\subset T^{*}Q.
Hence we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}P).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}

(2.84)

On the other hand, there are a pull-back functor
\Mod{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)\to\Mod T_{q}^{*}Q=\Mod CW(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
and the same functor for
P
in place of
Q.
We use also (
2.81
) with
{\mathbin{\cal B}}={\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q),

{\mathbin{\cal A}}={\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}P).

From these we get a digram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal W}}(T^{*}P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod CW(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod CW(T_{p}^{*}P,T_{p}^{*}P)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

(2.85)

which commutes after passing to the cohomology categories, where
f^{*}
denotes the pull-back by
f_{*}.

Combining this with (
2.84
) we get a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mod CW^{*}(T_{p}^{*}P,T_{p}^{*}P)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

(2.86)

which commutes after passing to the cohomology categories; note that we have got rid of the horizontal arrow in the left direction so that the horizontal arrows of (
2.86
) are both in the right direction.
We have thus proved

Proposition 2.93
.

For every finite cover
f:(P,p)\to(Q,q)
the pull-back
A_{\infty}
functor
f^{*}:{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}Q)\to{\mathbin{\cal F}}(T^{*}P),
the push-forward
A_{\infty}
algebra homomorphism

f_{*}:CW^{*}(T_{p}^{*}P,T_{p}^{*}P)\to CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)

and the Yoneda
A_{\infty}
functors fit into the diagram (
2.86
) which commutes after passing to the cohomology categories. ∎

3
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Let
Q
be a compact connected orientable Riemannian manifold without boundary.
We introduce a word we use in what follows.

Definition 3.1
.

A
standard
Calabi–Yau structure near the zero-section
Q\subset T^{*}Q
is a pair consisting of
a compatible (integrable) complex structure
J
and a
J
-holomorphic volume form relative to which
Q
is a special Lagrangian.

We begin in §
3.1
with the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i).
In §
3.2
we prove the key lemma for Theorem
1.3
(ii).
In §
3.3
we prove Theorem
1.3
(ii) when
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian.
In §
3.4
we prove a few facts we shall need to deal with finite covers of
Q.

In §
3.5
we prove Theorem
1.3
(ii) in the general case.

3.1
Proof of Theorem
1.3
(i)

Let
U\subseteq X
be a Weinstein neighbourhood of
Q
and regard this as embedded in
T^{*}Q
with a standard Calabi–Yau structure.
Suppose first that
\pi_{1}Q
is trivial.

Proposition 3.2
.

If the fundamental group
\pi_{1}Q
is trivial then the Fukaya category
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
is generated by the single object
Q,
the zero-section.

Proof.

Let
{\mathbf{b}}
be an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
and consider its image under the Yoneda functor
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)\to H\Mod CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q).
Applying the homological transfer lemma, we may replace
CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
by an
A_{\infty}
algebra whose underlying graded vector space is given by its cohomology, which is isomorphic to
H_{-*}(\Omega_{q}Q,\Lambda)
and hence is supported in non-positive degrees. It follows (by the degree-filtration argument
[
5
, Appendix A]
) that the Yoneda image of
{\mathbf{b}}
is a repeated extension of ungraded modules over the degree
0
algebra
HW^{0}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)\cong H_{0}(\Omega_{q}Q,\Lambda).

But
H_{0}(\Omega_{q}Q,\Lambda)
is isomorphic to
\Lambda[\pi_{1}Q]\cong\Lambda
so the image of
{\mathbf{b}}
is a repeated extension of ungraded
\Lambda
-vector spaces.
As
{\mathbf{b}}
is supported on a compact Lagrangian without boundary, the number of these extensions is finite and each ungraded piece is finite-dimensional over
\Lambda.

Recall now that
Q
intersects
T_{q}^{*}Q
transversely and exactly at one point
q
and that
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,Q)
is one-dimensional over
\Lambda.

Then each ungraded piece of the Yoneda image of
{\mathbf{b}}
may be written as
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,Q)\otimes V
for some finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-vector space
V.

As the functor
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)\to H\Mod CW^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,T_{q}^{*}Q)
is fully faithful, the object
{\mathbf{b}}
is isomorphic in the cohomology Fukaya category to a repeated extension of objects of the form
Q\otimes V.

This completes the proof.
∎

We use an algebraic lemma by the first author
[
5
, Lemma A.4]
, which goes back to the first general results on Arnold’s nearby Lagrangian problem
[
25
]
.
The original statement is over
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}/2{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
but the proof shows that it applies also to
\Lambda
:

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
{\mathbin{\cal A}}
be a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded
\Lambda
-linear triangulated
A_{\infty}
category generated by one object
\mathbf{a}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
whose endomorphism algebra
\hom^{*}({\bf a},{\bf a})
is supported in non-negative degrees.
Then every object
{\mathbf{b}}\in{\mathbin{\cal A}}
with
\hom^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
supported in non-negative degrees
is isomorphic in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal A}}
to
{\bf a}[k]\otimes V
for some integer
k\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and for some finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-vector space
V.

∎

Let
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
be supported on a closed special Lagrangian
L\subset T^{*}Q
relative to a standard Calabi–Yau structure.
Then
{\mathbf{b}}
is a non-zero object and by Proposition
2.55
the graded group
HF^{*}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
is supported in non-negative degrees.
Proposition
3.2
and Lemma
3.3
imply then that
{\mathbf{b}}
is isomorphic in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
to some
Q[k]\otimes V,

k\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}},
with the trivial bounding cochain.
Since
{\mathbf{b}}
is non-zero it follows that
V
is a non-zero vector space.
Note also that there are vector space isomorphisms

HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\cong HF^{n}(Q[k]\otimes V,Q[k]\otimes V)\cong HF^{n}(Q\otimes V,Q\otimes V)\cong HF^{n}(Q,Q)\otimes\mathop{\rm End}(V,V),

which is non-zero (by Corollary
2.64
or because
HF^{n}(Q,Q)
is isomorphic to the ordinary cohomology group).
Hence it follows by Proposition
2.67
(ii) that
L=Q.

If
\pi_{1}Q
is not trivial but finite, taking the universal cover
f:T^{*}P\to T^{*}Q
we find
f^{*}L=P
so
L=Q.

∎

3.2
The Key Lemma

Put
n:=\dim Q.

Fix a point
q\in Q
and fix a grading of
T_{q}^{*}Q
by phase
\frac{n\pi}{2}
at
q.

Then the following holds, for which we do
not
need any hypothesis on
\pi_{1}Q
:

Lemma 3.4
.

For every standard Calabi–Yau structure near
Q\subset T^{*}Q

there exists a neighbourhood
U
of
Q\subset T^{*}Q
such that for every
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
supported on a closed special Lagrangian graded by
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
the graded vector space
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
is supported in degree
0.

Remark 3.5
.

Here the speciality is essential because otherwise
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
may be supported in two or more degrees; see Example
5.10
.

There is in fact an analytical theorem by Almgren
[
10
]
, explained also by De Lellis and Spadaro
[
14
, Theorem 2.4]
. Suppose that the nearby special Lagrangians are in a given homology class. Then they are close to the zero-section
Q
as varifolds in the sense of geometric measure theory. Then there exists a small set in
Q
such that if
q
is outside it then near
T_{q}^{*}Q
each special Lagrangian
L
is
C^{1}
close to the zero-section
Q
(the same arguments apply also to area-minimizing currents).
This implies that the Maslov index of the pair
(T_{q}^{*}Q,L)
is zero, so the result holds at the chain level.
But we do not want to fix the homology class of
L,
and the cohomology level result will do for us.

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

Suppose first that the Calabi–Yau structure near
Q\subset T^{*}Q
is real analytic.
For each
k=1,\cdots,n-1
we define a compactly-supported Hamiltonian perturbation
L
of
T_{q}^{*}Q,
which is special Lagrangian near
q\in T^{*}Q.

To start with, take a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
-linear isomorphism
T_{q}(T^{*}Q)\cong{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}
which maps
T_{q}Q
to
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\subseteq{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n},

T_{q}^{*}Q
to
(i{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})^{n},
the symplectic form to
(i/2)\sum_{j=1}^{n}dz_{j}\wedge d\bar{z}_{j}
and the holomorphic volume form to
\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}dz_{j}
where
z_{1},\cdots,z_{n}
are the coordinates of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}.

Take
\theta_{1},\cdots,\theta_{k}\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},0)
and
\theta_{k+1},\cdots,\theta_{n}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
with

\theta_{1}+\cdots+\theta_{n}=0
so that the plane

\Pi:=\{(e^{i\theta_{1}}x_{1},\cdots,e^{i\theta_{n}}x_{n})\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}^{n}:x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\}

is special Lagrangian with respect to the flat Calabi–Yau structure.
Take a small ball in
\Pi
about
0
and embed it into
T^{*}Q
near
q,
mapping
0\in T_{q}(T^{*}Q)
to
q\in T^{*}Q.

Denote the image of the ball by the same
\Pi
for short.
Take a neighbourhood
W
of
q
in
Q
over which
T^{*}Q
is a product bundle.
We can suppose that the ball
\Pi
lies in the product bundle
(T^{*}Q)|_{W}.

Denote by
{\rm pr}:(T^{*}Q)|_{W}\to T_{q}^{*}Q
the vertical projection of the product bundle.
Put
U_{q}:={\rm pr}(\Pi),
which is a ball in
T_{q}^{*}Q
about the origin.
Choose a neighbourhood
U\subset T^{*}Q
of
Q
which is so small that
(U\cap T_{q}^{*}Q)\Subset U_{q}.

We perturb
\Pi
into a special Lagrangian with respect to the Calabi–Yau structure of
U.

There are two ways of doing this.
The first is to use Cartan–Kähler’s theorem as Harvey and Lawson do
[
28
, Chapter III, Proof of Theorem 5.5]
, which we can do because we have supposed that our Calabi–Yau structure is real analytic.
The second is to use the implicit function theorem again as Harvey and Lawson do
[
28
, Chapter III, Proof of Corollary 2.14]
.
In either case we get a special Lagrangian near
q\in T^{*}Q.

We can suppose that this is a graph over
U_{q}.

Using a cut-off function we get then a compactly-supported Hamiltonian perturbation
L
of
T_{q}^{*}Q
such that
L\cap{\rm pr}^{*}U_{q}
is a special Lagrangian in
U.

Grade the Lagrangians
L
and
\Pi
by phase
0
and the Lagrangians
T_{q}^{*}Q
and
(i{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})^{n}
by phase
\frac{n\pi}{2}
at
q.

For
j=1,\dots,k
define an affine function
[0,1]\to(-\frac{\pi}{2},\theta_{j})
by
t\mapsto-\frac{\pi}{2}+t(\theta_{j}+\frac{\pi}{2}).

For
j=k+1,\dots,n
define an affine function
[0,1]\to(\frac{\pi}{2},\theta_{j})
by
t\mapsto\frac{\pi}{2}+t(\theta_{j}-\frac{\pi}{2}).

Using these we get a Hamiltonian isotopy from
(i{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})^{n}
to the plane
\Pi
which does not cross
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}.

As
k(-\frac{\pi}{2})+(n-k)\frac{\pi}{2}=\frac{n\pi}{2}-k\pi
there is a graded Hamiltonian isotopy from
(i{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})^{n}[k]
to
\Pi.

This induces a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded Hamiltonian isotopy from
T_{q}^{*}Q[k]
to
L.

Suppose now that
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
is supported on a closed special Lagrangian
N\subset U.

Since
(U\cap T_{q}^{*}Q)\Subset U_{q}
it follows then that the special Lagrangian part of
L
does not agree with
N.

We can thus apply Proposition
2.69
to
N,L
and consequently
HF^{*}(L,{\mathbf{b}})\cong HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q[k],{\mathbf{b}})
is supported in degrees
1,\cdots,n-1.

So
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
is supported in degrees
1-k,\cdots,n-1-k.

Since this holds for every
k\in\{1,\cdots,n-1\}
it follows that
\bigcap_{k=1}^{n-1}[1-k,n-1-k]=\{0\}
as we want.

Finally, if the Kähler form
\omega
near
Q\subset T^{*}Q
is not real analytic we can approximate it by a real analytic one (after taking a Kähler potential) and find a compactly-supported Hamiltonian diffeomorphism
\phi:T^{*}Q\to T^{*}Q
such that
\phi_{*}\omega
is real analytic. Applying to this the result above, we get a corresponding neighbourhood
V
of
Q\subset T^{*}Q.

Put
U:=\phi^{*}V.
Let
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
be supported on a closed special Lagrangian (relative to
\omega
) and denote by
\phi{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(V)
the push-forward.
Then
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,\phi{\mathbf{b}})
is supported in the single degree
n.

But as
\phi
is compactly supported, we have
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,\phi{\mathbf{b}})\cong HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}}),
which completes the proof.
∎

The effect of the lemma above is that we do not have to deal with the extension problem which we explain now.
Let
{\mathbf{b}}
be an object of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U).

As in the proof of Theorem
1.3
(i) the Yoneda module
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
is a repeated extension of ungraded modules over
\Lambda[\pi_{1}Q],
that is, representations of
\pi_{1}Q.

These extensions may be nontrivial.
In Example
5.10
, for instance, the direct sum of
Q
and the graph of
\alpha
are different from their surgery.
More precisely, these define non-isomorphic objects of
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U).
They are made up of the same representations but with different extensions.

3.3
The
\pi_{1}Q
abelian Case

We prove Theorem
1.3
(ii) when
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian.
We work in a Weinstein neighbourhood of
Q\subset X
which we embed in
T^{*}Q.

Denote by
g
the metric on
Q
induced from
X,
and by
(J,\Omega)
the standard Calabi–Yau structure near
Q
induced from
X.

Define a
C^{\infty}
function
\psi:Q\to(0,\infty)
by
\psi^{2}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}=(\frac{i}{2})^{n}(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\Omega\wedge\overline{\Omega}.

Denote by
\Psi:C^{\infty}(T^{*}Q)\to C^{\infty}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
the linear elliptic operator
\alpha\mapsto d^{*}(\psi\alpha)
where
d^{*}
is computed with respect to
g.

This
\Psi
is the linearized operator we use to prove McLean’s theorem
[
34
, Proposition 2.13]
.
By a version of Hodge theory the de Rham cohomology group
H^{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
is isomorphic to the vector subspace
\ker d\cap\ker\Psi\subset C^{\infty}(T^{*}P).

By the de Rham theorem there exists
M>0
such that the following holds: suppose given
\delta>0
and a group homomorphism
\beta:H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/{\rm(torsion)}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
with
|\beta x|\leqslant\penalty\delta|x|
for
x\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/{\rm(torsion)};

then
\beta
may be represented by some closed
1
-form
\alpha
on
Q
with
\Psi\alpha=0
and
\sup_{P}|\alpha|_{C^{0}}\leqslant\penalty M\delta

where the pointwise
C^{0}
norm is computed with respect to
g.

By McLean’s theorem
[
34
,
43
]
there exists
\epsilon>0
such that the following holds:
let
\alpha
be a closed
1
-form on
Q
with
\Psi\alpha=0
and
\sup_{Q}|\alpha|_{C^{0}}\leqslant\penalty\epsilon;

then the graph
Q^{\alpha}
of
\alpha
is Hamiltonian equivalent to some special Lagrangian near
Q\subset T^{*}Q
with respect to
(J,\Omega).

Denote by
U\subset T^{*}Q
the disc sub-bundle of radius
\delta\in(0,M^{-1}\epsilon]
which is so small too that we can use Lemma
3.4
.

Let
L\subset U
be a closed
HF^{*}
-unobstructed special Lagrangian and let
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
be supported on
L.

Corollary
2.56
implies then that
{\mathbf{b}}
is a non-zero object. By Lemma
3.4
the graded vector space
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
is supported in a single degree.
Denote by
\rho:\pi_{1}Q\to\mathop{\rm GL}HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
the representation made from the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
products in
H{\mathbin{\cal W}}(U).

By hypothesis the group
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian so
\rho
has a one-dimensional sub-representation
\sigma:\pi_{1}Q\to\Lambda^{*}.

Recall that
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma\in\hom(\pi_{1}Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\cong H^{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
and denote by
\alpha
the closed
1
-form on
Q
which represents the de Rham class
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma
and satisfies the equation
\Psi\alpha=0.

Using the notation of Lemma
2.79
write
\sigma\cong T^{-[\alpha]}\otimes E
where
E
is some group homomorphism
\pi_{1}Q\to\mathop{\rm GL}_{1}\Lambda^{0}.

This defines a rank-one
\Lambda^{0}
local system over
Q^{\alpha}\cong Q.

As in Remark
2.11
the category of rank-one free
\Lambda^{0}
modules is equivalent to that of one-dimensional valued vector spaces.
Hence we get a filtered local system over
Q^{\alpha}
which we denote by the same
E.

Lemma
2.79
implies then that
\sigma:\pi_{1}Q\to\Lambda^{*}
is isomorphic to the representation on
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,E).

As
\sigma
is a sub-representation of
\rho
there exists a degree-zero non-zero morphism from
\sigma
to
\rho.
Thus
HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}},E)\neq 0.

By Corollary
2.83
we have
|{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)|\leqslant\penalty\delta|x|
for
x\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/{\rm(torsion)}.

We have chosen
M>0
so that
\sup_{Q}|\alpha|_{C^{0}}\leqslant\penalty M\delta\leqslant\penalty\epsilon.

Then the graph
Q^{\alpha}
is Hamiltonian equivalent to some special Lagrangian
Q^{\prime}.
As this Hamiltonian perturbation does not change the isomorphism class of
{\mathbf{b}}
it follows from Proposition
2.67
(i) that
Q^{\prime}=L.

This completes the proof.
∎

3.4
Finite Covers and McLean’s Theorem

Let
(Q,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold without boundary, and
q\in Q
a point.
Recall that for
y\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion)
we have defined
|y|\geqslant 0
before stating Lemma
2.80
.
Let
f:P\to Q
be a finite cover and denote by
f_{*}:H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})\to H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
the homomorphism induced by
f.

We use the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space isomorphism

H^{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\cong\hom(H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion),{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}).

Proposition 3.6
.

For every integer
k>0
there exists
M>0
independent of the finite cover
f:P\to Q
and so large that every linear map
\tau:H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion)\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
with
|\tau x|\leqslant\penalty|f_{*}x|,

x\in H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion),
may be represented by a closed
1
-form with
C^{k}
norm
\leqslant\penalty M
with respect to the induced metric
f^{*}g.

Proof.

The proof contains several key steps.
We begin by choosing some geodesics in
P
and a triangulation of
P.

We prove in Lemma
3.7
a uniform estimate for the geodesics; uniform means that the relevant constants are independent of the finite cover
f:P\to Q.

We choose then a subgroup
G<C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
of
1
-cycles which generate
H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion}).

We prove in Lemma
3.8
a uniform estimate for the elements of
G.

We pass then to the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-coefficients and introduce a
1
-cocycle
\sigma\in C^{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
of cohomology class
\tau.

We prove in Corollary
3.9
and Lemma
3.10
that
\sigma
satisfies a uniform estimate.
We use the de Rham theorem to define a closed
1
-form corresponding to
\sigma,
which will satisfy the uniform estimate we want.

We turn now to the formal treatment. Recall that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(Q,q)
of the compact manifold
Q
is generated by some finitely many elements
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}.

For
a=1,\dots,k
with
[x_{a}]\neq 0\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
represent
x_{a}
by a geodesic
\gamma_{a}:[0,1]\to Q
with
\gamma_{a}(0)=\gamma_{a}(1)=q
and of length
[x_{a}].

For
a=1,\dots,k
with
[x_{a}]=0\in H_{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
take
\gamma_{a}:[0,1]\to Q
to be the constant loop with value
q.

Note that
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}
intersect one another only at finitely many points. Choose then a smooth triangulation of
Q
which contains
\gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\gamma_{k}
as a sub-complex and is so small that every simplex is contained in some geodesic ball of the Riemannian manifold
(Q,g).

The latter condition implies that for distinct
x,y\in P
with
f(x)=f(y)
the simplices containing
x,y
are disjoint (for if there were some intersection point
z
then the geodesic between
x,z
and that between
y,z
would be two different lifts of the geodesic between
f(x),f(z);
but the lift ought to be unique).
The finite cover
f:P\to Q
induces then a triangulation of
P,
which we use in what follows.

Put
l:=k\deg f
and denote by
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{l}:[0,1]\to P
the lifts under
f:P\to Q
of
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}:[0,1]\to Q.

Denote by
|\delta_{a}|\geqslant 0
the length of each
\delta_{a}.

Regard
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{l}
as
1
-chains in the free abelian group
C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}).

Denote by
|\,\,|_{\ell^{p}},

p\geqslant 1,
the
\ell^{p}
norm on
C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}});
that is, if we denote by
\{e\}
the set of edges (or
1
-simplicies) in
P
then for
y=\sum_{e}y_{e}e\in C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
we have
|y|_{\ell^{p}}:=(\sum_{e}|y_{e}|^{p})^{1/p}.

We prove

Lemma 3.7
.

There exists
M_{0}>0
independent of
f:P\to Q
and so large that

|\delta_{a}|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}|\delta_{a}|_{\ell^{2}}\text{ for every }a=1,\dots,l.

Proof.

Put
M_{1}:=\max\{|\delta_{1}|,\dots,|\delta_{l}|\}=\max\{|\gamma_{1}|,\dots,|\gamma_{k}|\},
which is independent of
f:P\to Q.

Denote by
\rho_{1}>0
the greatest length of edges in
P
and by
\rho_{0}>0
the least length of edges in
P,
which are also independent of
f:P\to Q.

Fix
a\in\{1,\dots,l\}
and denote by
N_{a}
the number of edges contained in
\delta_{a};

then
N_{a}\leqslant\penalty\rho_{0}^{-1}M_{1}=:M_{2}.

On the other hand, the
\ell^{1}
norm of
\delta_{a}
is the multiple count of the relevant edges so

|\delta_{a}|\leqslant\penalty\rho_{1}|\delta_{a}|_{\ell^{1}}\leqslant\penalty\rho_{1}\sqrt{N}_{a}|\delta_{a}|_{\ell^{2}}\leqslant\penalty\rho_{1}\sqrt{M_{2}}|\delta_{a}|_{\ell^{2}}.

This proves the lemma with
M_{0}:=\rho_{1}\sqrt{M_{2}}>0.

∎

Denote by
\ker{\partial}<C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
the subgroup of
1
-cycles.
We show that the natural projection
\ker{\partial}\cap({\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\delta_{1}+\dots+{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\delta_{l})\to H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
is surjective.
Let
x\in H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
be any element and represent this by some loop
y
based at
p.

Then
f_{*}y
is a loop in
Q
based at
q,
which is based homotopic to some concatenation of
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}.
Lifting this to
P
we see that
y
is based homotopic to some concatenation of
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{l}.
The corresponding
1
-cycle in
P
has then homology class
x,
which proves our claim.

As
H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
is a free abelian group, we can choose a subgroup
G<\ker{\partial}\cap({\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\delta_{1}+\dots+{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}\delta_{l})
such that the natural projection
G\to H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
is surjective.
For
g\in G
denote by
[g]\in H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
its homology class modulo torsion.

Lemma 3.8
.

For
g\in G
we have

|f_{*}[g]|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}|g|_{\ell^{2}}.

Proof.

Write
g=\sum_{a=1}^{l}g_{a}\delta_{a}
with
g_{a}\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Recall that each
f_{*}\delta_{a}
agrees with some
\gamma_{b}
and that the least length
|[f_{*}\delta_{a}]|
of the homology class
[f_{*}g_{a}]
is equal to the length
|f_{*}\delta_{a}|=|\delta_{a}|
of the paths
f_{*}\delta_{a},\delta_{a}.
So

|f_{*}[g]|\leqslant\penalty\sum_{a=1}^{l}|g_{a}|\bigl|[f_{*}\delta_{a}]\bigr|\leqslant\penalty\sum_{a=1}^{l}|\mu_{a}||f_{*}\delta_{a}|=\sum_{a=1}^{l}|\mu_{a}||\delta_{a}|.

(3.1)

Lemma
3.7
implies then

|f_{*}[g]|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\sum_{a=1}^{l}|\mu_{a}||\delta_{a}|_{\ell^{2}}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\Bigl|\sum_{a=1}^{l}\mu_{a}\delta_{a}\Bigl|_{\ell^{2}}=M_{0}|g|_{\ell^{2}}

(3.2)

because
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{l}
have no common edges and are
\ell^{2}
orthonormal. This completes the proof.
∎

We pass now to the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-coefficients. Extend the natural projection
G\to H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})
to the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-linear map
G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\to H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}).

Extend also the given homomorphism
\tau:H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/({\rm torsion})\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
to
H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}).

Define an
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-linear map
\tau^{\prime}:G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
to be the composite of the natural projection
G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\to H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
and the extended
\tau:H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.
We prove

Corollary 3.9
.

For
g\in G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
we have
|\tau^{\prime}g|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}|g|_{\ell^{2}}.

Proof.

The hypothesis on
\tau
and Lemma
3.8
imply that
|\tau^{\prime}g|\leqslant\penalty|g|_{\ell^{2}}
for
g\in G.
This extends readily to
g\in G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
because we can cancel out the denominators.
Note that
\tau^{\prime}:G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
is an
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-linear map between finite-dimensional vector spaces, which is automatically continuous with respect to the norm topologies.
Moreover
G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{Q}}}
is dense in
G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
so the estimate holds with
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-coefficients as we want.
∎

We introduce a
1
-cocycle
\sigma\in C^{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
of cohomology class
\tau.

Denote by
\im{\partial}\subset C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
the subspace of
1
-boundaries and by
(\im{\partial})^{\perp}
its
\ell^{2}
orthogonal complement. Put
G^{\prime}:=(\im{\partial})^{\perp}\cap(G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
and denote by
\Pi:C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\to G^{\prime}
the
\ell^{2}
orthogonal projection.
Define an
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-linear map
\sigma:C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
to be the composite of this
\Pi
and that
\tau^{\prime}
defined above.
Then
\sigma
vanishes on
\im{\partial}
and is accordingly a
1
-cocycle on
P.

Since
G^{\prime}
agrees with the orthogonal complement in
G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
to
(\im{\partial})\cap(G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
it follows that for
g\in G,
if we denote by
g^{\prime}
its projection onto
G^{\prime}
then
g-g^{\prime}\in(\im{\partial})\cap(G\otimes{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\subseteq\im{\partial};
and in particular,

\tau^{\prime}g=\tau^{\prime}g^{\prime}=\sigma g,
so
\sigma
has cohomology class
\tau.
We prove

Lemma 3.10
.

For every edge
e\in C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})
we have
|\sigma e|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}.

Proof.

Corollary
3.9
implies that for
y\in C_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
we have

|\sigma y|=|\tau^{\prime}\Pi y|\leqslant\penalty M_{0}|\Pi y|_{\ell^{2}}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}|y|_{\ell^{2}}.

(3.3)

Taking
y
to be an edge
e
we get the estimate we want.
∎

We use now the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-linear map
C^{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\to\Omega^{1}_{P}
from the
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
-vector space of
1
-cochains to that of
1
-forms, defined by Singer and Thorpe
[
57
, §6.2, Lemma 1]
, which maps
1
-cocycles to closed
1
-forms and induces the de Rham isomorphism of the two cohomology groups.
Part (4) of their lemma shows that for each edge
e
in
P
the corresponding
1
-form is supported near
e
in
P.

The process of assigning the
1
-form is thus local, which we can do essentially in
Q
before passing to the finite cover
P.

Hence it follows by Lemma
3.10
that the
1
-form corresponding to
\sigma
is bounded uniformly with respect to
f:P\to Q,
completing the proof.
∎

We prove also that McLean’s theorem holds uniformly with respect to finite covers.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
(Q,g)
be a compact Riemannian manifold without boundary,
X\subset T^{*}Q
the disc sub-bundle of radius
\epsilon_{X}>0,
and
(J,\Omega)
a standard Calabi–Yau structure on
X.

Define a
C^{\infty}
function
\psi:Q\to(0,\infty)
by
\psi^{2}\frac{\omega^{n}}{n!}=(\frac{i}{2})^{n}(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\Omega\wedge\overline{\Omega}.

Then there exists
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon_{X})
such that the following holds: let
f:P\to Q
be a finite cover and
\alpha
a closed
1
-form on
P
with
\sup_{P}|\alpha|_{C^{0}}\leqslant\penalty\epsilon
and
d^{*}(f^{*}(\psi\alpha))=0,
both computed with respect to the induced metric
f^{*}g;
then there exists a
C^{\infty}
function
v:P\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
such that
\alpha+dv
is a special Lagrangian section of
(f^{*}X,f^{*}\Omega).

Proof.

For an integer
k\geqslant 0
denote by
L^{2}_{k}(T^{*}P)
the Sobolev space of
1
-forms on
P
and by
L^{2}_{k}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
that of
0
-forms on
P.

Denote by
\Xi\subset L^{2}_{k}(T^{*}P)
the co-dimension one closed linear subspace consisting of
\xi\in L^{2}_{k+2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
with
\int_{P}\xi d\mu=0.

Denote by
\Psi:L^{2}_{k+2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\to\Xi
the linear elliptic operator
v\mapsto d^{*}(\psi dv).

We regard
\psi
as a multiplication operator and write
\Psi=d^{*}\psi d
for instance.
By the definition of
\Xi
the operator
\Psi:L^{2}_{k+2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\to\Xi
is
surjective
so there exists a right inverse to it, which we denote by
G:\Xi\to L^{2}_{k+2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}).

The key fact is that we can choose Sobolev norms on these spaces such that
G
is bounded
uniformly
with respect to the finite cover
f:P\to Q.

Those Sobolev norms we get directly from the pull-back metric
f^{*}g
will not do for this purpose. For instance, take
P=Q=S^{1}={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
with
g=dt\otimes dt
using the co-ordinate
t
on
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.
Let
m>0
be an integer and
f:S^{1}\to S^{1}
the
m
-fold cover
x\mapsto mx
modulo
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
for
x\in S^{1}={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.
Then
f^{*}g=m^{2}g,
which contributes
m^{-2}
to the Laplacian
\Psi
and
m^{2}
to the Green operator
G
by
m^{2}.
The latter is clearly unbounded, which we shall not want to happen.

We define now the Sobolev norms we use. Let
\rho>0
be less than half the injectivity radius of the Riemannian manifold
(Q,g).
For
q\in Q
denote by
B_{q}\subset Q
the open geodesic ball of radius
\rho
about
q.

As
Q
is compact there exists a finite set
A\subset Q
such that
\bigcup_{a\in A}B_{a}=Q.

We look now at the finite cover
f:P\to Q.
For
x\in P
with
f(x)\in A
denote by
B_{x}\subset P
the lift of
B_{f(x)}\subset Q
with respect to
f:P\to Q,
which exists because
B_{f(x)}
is simply connected. Thus
B_{x}
is an open geodesic ball in
(P,f^{*}g).
Define then a norm function
L^{2}_{k}(T^{*}P)\to[0,\infty)
by

\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k}}:=\sum_{x\in f^{*}A}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k}(B_{x})}

(3.4)

where the latter is the
ordinary
Sobolev norm; that is,
\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k}(B_{x})}^{2}:=\int_{P}|f_{*}u|^{2}+\dots+|\nabla^{k}(f_{*}u)|^{2}d\mu
where
|\,\,|,d\mu
are computed with respect to
g.

We prove now that
\Psi
is bounded below in an
f
-uniform way.

Lemma 3.12
.

There exists
M_{0}>0
independent of the finite cover
f:P\to Q
and so large that for every
v\in L^{2}_{2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
we have
\|v\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\|\Psi v\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

Recall that if
Z
is a compact Riemannian manifold without boundary and
\psi:Z\to(0,\infty)
a smooth function then there exists
M>0
such that for every
v\in L^{2}_{2}(Z,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
with
\int_{Z}vd\mu=0
we have
\|v\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty M\|d^{*}(\psi dv)\|_{L^{2}};
the constant
M
is the inverse of the first non-zero eigenvalue of the elliptic operator
d^{*}\psi d.
This extends to a compact Riemannian manifold
with
boundary, because the estimate follows from that on the double (with a modified metric). Suppose therefore that
Z
may now have boundary and note that for every
v\in L^{2}_{2}(Z,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
with
\int_{Z}ud\mu=0
we have

\min\psi(dv,dv)_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty(\psi dv,dv)_{L^{2}}=(d^{*}\psi dv,v)_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty\|\Psi v\|_{L^{2}}\|v\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty M\|\Psi v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Thus
\|dv\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty(\min\psi)^{-1/2}M^{1/2}\|\Psi v\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Given an element of
L^{2}_{2}(Y,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
not satisfying the vanishing mean condition, add to this a constant and use the result we have just obtained; then we get the same estimate for it.
Consequently, for each
a\in A
there exists
M_{a}>0
independent of
f
and so large that for every
v_{a}\in L^{2}_{2}(B_{a},{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
we have

\|dv_{a}\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty M_{a}\|\Psi v_{a}\|_{L^{2}}.

Take any
v\in L^{2}_{2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
and for
x\in f^{*}A
put
v_{x}:=v|_{B_{x}}.
Then

\|dv\|_{L^{2}}=\sum_{x\in f^{*}A}\|d(f_{*}v_{x})\|_{L^{2}(B_{f(x)})}\leqslant\penalty\sum_{x\in f^{*}A}M_{f(x)}\|\Psi(f_{*}v_{x})\|_{L^{2}(B_{f(x)})}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\|\Psi v\|_{L^{2}}

where
M_{0}:=\max_{x\in f^{*}A}M_{f(x)}=\max_{a\in A}M_{a}.
This completes the proof.
∎

Fix now
k>\frac{n}{2}
so that we have the Sobolev embedding
L^{2}_{k+1}(T^{*}P)\subset C^{1}(T^{*}P).
We prove that the Sobolev embedding and the elliptic regularity are
f
-uniform too.

Lemma 3.13
.

There exists
M_{1}>0
independent of the finite cover
f:P\to Q
and so large that for every
u\in L^{2}_{k+1}(T^{*}P)
we have

\sup_{P}|u|_{C^{1}}\leqslant\penalty M_{1}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty(M_{1})^{2}(\|u\|_{L^{2}}+\|(d+d^{*}f^{*}\psi)u\|_{L^{2}_{k}}).

(3.5)

Proof.

By the Sobolev embedding on
P
there exists
M>0
independent of
f
and so large that for every
a\in A
and
u\in L^{2}_{k+1}(T^{*}B_{a})
we have

\sup_{B_{a}}|u|_{C^{1}}\leqslant\penalty M\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{a})}.

Then for
u\in L^{2}_{k+1}(T^{*}P)
we have

\sup_{B_{x}}|u|_{C^{1}}=\sup_{B_{a}}|f_{*}u|_{C^{1}}\leqslant\penalty M\|f_{*}u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{a})}\leqslant\penalty M\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(P)},

which proves the left inequality of (
3.5
).

We turn now to the other part. We show first that for
a,b\in A
with
B_{a}\cap B_{b}\neq\emptyset
the non-empty intersection is necessarily connected. If there were two components, there would be two distinct geodesics connecting these two.
But
B_{a},B_{b}
have been chosen to have the common radius
\rho
which is less than half the injectivity radius. So
B_{a}\cup B_{b}
lies in a single geodesic ball, which contradicts that the two geodesic exists. Thus
B_{a}\cap B_{b}
is connected. The union
B_{a}\cup B_{b}
is accordingly simply connected.

As
Q
is covered by
B_{a}
’s of radius
\rho>0
there exists
\sigma\in(0,\rho)
so close to
\rho
that if we denote by
B_{a}^{\prime}\subseteq B_{a}
the concentric ball of radius
\sigma
then
(B_{a}^{\prime})_{a\in A}
still covers
Q.

Then for
u\in L^{2}_{k+1}(T^{*}P)
we have

\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}=\sum_{y\in f^{*}A}\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{y}})\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{f(y)})}\leqslant\penalty\sum_{y\in f^{*}A}\sum_{B_{a}^{\prime}\cap B_{f(y)}\neq\emptyset}\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{y}})\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{f(y)}\cap B_{a}^{\prime})}.

(3.6)

For
a\in A
and
x\in f^{-1}(a)
denote by
Y_{ax}\subseteq f^{*}A
the set of
y
such that
B_{f(y)}\cap B_{a}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
and the simply connected set
B_{f(y)}\cap B_{a}
lifts to
B_{y}\cap B_{x}.

Exchanging the order of the two sums in (
3.6
) we see then that

\begin{split}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}&=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(a)}\sum_{y\in Y_{ax}}\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{y}})\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{f(y)}\cap B_{a}^{\prime})}\\
&=\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(a)}\sum_{y\in Y_{ax}}\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{y}\cup B_{x}})\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{f(y)}\cap B_{a}^{\prime})}\\
&\leqslant\penalty\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(a)}(\#Y_{ax})\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{x}})\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{a}^{\prime})}.\end{split}

(3.7)

Note that there is an injection
Y_{ax}\to\{z\in A:B_{a}^{\prime}\cap B_{z}\neq\emptyset\}
defined by
y\mapsto f(y)
so that the number
\#Y_{ax}
is bounded above by some
f
-independent constant
N>0.

It follows then from (
3.7
) that

\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty N\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(a)}\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{x}})\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{a}^{\prime})}.

(3.8)

On the other hand, by the ordinary a priori estimates over
Q
there exists
M>0
independent of
f:P\to Q
and so large that for every
a\in A
and
x\in f^{-1}(a)
we have

\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{x}^{\prime}})\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}(B_{a}^{\prime})}\leqslant\penalty M(\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{x}})\|_{L^{2}(B_{a})}+\|(d+d^{*}\psi)f_{*}(u|_{B_{x}})\|_{L^{2}_{k}(B_{a})}).

Hence it follows by (
3.8
) that

\begin{split}\|u\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}&\leqslant\penalty MN\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(a)}(\|f_{*}(u|_{B_{x}})\|_{L^{2}(B_{a})}+\|(d+d^{*}\psi)f_{*}(u|_{B_{x}})\|_{L^{2}_{k}(B_{a})})\\
&\leqslant\penalty MN\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(a)}(\|u\|_{L^{2}(B_{x})}+\|(d+d^{*}f^{*}\psi)u\|_{L^{2}_{k}(B_{x})})\\
&=MN(\|u\|_{L^{2}(P)}+\|(d+d^{*}f^{*}\psi)u\|_{L^{2}_{k}(P)}).\end{split}

Thus
M_{1}:=MN
will do for the right part of (
3.5
).
∎

Corollary 3.14
.

There exists
M_{2}\geqslant 1
independent of the finite cover
f:P\to Q
and so large that for every
\xi\in\Xi
we have
\|dG\xi\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty M_{2}\|\xi\|_{L^{2}_{k}}.

Proof.

Applying Lemma
3.12
to
G\xi\in L^{2}_{k+2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\subset L^{2}_{2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
in place of
v
we see that
\|dG\xi\|_{L^{2}}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}\|d^{*}\psi dG\xi\|_{L^{2}}=M_{0}\|\xi\|_{L^{2}}.

This with the right inequality of (
3.5
) implies

\|dG\xi\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty M_{1}(\|dG\xi\|_{L^{2}}+\|(d+d^{*}\psi)dG\xi\|_{L^{2}_{k}}\leqslant\penalty M_{1}(M_{0}\|\xi\|_{L^{2}}+\|\xi\|_{L^{2}_{k}}).

Thus
M_{2}:=M_{1}(M_{0}+1)
will do.
∎

We complete now the proof of Proposition
3.11
.
Let
\epsilon_{0}>0
be so small that if
\beta
is a
C^{0}
section of
T^{*}P
with
\sup|\beta|\leqslant\penalty\epsilon_{0}
then
\alpha+\beta
is a
C^{0}
section over
P
of
f^{*}U\subset T^{*}P.
Note that if
\beta
is a
C^{1}
section of
T^{*}P
with
\sup_{P}|\beta|\leqslant\penalty\epsilon_{0}
then we can define the pull-back by
\alpha+\beta:P\to f^{*}U
and in particular the
n
-form
(\alpha+\beta)^{*}\mathop{\rm Im}\Omega.

Put
V:=\{v\in L^{2}_{k+2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}):\|dv\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty M_{1}^{-1}\epsilon_{0}\}.
The left inequality of (
3.5
) implies then that for every
v\in V
we can define
dv
as a
C^{1}
section over
P
of
f^{*}U.

By the proof of McLean’s theorem
[
43
,
34
]
there exists a non-linear operator
\Phi:V\to\Xi
defined by

\Phi v:=-f^{*}\psi*(\alpha+dv)^{*}\mathop{\rm Im}f^{*}\Omega.
We solve the equation
\Phi v=0.
Standard computation shows that
\Phi
is differentiable at
v=0
with respect to the Sobolev norms, whose differential
\nabla_{0}\Phi:L^{2}_{k+2}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\to\Xi
agrees with
\Psi.

Corollary
3.14
implies then that for
\xi\in\Xi
with
\|\xi\|\leqslant\penalty M_{2}^{-1}M_{1}^{-1}\epsilon_{0}=:\epsilon_{1}
we have

\|dG\xi\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty M_{0}^{-1}\epsilon_{0}
so
G\xi\in V.
We solve the equation
\Phi G\xi=0
for
\xi\in L^{2}_{k}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
with
\|\xi\|\leqslant\penalty\epsilon_{1}.

Define
\Theta:V\to L^{2}_{k}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
by
\Theta:=-\Phi+\Psi.
We seek then a fixed point of the composite
\Theta G
and use therefore the contraction mapping principle.
As
\Theta
is the quadratic or higher term made up of
\psi,\Omega
there exists
M>0
independent of
f
and so large that the following holds: let
v,v^{\prime}\in C^{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
satisfy
|dv|,|dv^{\prime}|<\epsilon_{0}
at every point of
P;
and put
\beta=\alpha+dv,

\beta^{\prime}:=\alpha+dv^{\prime};
then

|\Theta v-\Theta v^{\prime}|_{C^{k}}\leqslant\penalty M|\beta-\beta^{\prime}|_{C^{k+1}}(|\beta|_{C^{k+1}}+|\beta^{\prime}|_{C^{k+1}})

at every point of
P.
Integrating this and using the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality we get

\int_{P}|\Theta\beta-\Theta\beta^{\prime}|_{C^{k}}d\mu\leqslant\penalty M\sqrt{\int_{P}|\beta-\beta^{\prime}|_{C^{k+1}}^{2}d\mu}\sqrt{\int_{P}(|\beta|_{C^{k+1}}+|\beta^{\prime}|_{C^{k+1}})^{2}d\mu}.

(3.9)

Putting
A:=|\beta|_{C^{k+1}}
and
B=|\beta^{\prime}|_{C^{k+1}}
we have

\sqrt{\int_{P}(A+B)^{2}d\mu}\leqslant\penalty\sqrt{2\int_{P}(A^{2}+B^{2})d\mu}\leqslant\penalty\sqrt{2}\left(\sqrt{\int_{P}A^{2}d\mu}+\sqrt{\int_{P}B^{2}d\mu}\right).

This with (
3.9
) implies

\|\Theta v-\Theta v^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty\sqrt{2}M\|\beta-\beta^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{k}}(\|\beta\|_{L^{2}_{k}}+\|\beta^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{k}})\leqslant\penalty 2\sqrt{2}M(\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}_{k}}+\epsilon_{0})\|v-v^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}_{k}}.

Hence choosing
\epsilon\in(0,\min\{(3\sqrt{2}M(\|\alpha\|_{L^{2}_{k}}+\epsilon_{0}))^{-1},\epsilon_{1}\})
and using Corollary
3.14
we see that
\Theta G
is a contraction map from the closed ball
\{\xi\in\Xi:\|\xi\|_{L^{2}_{k}}\leqslant\penalty\epsilon\}
to itself.
So there exists
\xi\in\Xi
with
\Theta G\xi=\xi.

Recall that
k
has been any integer
>\frac{n}{2}
and that fixed points are unique; then we see that
\xi\in L^{2}_{k}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
for every
k>\frac{n}{2}.
Thus
\xi
is
C^{\infty}
and accordingly so is
G\xi.

Now
\xi=\Theta G\xi=-\Phi G\xi+\Psi G\xi=-\Phi G\xi+\xi
so
\Phi G\xi=0.

The graph of
\alpha+dG\xi
is the special Lagrangian we want, which proves Proposition
3.11
.
∎

Here is a corollary of Proposition
3.6
and the estimates in the proof above.

Corollary 3.15
.

There exists
M>0
independent of the finite cover
f:P\to Q
and so large that every linear map
\tau:H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion)\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
with
|\tau x|\leqslant\penalty|f_{*}x|,

x\in H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/(\rm torsion),
may be represented by a closed
1
-form on
P
with
C^{1}
norm
\leqslant\penalty M
and
d^{*}(f^{*}\psi\alpha)=0
where the
C^{1}
norm and
d^{*}
are computed with respect to the induced metric
f^{*}g.

Proof.

Fix an integer
k>\frac{n}{2}.
Then by Proposition
3.6
there exist a constant
N_{0}>0
independent of
f
and a closed smooth
1
-form
\beta
on
P
with
\|\beta\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty N_{0}.

Define a
1
-form
\alpha
on
P
by

\alpha:=\beta-dG(d^{*}\psi\beta).

Then
d\alpha=0
and
d^{*}\psi\alpha=d^{*}\psi\beta-\Psi G(d^{*}\psi\beta)=0.

We estimate the
C^{0}
norm of
\alpha.

By Corollary
3.14
there exist constants
N_{1},N_{2}>0
independent of
f,\beta
and so large that

\|dG(d^{*}\psi\beta)\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty N_{1}\|d^{*}\psi\beta\|_{L^{2}_{k}}=N_{1}N_{2}\|\beta\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty N_{0}N_{1}N_{2}.

By Lemma
3.13
there exists a constant
N_{3}
independent of
f,\beta
and so large that
\sup_{P}|\beta|\leqslant\penalty N_{3}\|\beta\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty N_{0}N_{3}
and

\textstyle\sup_{P}|dG(d^{*}\psi\beta)|_{C^{1}}\leqslant\penalty N_{3}\|dG(d^{*}\psi\beta)\|_{L^{2}_{k+1}}\leqslant\penalty N_{0}N_{1}N_{2}N_{3}.

Thus
|\alpha|_{C^{1}}\leqslant\penalty|\beta|_{C^{1}}+|dG(d^{*}\psi\beta)|_{C^{1}}\leqslant\penalty N_{0}N_{3}(1+N_{1}N_{2}),
which completes the proof.
∎

3.5
Proof of Theorem
1.3
(ii)

We prove Theorem
1.3
in the general case (when
\pi_{1}Q
is not abelian).
We begin by choosing a disc sub-bundle
U\subset T^{*}Q.
We impose two conditions. Firstly,
U
should be so small that we can use Lemma
3.4
. Secondly, let
M>0
be as in Corollary
3.15
and
\epsilon>0
as in Proposition
3.11
; then the radius
\delta
of
U
should be
\leqslant\penalty M^{-1}\epsilon.

Let
L\subset U
be a closed
HF^{*}
-unobstructed special Lagrangian and
{\mathbf{b}}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(U)
an object supported on
L.

Then
{\mathbf{b}}
is a non-zero object. By Lemma
3.4
the graded vector space
HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
is supported in a single degree.
Denote by
\rho:\pi_{1}Q\to\mathop{\rm GL}HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
the representation made from the
{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}
products in
H{\mathbin{\cal W}}(U).

By hypothesis the group
\pi_{1}Q
has no non-abelian free subgroup,
and accordingly its image
\rho(\pi_{1}Q)\subset\mathop{\rm GL}HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}})
does not have such a subgroup either.
Since
\pi_{1}Q
is finitely generated it follows that so is
\rho(\pi_{1}Q).

To this we apply the following two results: one is by Tits
[
60
, Corollary 1]
and the other is
a Lie–Kolchin theorem stated for instance by Borel
[
12
, Chapter III 10.5]
.
Both apply to fields of arbitrary characteristic.
For the latter statement we need the field to be algebraically closed.
We therefore pass to the algebraic closure of
\Lambda
as in §
2.1
. This is possible because the bounding cochain equations will still have solutions with the larger field. We denote by the same
\Lambda
the algebraic closure.

Theorem 3.16

(Tits)
.

Every finitely-generated linear group has either a nonabelian free subgroup or a finite-index solvable subgroup.

Theorem 3.17

(Lie–Kolchin)
.

Every solvable linear group over an algebraically closed field has a finite-index triangularisable subgroup.

Consequently there exists a finite-index subgroup of
\pi_{1}Q
on which
\rho
is triangularisable.
Let
f:(P,p)\to(Q,q)
be the corresponding finite cover,
and take a one-dimensional sub-representation
\sigma:\pi_{1}P\to\Lambda^{*}
of the triangularisable group.

Recall that
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma\in\hom(\pi_{1}P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})\cong H^{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}})
and denote by
\alpha
the closed
1
-form on
P
that represents the cohomology class
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma
and satisfies the equation
d^{*}(f^{*}\psi d\alpha)=0.

Denote by
P^{\alpha}\subseteq T^{*}P
the graph of
\alpha.

Using the notation of Lemma
2.79
write
\sigma\cong T^{-[\alpha]}\otimes E
where
E
is some group homomorphism
\pi_{1}P\to\mathop{\rm GL}_{1}\Lambda^{0}.

This defines a rank-one
\Lambda^{0}
local system over
P^{\alpha}.

Hence we get a filtered local system over
P^{\alpha}
which we denote by the same
E.

Lemma
2.79
implies then that
\sigma:\pi_{1}P\to\Lambda^{*}
is isomorphic to the representation on
HF^{*}(T_{p}^{*}P,E).

Denote by
f^{*}\rho
the restriction to
\pi_{1}P
of
\rho:\pi_{1}Q\to\mathop{\rm GL}HF^{*}(T_{q}^{*}Q,{\mathbf{b}}).

As
\sigma
is a sub-representation of
f^{*}\rho
there exists a degree-zero non-zero morphism from
\sigma
to
f^{*}\rho.
Hence it follows by Proposition
2.93
that there exists a degree-zero non-zero morphism from
f^{*}{\mathbf{b}}
to
E.
Thus
HF^{0}(f^{*}{\mathbf{b}},E)\neq 0.

By Corollary
2.83
we have
|{\mathbf{v}}\sigma(x)|\leqslant\penalty\delta|x|
for
x\in H_{1}(P,{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}})/{\rm(torsion)}.

Corollary
3.15
implies then that
{\mathbf{v}}\sigma
may be represented by a closed
1
-form
\alpha
on
P
with
d^{*}(\psi d\alpha)=0
and
\sup_{P}|\alpha|_{C^{0}}\leqslant\penalty M\delta\leqslant\penalty\epsilon.

Proposition
3.11
implies then that the graph of
\alpha
is Hamiltonian equivalent to some special Lagrangian
P^{\prime}.
As this Hamiltonian perturbation does not change the isomorphism class of
f^{*}{\mathbf{b}}
it follows from Proposition
2.67
(i) that
P^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
f^{*}L.

Thus
L
is unbranched, which completes the proof of Theorem
1.3
(ii).
∎

Remark 3.18
.

Theorem
1.3
holds for some badly immersed special Lagrangians.
In the known construction of Fukaya categories, the underlying Lagrangians are required at least to be cleanly immersed
[
20
]
.
On the other hand, in our nearby problem we want to include as many (special) Lagrangians as possible, so we introduce the following notion
[
33
, before Corollary 4.6]
.

Let
\hat{L}
be a closed manifolds and
\iota:\hat{L}\to X
be a Lagrangian immersion. Put
L:=\iota(\hat{L}).

We say that an object
{\mathbf{b}}
in
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
is supported
near

L
if there exist arbitrarily-small Lagrangian perturbations of
L
which underlie some objects in the same isomorphism class
[{\mathbf{b}}]
in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X).

More concretely, this means that for every
\epsilon>0
there exists a perturbation of the immersion
\hat{L}\to X
with
C^{k}
norm bounded by
\epsilon
for all
k
, and which supports an object representing the given isomorphism class
[{\mathbf{b}}].

Theorem
1.3
holds for a special Lagrangian
L
which admits such an isomorphism class
[{\mathbf{b}}]
as above.
The reason is that the results of the paper
[
33
]
are stated and proved for objects of
{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
supported near a special Lagrangian. In particular, Proposition
2.67
and Lemma
3.4
are still valid for these objects. So the proof of Theorem
1.3
will work.

But the condition above on
[{\mathbf{b}}]
is rather strong. Here is one possible way of verifying it.
Suppose given a smooth family
\iota^{t}:\hat{L}\to X,

t\in[0,1],
such that
\iota^{0}=\iota
and the other
\iota^{t},

t\in(0,1],
are all generic and mutually isotopic under
domain
Hamiltonians on
\hat{L}
in the sense of Akaho–Joyce
[
9
, Definition 13.14 (ii)]
(this is a family version of the classical transversality condition in differential topology).
If we could lift such an isotopy to an ambient Hamiltonian isotopy, then it would follow that any isomorphism class in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
supported on the immersion
\iota^{1}
is also supported on
\iota^{t}
for all
t,
generalising the Hamiltonian invariance theoreom by Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
]
. In general, such a lift does not exist because then those strips which bound arcs between self-intersection points will have constant area under the isotopy. We can perhaps use the methods of
[
48
]
to prove invariance statements for this perturbation problem.

4
Tsai–Wang’s Method

We begin by recalling the key formula from geometric measure theory.
Let
X
be a Riemannian manifold.
Given a vector field
v
on
X
, a point
x\in X
, and an
n
-dimensional plane
S\subseteq T_{x}X
we denote by
\di_{S}v
the trace of the map

S\subseteq T_{x}X\to T_{x}X\to S
where the two arrows are the covariant derivative
\nabla v:T_{x}X\to T_{x}X
and the orthogonal projection
T_{x}X\to S.

In particular, if
v
is the gradient vector field of a smooth function
f:X\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
then

\di_{S}\grad f=\tr_{S}\nabla^{2}f.

We use also the notion of
rectifiable integral varifolds
in
X
which we treat, following Simon
[
56
]
, as Radon measures on
X.

Recall that if
V
is a compactly supported stationary integral
n
-varifold in
X
then for every vector field
v
on
X
we have
\int_{X}\di_{TV}v\,dV=0.

In particular,

\int_{X}\tr_{TV}\nabla^{2}f\,dV=0

(4.1)

for every smooth function
f:X\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

We recall now the following result from the literature
[
14
,
61
,
62
]
. We prove this to show how to use (
4.1
).

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
X
be a Ricci-flat Calabi–Yau manifold of complex dimension
n,
and
Q\subset X
a closed embedded special Lagrangian which has positive Ricci curvature with respect to the induced metric from
X.

Then there exists a neighbourhood
U\subseteq X
of
Q
in which every compactly supported stationary integral
n
-varifold is supported on
Q.

Proof.

Tsai and Wang prove that
Q\subset X
is
strongly stable
in their sense
[
62
, Proposition A(iii)]
and that the following holds
[
62
, Proposition 4.1]
: there exists an open neighbourhood
U\subseteq X
of
Q
such that for every point
x\in U
and for every
n
-dimensional plane
S\subseteq T_{x}U
we have at
x

\tr_{S}\nabla^{2}d_{Q}^{2}\geqslant c\,d_{Q}^{2}

(4.2)

where
d_{Q}:U\to[0,\infty)
is the distance from
Q
and
c>0
depends only upon
Q.

Applying (
4.1
) with
f=d_{Q}^{2}
and using (
4.2
) we find
d_{Q}^{2}=0
on the support of the varifold
V,
completing the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

The geometric meaning of the proof is as follows: let
U
be a
tubular
neighbourhood and
\phi^{t}:U\to U,

t\leqslant\penalty 0,
the flow of the gradient vector field of
d_{Q}^{2};
then
\phi^{t}
does not increase the area of
V.

As is well known, closed special Lagrangian currents are area-minimizing and in particular area-stationary
[
28
]
. Combining this with Proposition
4.1
we see immediately that the following holds:

Corollary 4.3
.

In the circumstances of Proposition
4.1
every compactly supported closed special Lagrangian current in
U
is necessarily non-singular, of phase
0\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/2\pi{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and supported on
Q.

∎

Example 4.4
.

Let
X
be either
T^{*}S^{n},
for
n>1,
or
T^{*}{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}P^{n}
for arbitrary
n
, and let
Q
denote its zero-section;
then
X
has a Ricci-flat metric constructed respectively by Stenzel
[
58
]
and Calabi
[
13
]
.
In these two cases the hypothesis of Proposition
4.1
holds and moreover its conclusion holds in the whole
X
rather than in a neighbourhood of
Q.

We turn now to the example which shows that
\pi_{1}Q
abelian is essential to the latter part of Theorem
1.3
(ii).
We begin by proving a lemma in slightly more general circumstances.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
n,k\geqslant 0
be integers. Take
X:=T^{n}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{k},

Q:=T^{n}\times\{0\}
and give these the flat metrics.
Then every compactly supported stationary integral
n
-varifold in
X
is parallel to
Q,
that is, supported on
T^{n}\times A
for some finite set
A\subseteq{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}.

Proof.

Let
V
be a compactly supported stationary integral
n
-varifold in
X.

Applying (
4.1
) again with
v=\grad d_{Q}^{2}
we get

\int_{X}\tr_{TV}\nabla^{2}d_{Q}^{2}\,dV=0.

But
\nabla^{2}d_{Q}
is pointwise non-negative and degenerates only in the direction of
T^{n}\times\{0\}
so
V
-almost everywhere in
X
we have

\tr_{TV}\nabla^{2}d_{Q}^{2}=0
and
TV
is parallel to
T^{n}\times\{0\}.

We recall a lemma of Simon
[
56
, Lemma 19.5]
.
Let
x
be a point on the support of
V
and we work near this so we can regard
x
as a point of the universal cover
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
of
T^{n}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}.

Let
\epsilon>0
be so small that for every point
y
of distance
<\epsilon
from
x
we can define
y-x\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
and let
y
be a point on the support of
V
of distance
\epsilon\delta/4
from
x
and such that
y-x
is
not
parallel to
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times\{0\}.

Simon
[
56
, Lemma 19.5]
proves then that

\Theta_{V}(x)+\Theta_{V}(y)\leqslant\penalty(1-\delta)^{-n}\epsilon^{-n}\omega_{n}^{-1}V(B_{\epsilon})

(4.3)

where
\Theta_{V}
denotes the density of
V
and
B_{\epsilon}
the ball of radius
\epsilon
about
x.

The original formula has in fact another term on the right-hand side, which contains the integral over
z\in B_{\epsilon}
of
\|p_{{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times\{0\}}-p_{z}\|
where
p_{{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times\{0\}},p_{z}
are the projections of
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{k}
onto
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times\{0\},T_{z}V
respectively. But
T_{z}V
is parallel to
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times\{0\}
so the additional term vanishes and (
4.3
) holds.

As
\epsilon,\delta
tend to
0
the right-hand side of (
4.3
) tends to
\Theta_{V}(x)
and in particular becomes less than
\Theta_{V}(x)+1.
But then by (
4.3
) we have
\Theta_{V}(y)<1,
which is impossible. So the hypotheses above fail; that is, if
y
is a point of the support of
V
close enough to
x
then
y-x
is parallel to
{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{n}\times\{0\}.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

The hypothesis of Proposition
4.1
is stable under small perturbations of the Ricci-flat Kähler metric (just because positivity is an open condition). On the other hand, Lemma
4.5
will fail under perturbations of the ambient metric.
For instance, Kapouleas and Yang
[
37
]
construct a sequence of minimal surfaces converging to the flat Clifford torus
T^{2}\subset S^{3}
with multiplicity two;
and a suitable rescaling of the ambient metric converges to the flat metric on the tubular neighbourhood of
T^{2}\subset S^{3}.

Corollary 4.7
.

Let
X
be a flat Calabi–Yau manifold,
Q\subset X
a closed embedded totally geodesic special Lagrangian, and
U\subseteq X
a tubular neighbourhood of
Q.

Then every compactly-supported closed special Lagrangian current in
U
is parallel to
Q,
that is, a locally constant multi-valued graph over
Q.
Every such graph is a disjoint union of single-valued graphs if and only if
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian.

Proof.

Since
Q
is a closed flat manifold we get according to Bieberbach a finite Galois cover
f:T^{n}\to Q

such that
\pi_{1}T^{n}
is a maximal abelian subgroup of
\pi_{1}Q.

Denote by the same
f
the finite cover
T^{*}T^{n}\to T^{*}Q
between the cotangent bundles.
Identify
U
with a neighbourhood of the zero-section
Q\subset T^{*}Q,
and the pull-back
f^{*}U
with a neighbourhood of
T^{n}\subset T^{*}T^{n}.

As
T^{*}T^{n}
is a product bundle we can use Lemma
4.5
to show that the pull-back of a compactly supported closed special Lagrangian current
L
is parallel; and accordingly, so is the original
L.

Now if
\pi_{1}Q
is abelian, this is the maximal abelian subgroup of itself and
Q=T^{n};
so every special Lagrangian is a disjoint union of single-valued graphs.
If
\pi_{1}Q
is non-abelian, there exist a non-trivial covering transformation
\gamma:T^{n}\to T^{n}
and accordingly an element
\gamma\in\pi_{1}Q\setminus\pi_{1}T^{n}
which acts non-trivially upon
H^{1}(T^{n},{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}).
So there exists a constant
1
-form
\alpha
on
T^{n}
that does change under
\gamma:T^{n}\to T^{n}.

By rescaling
\alpha
we can suppose that
\alpha
is so small that we can define its graph in
f^{*}U.
The
push-forward of this defines then a truly multi-valued graph in
U.

∎

Example 4.8
.

Let
X=U=T^{*}Q
with
Q
a Klein bottle. Write
Q
as the mapping torus of
the automorphism
S^{1}\to S^{1}
given by
x\mapsto-x
modulo
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
for
x\in S^{1}={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}.

Then there are corresdponding double covers
T^{2}\to Q
and
T^{*}T^{2}\to T^{*}Q,
under which the graph of
dx
is pushed forward to a multi-valued graph.
Precisely speaking,
X
is not Calabi–Yau and
Q
is not even orientable.
But
Q
is still a special Lagrangian in the extended sense as in Definition
2.32
; see also Remark
2.33
. The multi-valued graph is also a special Lagrangian in the same sense.
It is easy to find multi-valued graphs which are ordinary special Lagrangians. For instance, take
Q
to be the mapping torus of the
orientation-preserving
diffeomorphism

T^{2}\to T^{2}
given by
(x,y)\mapsto(-x,-y)
where
(x,y)\in T^{2}={\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}^{2}/{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}^{2};
then the graph of
dx
is pushed forward to a multi-valued graph which is an ordinary special Lagrangian.

5
Branched Examples

Here are examples of
branched

C^{0}
nearby closed Lagrangians,
which will all be Lagrangian surgeries.
We begin by recalling the result of Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono
[
21
, Chapter 10]
which describes the effect of applying Lagrangian surgeries to pairs of objects in the Fukaya category.

Theorem 5.1

(cf.
[
21
, Chapter 10]
)
.

Let
L,L^{\prime}\subseteq X
be two closed embedded
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded Lagrangians which intersect each other transversely at least at one point, with the Maslov index function
\mu_{LL^{\prime}}:L\times_{X}L^{\prime}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
identically equal to
1.

Let
L\#L^{\prime}
be a
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded Lagrangian surgery
[
51
, Lemma 2.14]
.

Then for any
{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
supported on
L,L^{\prime}
respectively,
there exist an object
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
supported on
L\#L^{\prime}

and for every other
{\bf a}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
a long exact sequence

\cdots\to HF^{*}({\bf a},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\to HF^{*}({\bf a},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime})\to HF^{*}({\bf a},{\mathbf{b}})\to\cdots

(5.1)

where the connecting homomorphism
HF^{*}({\bf a},{\mathbf{b}})\to HF^{*+1}({\bf a},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
is the right multiplication by some element of
HF^{1}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}).

∎

Remark 5.2
.

We allow
L,L^{\prime}
to have two or more intersection points, which is possible as Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono mention
[
21
, after Theorem 55.7]
although they deal only with singular intersection points in their formal treatment. So there are many objects
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime}
of the form above according to different scales of surgeries at different intersection points.

Here are remarks on the proof. Fukaya, Oh, Ohta and Ono use the cobordism method for the moduli spaces of holomorphic discs with boundary on the surgery; the cobordism is associated to stretching the neck (degenerating the almost complex structure) near the intersection points.
The method of Biran–Cornea
[
18
]
provides an easier proof which does not rely on a degeneration argument: one can associate to the surgery a Lagrangian cobordism in
X\times{\mathbin{\mathbb{C}}}
, and the count of holomorphic triangles with boundary on this cobordism yields the desired long exact sequence. Although their results are stated for monotone or exact Lagrangians, these assumptions are imposed only to avoid using the theory of virtual chains in case the Lagrangians bound holomorphic discs. We shall in practice take
L,L^{\prime}
to be graphical Lagrangians in a cotangent bundle, which do not bound any non-constant holomorphic discs; so their proof can be implemented with no difficulty.

For special Lagrangians we have

Corollary 5.3
.

In the circumstances of Theorem
5.1
let
L,L^{\prime}
be both special Lagrangians.
Then
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime}\in\obj{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
is not isomorphic in
H^{0}{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
to any other object supported on a closed special Lagrangian of any phase.

Proof.

By shifting
L,L^{\prime}
we may suppose that
L
has phase
2i\pi
for some
i\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
and that
L^{\prime}
has phase
0.

Then by (
2.28
) we have
-i\in(1-n,1)
so
i\geqslant 0.

Applying (
5.1
) to
{\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime}
in place of
{\bf a}
we get two exact sequences

\displaystyle HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime})\to HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})\to HF^{n+1}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})=0,

(5.2)

\displaystyle 0=HF^{-1}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}})\to HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})\to HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime}).

(5.3)

On the other hand
HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}})
and
HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime})
are both nonzero, so

HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime})
and
HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime})
are both nonzero.
Suppose contrary to our conclusion that
{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime}
is isomorphic to an object
{\bf a}\in{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
supported on a closed special Lagrangian
L^{\prime\prime}
of phase
\theta\in{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}.

By perturbing
L^{\prime\prime}
we may suppose that
{\bf a}
is supported on a nearly-special Lagrangian
L^{*}
which intersect
L
and
L^{\prime}
generically.
Since
HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\bf a})\cong HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\mathbf{b}}^{\prime\prime})\neq 0
we get an intersection point
x\in L\cap L^{*}
with Maslov index
n.

Hence denoting by
\phi:L^{*}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
the phase function and using (
2.28
) we find

-\phi(x)/2\pi\in(0,n)
and in particular
\phi(x)<0.

Also since
HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},{\bf a})\neq 0
we get an intersection point
x^{\prime}\in L^{\prime}\cap L^{*}
with

i-\phi(x^{\prime})/2\pi\in(-n,0)
and in particular
\phi(x^{\prime})>i\geqslant 0.

But both
\phi(x)
and
\phi(x^{\prime})
are near
\theta
so
i=\theta=0;
that is,
L,

L^{\prime}
and
L^{\prime\prime}
have all phase
0.

Since
HF^{n}({\mathbf{b}},{\bf a})
and
HF^{0}({\mathbf{b}}^{\prime},{\bf a})
are both non-zero it follows by Proposition
2.67
(i)
that these three Lagrangians have a common irreducible component.
But this is impossible because
L,L^{\prime}
intersect each other transversely at least at one point.
∎

We recall now a key fact about
Morse

1
-forms;
a Morse
1
-form on a manifold
Q
is a closed
1
-form
\alpha
on
Q
such that every point of
Q
has a neighbourhood on which
\alpha=df
for some Morse function
f.

Theorem 5.4

(Honda
[
32
]
)
.

Let
Q
be a closed manifold of dimension
n,
and
\alpha
a Morse
1
-form on
Q
with no critical point of index
0
or
n.

Then there exists on
Q
a Riemannian metric
g,
and in the de Rham class
[\alpha]\in H^{1}(Q,{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}),
a
g
-harmonic Morse
1
-form
\beta
which has the same distribution of indices as
\alpha
has;
that is, for each
i\in\{0,\cdots,n\}
the number of index-
i
critical points of
\beta
is equal to that of
\alpha.

∎

Remark 5.5
.

If
Q
is equipped with any real analytic structure, we can suppose that
g
and
\beta
above are real analytic. To see this, perturb the metric from Honda’s theorem to a real analytic Riemannian metric, which we denote still by the same
g
. Choosing the perturbation to be sufficiently small, there exists a unique
g
-harmonic
1
-form near
\beta
in the same de Rham class, which we denote again by the same
\beta.
This
\beta
is analytic by elliptic regularity.

Here is another fact we will use. We give
T^{*}Q
the canonical symplectic and Liouville structures.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
(Q,g)
be a closed oriented real analytic Riemannian manifold.
Then there exist a disc sub-bundle
X\subseteq T^{*}Q
and a standard Calabi–Yau structure
(J,\Omega)

(
in the sense of Definition
\ref{dfn: standard CY})
such that the restriction of the Kähler metric to
Q
is equal to
g,
and the complex structure is compatible with the Liouville structure of
X

(
in the sense of Definition
\ref{dfn:Liouv}).

∎

Remark 5.7
.

The real analytic structure induced from the complex structure above is possibly different from that induced from the real analytic structures of
Q
and
T^{*}Q.

Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

Guillemin and Stenzel
[
27
, §5]
prove this except the part which has to do with
\Omega;
that is, there exist a sufficiently small disc sub-bundle
X\subseteq T^{*}Q
and a complex structure
J
on
X
compatible with the Liouville structure of
X
and such that the restriction to
Q
of the corresponding Kähler metric is equal to
g.

Making
X
smaller if we need, extend
g
to a holomorphic section of
\sym^{2}T^{*}X
which is locally of the form
g_{ab}(z^{1},\dots,z^{n})dz^{a}dz^{b}
where
z^{1},\dots,z^{n}
are local holomorphic co-ordinates with which
Q
is defined by
\mathop{\rm Im}z^{1}=\dots=\mathop{\rm Im}z^{n}=0.
This is possible because every
g_{ab}
is real analytic.
Since
Q
is oriented we get, making
X
yet smaller if we need, a holomorphic volume form on
X
which is locally of the form
\sqrt{\det g_{ab}}dz^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dz^{n}
and whose restriction to
Q
agrees with the volume form of
(Q,g).
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Guillemin and Stenzel
[
27
, §5]
do not suppose that
Q
is oriented.
They show also that
J
is invariant under the involution
\sigma:T^{*}Q\to T^{*}Q
defined by
p\mapsto-p
and that
J
is the unique complex structure satisfying this and the other conditions in the lemma.

We give now an example of
non-special
Lagrangian surgeries.
Let
Q
be a closed connected orientable manifold of dimension
n,
and
\alpha
a Morse
1
-form on
Q
whose critical points have the same index.
The Poincaré duality of Morse–Novikov homology implies then that
n
must be even and the critical points must have index
\frac{n}{2}.

We prove

Theorem 5.9
.

There exist a disc sub-bundle
X\subset T^{*}Q,
a closed embedded branched
{\mathbin{\mathbb{Z}}}
-graded Lagrangian
L,
and an object of
H{\mathbin{\cal F}}(X)
supported on
L
that is not isomorphic to any other object supported on a closed special Lagrangian
(
of any phase
relative to any standard Calabi–Yau structure
).

Proof.

Applying Theorem
5.4
to
\alpha
we get a harmonic
1
-form
\beta
with the same distributions of critical points as
\alpha
has.
Applying McLean’s theorem to
\beta
we get a
C^{1}
nearby special Lagrangian
Q^{\prime}
such that the pair
(Q^{\prime},Q)
has the same distribution of indices as
\alpha
has.
Consider now
Q^{\prime}[\frac{n}{2}-1]\#Q
as in Theorem
5.1
.
Corollary
5.3
implies then that this cannot be represented by a special Lagrangian, completing the proof.
∎

Example 5.10
.

Let
n\geqslant 4
be an even number and take
Q
to be the self-connected sum of
S^{n/2}\times S^{n/2};
that is,
Q
is obtained from
S^{n/2}\times S^{n/2}
by eliminating two points of it and attaching the handle between them.
Take a Morse function
S^{n/2}\times S^{n/2}\to{\mathbin{\mathbb{R}}}
with one critical point of index
0,
two of index
\frac{n}{2}
and one of index
n.
Performing the connected sum construction at the critical point of index
0
and that of index
n,
we obtain a
1
-form
\alpha
on
Q
